#374. Hurdle hints and answers for November 10, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

The Our Place Always Pan is my number-one kitchen sidekick. I use it nearly every single time I cook because it’s so versatile. The nontoxic nonstick coating is still holding up well, though it does stain a bit, which I don’t mind. It’s easy to clean, too. #372. GTA 6 will technically be playable on Xbox One at launch

On Tuesday, Rockstar Games gave us some details about how Grand Theft Auto VI will look and run on current-gen consoles. But there was a fascinating buried lede in that blog post.

Using Xbox Cloud Gaming, you'll be able to stream GTA 6 to an Xbox Series console or an Xbox One. I'm not entirely sure why you'd stream it to one of the newer consoles instead of just running it natively, but the option is there, nonetheless. If you play GTA 6 this way, the stream will use the Xbox Series S version's graphics settings.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Of course, there is one final very relevant detail here: Starting this month, Xbox has imposed strict hourly limits on cloud gaming. You can only stream games for five to 15 hours per month, depending on which version of Game Pass you pay for. There's also the matter of game streaming being subject to the whims of internet quality, so things like buffering, latency, and poor visual quality can be part of the experience.

So, it might still be worth it to just try and find a current-gen console instead.

#371. Ukraine’s fiber-optic drones get squeezed by AI data center demand

Ukraine competes with Russia and data centers for China’s optical fiber supply.

Impervious to battlefield jamming, drones physically connected to human operators by miles of fiber-optic cables have become such a common weapon of war in Ukraine that trees and fields are strewn with tangled cables thinner than fishing lines. But importing such fiber-optic material from China has become more costly as drone warfare’s needs compete with global demand from AI data centers for the same limited supply—and so Ukrainian drone makers have been adapting.

Since the full-scale Russian invasion of Ukraine began in February 2022, the Ukrainians have been forced out of necessity to continually develop battlefield innovations involving first-person view (FPV) quadcopter drones and other heavier types of drones. But it was the Russian military that first deployed unjammable fiber-optic drones to repel Ukrainian forces’ cross-border incursion into the Kursk Oblast in 2024. The Ukrainians quickly followed suit in developing their own fiber-optic drones, given the drones’ capability to shrug off electronic warfare jamming that could neutralize radio-controlled drones.

Russia and Ukraine have relied on Chinese suppliers for some off-the-shelf drones and a wide array of drone components, including bulk orders of fiber wound around very large spools or sometimes small finished spools ready for installation on drones. The finished spools can hold enough fiber-optic cable for drones to regularly operate at ranges of dozens of miles, and sometimes up to 62 miles (100 kilometers) in extreme cases, as reported by the independent Russian publication The Insider.

Ukraine and Russia have been producing an estimated 50,000 fiber-optic drones per month in recent years. That’s a lot of optical fiber that has effectively become a one-time battlefield consumable.

But China’s economic and security ties with Russia have enabled Russia to import significantly larger amounts of fiber-optic cable than Ukraine—at least according to China’s official customs data. For example, Russia imported 328,000 miles of fiber-optic cable from China in August 2025 alone, compared to Ukraine getting just 72 miles of cable from Chinese companies, according to The Washington Post.

To get a better sense of how drone warfare has increased demand for optical fiber technology, Russia alone bought more than 37 million miles (60 million kilometers) of fiber-optic cable in 2025, according to The Insider and Russian business publication Vedomosti. That means Russia’s purchases amounted to more than 10 percent of all fiber produced worldwide last year, when it had previously never accounted for more than 1 percent of global demand.

The global AI boom and ongoing data center buildout have also strained optical fiber supply, with an expected worldwide shortfall of 111 million miles (180 million kilometers) in 2026, according to the market research firm TrendForce. That has further increased the cost of optical fiber purchased by Russia and Ukraine, including the bend-insensitive fiber that is especially coveted for fiber-optic drones.

One Ukrainian drone manufacturer navigating these supply-chain challenges is BlueBird Tech, which produces fiber-optic drones along with other military drones, a ground robot, drone detection systems, and electronic warfare systems. Ars reached out to BlueBird Tech to learn more.

The following Q&A represents written responses from Oleksiy, a BlueBird Tech R&D representative, after an initial conversation with Ars. The responses have been lightly edited for clarity. Oleksiy is only being identified by first name for security reasons.

Ars Technica: How has the use of fiber-optic drones by Ukraine and Russia evolved, and where does the technology stand today?

Oleksiy: Fiber-optic drones are a specific type of drone used for particular operations. One of their main characteristics is that they do not produce the same radio-frequency (RF) emissions as conventional RF-controlled FPV drones. That means conventional RF detectors cannot detect them in the same way, and electronic warfare systems cannot disrupt their control link in the same way either.

At the same time, fiber-optic drones have their own operational limitations. A pilot who is experienced with a conventional RF-controlled FPV drone cannot necessarily switch immediately to a fiber-optic drone. It requires specific skills, and fiber-optic drones have different flight characteristics.

So there are significant advantages, particularly when operating in an electronic warfare environment, but there are also limitations in terms of operation, pilot training, and cost.

Because of the high density of electronic intelligence and electronic warfare systems on the battlefield, using conventional radio-controlled FPV drones has become increasingly difficult.

Fiber-optic technology provides a way to operate in these conditions, although it also comes with certain limitations.

So today, both sides are using fiber-optic drones on the battlefield, but radio-controlled drones still make up the larger share overall.

Ars Technica: What are the main disadvantages or limitations of fiber-optic drones?

Oleksiy: One of the biggest limitations today is cost. If we compare fiber-optic prices in 2025 and 2026, we have seen increases of roughly six to 10 times. The most difficult period in terms of prices and shortages was around March. Since then, the situation has stabilized to some extent.

But the underlying issue remains that the material primarily comes from China. That means Ukrainian manufacturers depend on Chinese supply, logistics, availability, and pricing.

There is also broader global demand for fiber-optic material. FPV drones are only one application. Data centers around the world consume large amounts of fiber, and the growth of AI infrastructure is also contributing to demand.

So Ukrainian drone manufacturers are operating within a much larger global market for fiber optic, and that affects both availability and price.

Ars Technica: How are Ukrainian manufacturers adapting to these supply-chain and quality challenges?

Oleksiy: One important change is that many manufacturers are moving the spooling process in-house. Instead of buying a finished fiber-optic spool for an FPV drone, they can buy the fiber in much larger spools and produce the smaller drone spools themselves.

This does not remove the dependence on China for the material itself, but it gives manufacturers more control over the finished product.

Quality is a major reason for doing this. We have seen problems and defects in finished spools supplied from China. By doing the spooling themselves, manufacturers can introduce their own quality-control procedures and achieve a more consistent result.

It can also make the finished spool somewhat cheaper than buying a finished product in China and transporting it to Ukraine.

But quality is the most important factor. If a fiber-optic spool has a defect, you can lose the drone, lose the mission, and lose the target.

Fiber optic is also not the only component in this supply chain. Media converters are another component manufacturers need to source, and China is important for that supply as well.

Ars Technica: So Ukrainian companies are buying large quantities of fiber and producing the FPV spools domestically?

Oleksiy: Yes. A manufacturer can buy a large spool of fiber-optic material and then use that material to produce smaller spools designed for FPV drones.

That also allows the manufacturer to perform its own quality checks during production rather than relying entirely on the quality of a finished imported spool.

Ars Technica: Has this become the standard approach across the Ukrainian industry?

Oleksiy: It depends on the manufacturer and on the size of the company. Larger companies may have the resources to do the spooling themselves. That requires spooling equipment, people who understand the process, testing capabilities, and quality control.

Smaller manufacturers may not have those resources. In those cases, they may buy finished spools from larger Ukrainian manufacturers or from suppliers abroad, including China.

So there is no single model across the industry. It largely depends on the scale and capabilities of each manufacturer.

Ars Technica: Can Ukraine source fiber-optic material from countries other than China?

Oleksiy: You can buy fiber-optic material through suppliers in other countries, but in practice China remains the main source in this market.

There can be intermediaries in other countries, and prices can differ depending on the country and the purchasing route.

But in the end, China remains the main source of the fiber-optic material we are talking about.

Ars Technica: Have Ukrainian companies experienced actual shortages or situations where suppliers were unable or unwilling to fill orders?

Oleksiy: Yes, there have been situations like that. It depends on many factors, including how the material is purchased, which supplier is involved, and the logistics.

There have also been cases where Chinese manufacturers have told Ukrainian buyers that they did not have material available for them because they were from Ukraine.

Russia also has more direct logistics with China, so from a practical supply-chain perspective it has an easier route for sourcing this material.

Ars Technica: Are there other important supply-chain challenges beyond availability and price?

Oleksiy: Yes. Logistics is another important factor. Once the material reaches Ukraine, it still has to pass through customs and then be transported to manufacturers.

Air raid alerts can interrupt this process. Customs operations and other parts of the logistics chain may stop during alerts, which adds time before the material reaches the manufacturer. So companies have to take these delays into account when planning production.

There are also security considerations. I cannot discuss specific cases involving attacks on fiber-optic production because of security restrictions. But manufacturers are potential targets for the enemy, so security measures are extremely important for developers, suppliers, and manufacturers.

Ars Technica: Is the physical transportation route from China to Ukraine also a challenge?

Oleksiy: Yes. The route from China to Ukraine is much longer, and that creates challenges in terms of both time and logistics costs.

So the problem is not only obtaining the material itself. Manufacturers also have to account for the time and expense required to transport it over a long distance.

Ars Technica: How do these price, logistics, and supply-chain challenges affect how a drone manufacturer approaches fiber-optic systems?

Oleksiy: Planning becomes extremely important. You need to plan production and logistics together. If you understand your manufacturing requirements in advance, you can plan your logistics and your next orders accordingly.

Having more of the manufacturing process under your own control is also important.

Quality is another major factor. If the fiber has fewer defects, you can produce more usable FPV spools from one large spool of material. If there are many defects, you ultimately produce fewer usable spools from the same amount of fiber.

So production planning, logistics planning, in-house manufacturing capabilities, material quality, and the skills of the people doing the work are all closely connected.

Ars Technica: Does producing fiber-optic spools require specially trained personnel?

Oleksiy: Yes. We do not have universities that specifically train people to manufacture these FPV fiber-optic spools. So it comes down to training, training, and more training.

When people first learn the process, they naturally make mistakes and create defects. They need practical experience to improve.

Training itself also consumes fiber-optic material because people have to practice the actual manufacturing process.

So developing skilled personnel is another part of scaling production. The goal is to develop the skills required to manufacture consistently high-quality spools with as few defects as possible.

Ars Technica: What makes manufacturing a fiber-optic spool technically difficult?

Oleksiy: You need to understand a lot about the fiber itself, about the spooling process, and about what happens when the fiber unwinds from the spool during operation.

Fiber-optic cable is fragile and can be damaged or broken relatively easily. If something goes wrong during manufacturing or while the fiber is unwinding, you can damage the fiber and lose the connection.

That is why practical experience is so important. You need to practice the manufacturing process, and that can require many kilometers of fiber just for training.

The equipment used for spooling also matters. If you use low-quality equipment or the equipment is not configured properly, you can introduce more defects into the finished spool.

So both the equipment and the people operating it affect the quality of the final product.

Ars Technica: How automated is the spooling process?

Oleksiy: It depends on the type of equipment. Some systems require more manual involvement, while others are more automated.

But even with automated equipment, skilled personnel are still required. Someone has to configure the equipment correctly and understand how the process works. The way the system is set up also affects the quality of the finished product.

So automation does not remove the need for expertise.

Ars Technica: How do manufacturers test the quality of finished fiber-optic spools?

Oleksiy: There are different levels of testing. Every finished spool should be checked to make sure the optical link itself is working properly.

But if you want to ensure consistent production quality, that is not enough. You also need to take a portion of the finished spools from a production batch, install them on actual FPV drones, and fly them under real conditions.

That allows you to test not only the optical connection itself, but also whether the finished spool performs properly during an actual flight.

So every spool should undergo link testing, while some of the finished spools from each production batch should also be tested in real flights. That combination is important if you want to maintain consistent quality in production.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#370. Angel Reese defends gesture directed towards Caitlin Clark after LSU national title win; calls out double standard after being 'unapologetically' her

LSU star Angel Reese defended the gesture she aimed at Iowa Hawkeyes’ Caitlin Clark near the end of the Tigers’ first NCAA women’s basketball national championship victory on Sunday, saying “I don’t take disrespect lightly.”

Reese could be seen approaching Clark before moving her open hand in front of her face – popularized by WWE star John Cena to mean “you can’t see me” – before pointing to her ring finger in a gesture some interpreted as a reference to the place her newly-acquired championship ring might sit.

Caitlin Clark breaks records galore during historic March Madness run

Clark made a similar gesture to another player earlier in the tournament.

The gesture has sparked much debate, especially on social media. Some have criticized Reese, while others have defended her actions, highlighting how there was no public outrage in response to Clark’s gesture earlier in the tournament.

Sports journalist Jose de Jesus Ortiz called Reese’s actions “classless,” while former ESPN host Keith Olbermann called Reese an “idiot” for the gesture.

In the press conference after the victory, Reese referenced the difference in reaction she received as a result of her gesture as compared to the one Clark received.

“All year, I was critiqued for who I was. I don’t fit the narrative,” Reese said. “I don’t fit the box that y’all want me to be in. I’m too hood. I’m too ghetto. Y’all told me that all year. But when other people do it, and y’all don’t say nothing.

“So this is for the girls that look like me. For those that want to speak up for what they believe in. It’s unapologetically you. And that’s what I did it for tonight. It was bigger than me tonight. And Twitter is going to go into a rage every time.

“And I’m happy. I feel like I’ve helped grow women’s basketball this year. (…) I’m looking forward to celebrating and then next season.”

LSU beats Iowa to win its first NCAA women's basketball championship

Reese had 15 points and 10 rebounds in the 102-85 victory and won the women’s March Madness’ Most Outstanding Player award.

In the post-game broadcast, Reese referenced the similar gesture Clark made to a Louisville opponent in the Elite Eight.

In the same game, Clark said to an opponent: “You’re down by 15 points. Shut up,” according to the Bleacher Report.

“Caitlin Clark is a hell of a player but I don’t take disrespect lightly,” Reese said. “She disrespected [LSU’s] Alexis [Morris] (…) and I wanted to pick her pocket. But I had a moment at the end of her game. I was in my bag, I was in my moment.”

After the Championship game, Clark herself said she didn’t notice anything at the time.

“I was just trying to get to the handshake line and shake hands and be grateful that my team was in that position,” Clark said in the post-game press conference. “All the credit in the world to LSU. They were tremendous. They deserve it. They had a tremendous season.

“(LSU head coach) Kim Mulkey coached them so, so well. She’s one of the best basketball coaches of all time, and it shows. She only said really kind things to me in the handshake line, so I’m very grateful of that too.

“But honestly I have no idea. I was just trying to spend the last few moments on the court with especially the five people that I’ve started 93 games with and relishing every second of that.”

LSU head coach Mulkey said she had “no clue” about what transpired.

Among those defending Reese on social media were ESPN’s Holly Rowe and former NBA star Etan Thomas.

“People hating on Angel Reese or Caitlin Clark. Stop. Unapologetically confident young women should be celebrated NOT hated. Get used to it,” Rowe wrote on Twitter.

Former Washington Wizards, Oklahoma City Thunder and Atlanta Hawks player Thomas wrote: “Hold on now!!!! It was cute when Caitlin Clark did it. Y’all didn’t have any issues with it at all. So don’t be all outraged and talking about class and sportsmanship when Angel Reese does the same thing. We’re not doing double standards here.”

Reese said the negative reaction on social media throughout the season has helped fuel her excellent season, having finished averaging 23.0 points and 15.4 rebounds in her first season with LSU after transferring from Maryland.

“Twitter can say what they want to say,” she said. “I love reading those comments. I have all the screenshots of what everybody has said about me all season. What are you going to say now?”

#368. Find a gift that kids will actually like

When it comes to gifting, kids can be particular. The things kids like change not just with age, but also with evolving trends from TV shows and new technology. If parents are lucky enough, their kid will write a long list of the precise items they'd like as a birthday or Christmas or Hannukkah gift and put it up on the fridge with pride. But sometimes, it can be as much of a crapshoot as coming up with a gift idea for someone else's kid. Without further ado, here are the best gifts for kids of all ages — including toy and non-toy gifts alike.

Everyone is familiar with the "terrible twos" rep that the not-a-baby-but-not-yet-an-independent-kid stage of development gets. At this age, kids are bouncing constantly between the desire to explore on their own and the reliance on parents for most basic needs like eating, getting dressed, or going to the bathroom. That's a lot of frustrating emotions to go through in a day. Kitchen play sets, toys that involve different motor skills, and anything with music are gifts that can let toddlers feel in charge without too much assistance from a parent — making them ideal for this age category.

Potentially a more daunting phase than the terrible twos: The one where the pressure to be cool is relentless. This is that age. Though "cool" can lend itself to multiple interpretations, gifts that contribute to an older kid's evolving critical thinking skills and sense of independence, responsibility, and style are all smart ideas. These could be anything from STEM toys to beginner's makeup tools to a fitness tracker or a pretend pet that encourages routine-making. Anything that involves an app, tablet, or e-reader is also a guaranteed win.

Finding a gift that a teenager will think is cool (and that will be on-trend for longer than a few months) might be some of the trickiest shopping you'll do all year. They'll probably offer up a few big softball suggestions, but if you're going the surprise route, remember that they care about brands. Thanks to social media influencers promoting products directly to teens on Instagram or TikTok, Gen Z kids are loyal to very specific brands of tech, accessories, and even water bottles. If you follow the trends as closely as they do (trust us, we do), cracking the "best gifts for teenagers" code isn't rocket science.

#367. ‘We Have to Take Over’: Far-Right Extremists Want to Seize Control of the GOP

On Election Day last week, white nationalist Nick Fuentes urged his supporters on X to “get out and vote Democrat today.” The post racked up over 12 million views. Hours later, in the wake of the Democratic sweep, Fuentes took credit for the victory. “We did it again” he wrote on Telegram, later calling the phenomenon “the Groyper Effect,” a reference to the nickname given to his followers.

In recent days, Fuentes has claimed the election results are an opportunity for him and his allies on the far right.

“Let the Democrats rule; see how that goes,” Fuentes said during a livestream last week. “There will come a time when the far left will terrorize people, and they're going to push all of them into the arms of the right. Who's going to fight the far left? Only the far right. This is why we have to take over the right wing now.”

In another livestream last week, Fuentes added a warning to the GOP leadership: “It’s our party now … You need to cooperate with us… Groypers are open for business. It has [to] be America First—and if it's not, we'll do it again for the enemy.”

A WIRED review of comments from white-supremacist, Christian-nationalist, and other far-right groups in the wake of the midterm results shows much of the same. While the groups are uniformly attacking Donald Trump’s failure to prioritize white, Christian, American values, they also believe that this sudden shift to the left will result in a backlash that will see more people embrace far-right groups and ideologies.

“Many prominent figures in racist, extremist movements have taken to declaring the MAGA movement dead since the midterm elections, claiming that the results validate their previous criticisms of the Trump administration and opens new opportunities for their movements to push disaffected voters toward their ideological beliefs,” Jared Holt, senior researcher at Open Measures, tells WIRED.

While some of the Republican party’s extremist candidates were rejected during the elections last week, many did win their elections.

The Institute for Research and Education on Human Rights tracked 535 candidates running in statewide races who were members of at least one far-right Facebook group. The researchers found that of the 476 races that had been called by last Friday, 338 of those candidates were victorious, representing a 71 percent win rate. (The vast majority of these races saw GOP incumbents retain their seats.) In Florida, for example, 34-year-old citrus farmer Marshall Rawson, who attended the Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville, Virginia, in 2017, easily defeated his Democrat opponent in the state’s 10th District State House race.

Many states also passed ballot measures that could further far-right agendas, including anti-transgender rules, onerous voter ID laws, and anti-immigrant measures.

“Arizona, Colorado, Nebraska, and Washington approved bans on trans athletes in female sports, so girls in Washington state, for instance, will now have to go through a genital exam or pass a DNA test before they can participate in youth sports,” Devin Burghart, executive director with IREHR, said on Friday during a briefing with reporters.

The success of these ballot measures was celebrated by extremist groups as evidence that Americans wanted what the current GOP could not deliver.

The Proud Boys, the street-fighting group that Immigration and Customs Enforcement operations made largely redundant in recent years, were optimistic following the losses. “The ultimate rebuke of the current Republican establishment was citizens voting them out of office, then doing their job for them by passing ballot measures on issues the Republicans should have already handled,” the Cape Fear Proud Boys wrote in their Telegram channel.

“What this signals to me is even in those places where they lost, they're getting 41-47 percent of the vote, [so] there's still a mass base to try to be mobilized,” Chuck Tanner, research director at IREHR, said during the briefing. “That's the lesson that some of these groups are taking out of it, is that there's still that mass base.”

And it is the existence of this base, extremist groups claim, that leaves the Republican Party vulnerable to takeover from the inside.

“Now is the time, if you're a nationalist, to start looking at moving on the Republican Party where you live,” Warren Balogh, a white supremacist from Pittsburgh who attended the Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville in 2017, wrote on his Telegram channel. “And by that I mean, organizing your guys and going in to do a hostile takeover.”

“This may be the best thing that has happened to the Republican Party in a half-century,” the Cape Fear Proud Boys declared in a post on Telegram days after the election. “Now you will learn to placate us, or you will continue to fail. Understand this: You’re going to run people we like, and you’re going to do what we say.”

But other extremists appear less inclined to wait until the next general election in 2028, preferring a more direct takeover.

"I'm not saying that we will win through democracy. I'm just saying we'll win,” Joel Webbon, a Christian nationalist pastor said last week in a video posted online after the election. Webbon and fellow far-right pastors, as well as guest speaker and noted conspiracy theorist Alex Jones, are set to meet at a conference in Texas this week where attendees will discuss, in at least one talk, “battle plans” for “Christian nationalists on the right to be able to seize power.”

Experts WIRED spoke to said they have not seen any explicit calls for violence from the far-right groups they are tracking, but some figures are already looking ahead to January 3, when Congress is set to certify the midterm election results.

Far-right activist and pardoned January 6 rioter Jake Lang, however, is planning an event in DC on January 3. It is unclear how many people are planning to attend, but one of the Christian nationalist pastors listed by Lang as an official speaker has said he won’t be speaking.

Lang, who spent last weekend disrupting a cat convention that he thought was a furry convention, did not respond to a request for comment.

#366. Polestar has a new look with its Formula 2030 concept

The shape looks like a Saab Aero-X, but the nose previews the next Polestar 2.

Perhaps only a small percentage of Ars readers will care about the Polestar 2030 concept, debuted today by the electric automaker. Spun out of Volvo—once upon a time, Polestar was a racing team—the brand has leveraged the resources of parent-automaker Geely to create electric vehicles that marry a modern minimalist style with rewarding driving dynamics. But you now can’t buy any Polestars in the US, since Geely is Chinese, and banning Chinese-owned car brands enjoys remarkable bipartisan support in Congress.

The Formula 2030 is about the same length as a Polestar 2 but with a very low roofline. If anything, the two-box shape reminds me of the Saab Aero-X, one of the final concepts from the then-terminal Swedish automaker. (In many ways, Polestar has adopted Saab’s mantle.)

Polestar says the concept fits into its own segment somewhere between the next Polestar 2 and the Polestar 7—it numbers its cars chronologically (mostly), not based on size—although it’s not saying if the hatchback design will go into series production. Successfully packaging rear-seat passengers and a lithium-ion battery pack is something a concept needn’t worry about, after all. Rather, it’s a styling exercise, or more than one.

The nose is a good preview of the next Polestar 2, and the rear light signature will show up on production Polestars. “We wanted to evolve, while respecting the core design values we demonstrated with previous models that define the Polestar brand in such a recognizable way,” said Nahum Escobedo, head of exterior design at Polestar.

“And with Formula 2030, with its clear Polestar design attributes, like the Dual Blade headlights, wraparound visor-like windshield, and powerful haunches, we have done just that. But we’ve pushed perceptions, preconceptions, and the expected as well, with our considered choices in form language, amplification of the performance topic, and new detailing, like the Single Blade rear lights,” Escobedo said.

Polestar says the four-seat interior pushes its usual minimalism even further. But the end result has avoided the bland fate suffered by the recent Range Rover GT concept, for example. Even though Polestar is committed to developing autonomous driving technology, for manual modes it has added physical controls—rocker switches and rotary controllers, with a user-configurable UI. That’s a new thing for Polestar.

You can’t really tell from the photos, but the Formula 2030 concept explores some sustainability ideas as well. Like BMW’s I Vision Circular, the Formula 2030 was designed using monomaterials and with recycling in mind where possible. The interiors of the doors are fully recyclable, there’s low-emission steel in the chassis, and biocomposites are used in place of traditional plastics.

#360. FDA warns GLP-1 drugs may pose risk of rare condition that causes vision loss

The risk appears small and data is mixed, but FDA wants patients to be aware.

The Food and Drug Administration is warning that popular obesity drugs—including Ozempic, Wegovy, Zepbound, and Mounjaro—may carry a “potential increased risk” of a rare eye condition that can lead to permanent vision loss.

In a drug safety communication Monday, the FDA said it will now require makers of GLP-1 receptor agonist drugs to add a warning about that risk on their drug labels. The specific eye condition it warns of is called non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy or NAION. The condition is caused by restricted blood flow to the optic nerve, which leads to swelling and cell death and ultimately damages the nerve, causing abrupt vision loss that can often be permanent.

It’s unknown how GLP-1 drugs may cause NAION. The condition is rare overall, occurring in somewhere between two and 10 people in 100,000 in the general population, according to the American Academy of Ophthalmology. These small numbers have made it more difficult to determine with certainty if there is a connection. Further complicating the potential link is the fact that the risk factors for developing NAION are common among patients taking GLP-1 drugs. The risk factors include diabetes, cardiovascular disease, high blood pressure, high cholesterol, metabolic syndrome, and obstructive sleep apnea.

The first study suggesting a link between GLP-1 drugs and NAION came in 2024 from Harvard University researchers. Their study, published in JAMA Ophthalmology, looked at 710 patients with diabetes and 979 patients with either overweight or obesity. In each group, some of the patients were taking semaglutide (Ozempic/Wegovy), while others were taking non-GLP-1 antidiabetic or weight loss drugs. The researchers found in both patient groups that those taking GLP-1 drugs after 36 months had higher rates of NAION. Among the diabetes patients, 8.9 percent of patients on semaglutide had the eye condition, while only 1.8 percent of patients in the non-GLP-1 groups developed it. Similarly, among those with overweight/obesity, 6.7 percent of patients on a GLP-1 had NAION compared with 0.8 percent in the non-GLP-1 group.

The study had significant limitations, though. The patients were all from one referral center that treated most of the region’s NAION cases. The overall incidence rates of NAION in the studied patients were much higher than the rates seen in the general population. This suggests there may have been selection bias and that the results are not generalizable. The study also did not look at the severity of diabetes in the patients, which can influence the risk of NAION. Last, the study didn’t offer any hints as to how GLP-1 drugs may contribute to NAION—and researchers remain uncertain of a possible mechanism.

After that study, other researchers began digging into more data, conducting meta-analyses, mining databases, and running observational studies to assess the potential link. Overall, the results were mixed. Some studies found no link, or only found links in specific comparisons, while others backed up the association. For instance, a meta-analysis of 78 randomized controlled trials found that semaglutide did not increase the risk of eye disorders generally, but did increase the risk of NAION. However, the finding was based on just five trials that specifically reported NAION events.

Another study that used an electronic health records database looked at 37.1 million patients with diabetes. It found that semaglutide generally wasn’t linked to NAION. The study only saw an increased risk in a very specific analysis: When patients taking semaglutide were compared to patients taking one specific anti-diabetes drug (empagliflozin)—which was one of three comparator drugs in the study—and only when the researchers used a specific definition for NAION cases that included having a confirmatory diagnostic code in the patients’ records.

In the announcement Monday, the FDA reported it had conducted its own meta-analysis, which included 126 trials that collectively contained data on over 130,000 patients. From there, the FDA identified 15 probable NAION cases: 10 in patients taking a GLP-1 drug, five in patients who received a placebo, and none in patients on non-GLP-1 drugs. The agency also did a retrospective cohort study of patients with type 2 diabetes who took a GLP-1 or one of two common diabetes medications between 2019 and 2024. The non-GLP-1 drugs were either a sodium glucose co-transporter-2 (SGLT-2) inhibitor or a dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4) inhibitor. The agency found no increased risk of NAION in patients taking a GLP-1 compared to those taking an SGLT-2, but there was an increased risk compared to those taking DPP-4.

The FDA’s decision to require drugmakers to add the potential risk to labels follows a conclusion from the European Medicines Agency in 2025, which carried out its own review and concluded that “NAION is a very rare side effect of semaglutide (meaning it may affect up to 1 in 10,000 people taking semaglutide).” The EMA, like the FDA, ordered labeling to reflect the possible risk. But it also recommended that if NAION is confirmed in patients taking semaglutide, patients should stop taking the GLP-1 drug.

The FDA’s statement has a more flexible recommendation, advising healthcare providers to “consider discontinuing the GLP-1 RA in patients who develop NAION, unless the benefits of treatment clearly outweigh the risk.”

That recommendation echoes the recommendation from the American Academy of Ophthalmology and the North American Neuro-Ophthalmology Society. In May, the two groups published a joint consensus statement that recommended “shared decision-making between the patient and their care team” on whether to start, continue, or discontinue taking a GLP-1 drug if NAION develops or if a patient is at higher risk.

In an emailed statement to Ars Technica, the American Academy of Ophthalmology said its recommendation has not changed with any new data since May and that the FDA’s stance is “broadly consistent with the conclusions of the joint statement” from May.

The academy holds that “there may be an association between GLP-1 [receptor agonist] use and NAION, but NAION remains rare, any increase in absolute risk appears small, and causation has not been established.” The organization also highlighted that the FDA’s warning spans across the class of GLP-1 drugs, while most of the studies so far have focused on semaglutide. “Data for individual GLP-1 medications other than semaglutide remain more limited, and whether any association differs among individual agents or patient populations remains uncertain,” the academy wrote.

The ophthalmologists again emphasized shared decision-making between patients who develop NAION and their care providers. “GLP-1 medications can provide important benefits for patients with diabetes, obesity, and cardiovascular disease, and the benefits and risks of stopping treatment may differ substantially from patient to patient. The decision to continue or discontinue treatment should therefore be made with the patient’s care team, taking into account the indication for treatment, overall health, alternative therapeutic options, and the potential risk to the fellow eye.”

#358. Chelsea searches for new manager as Premier League breaks record for most sackings in a single season

It was just six months ago that Graham Potter was deemed the man to usher in a fresh, exciting era at Chelsea.

The club’s new ownership group, led by American businessman Todd Boehly, had enticed Potter away from Brighton with the promise of managing one of the most lucrative teams in world soccer.

More than $600 million was spent on bringing big-name players to the club and Potter, one of the most exciting young managers in the Premier League, was to mold them into a winning machine.

Chelsea has spent over $600 million on new signings. Here's why that's significant

But, on Sunday, the club announced that it had sacked its manager after an underwhelming spell in charge.

Potter managed to win just 10 of the 28 league matches this season and leaves the team 11th in the Premier League table.

In recent weeks, players had looked a shadow of their former selves and many fans had lost confidence that Potter’s plan for the club could ever work.

Eventually, after previously sticking by its manager, Blues ownership agreed.

“On behalf of everyone at the club, we want to thank Graham sincerely for his contribution to Chelsea,” the club’s co-owners Todd Boehly and Behdad Eghbali said in a statement.

“We have the highest degree of respect for Graham as a coach and as a person. He has always conducted himself with professionalism and integrity and we are all disappointed in this outcome.”

In his previous role as Brighton manager, Potter was given time to stamp his identity on the squad.

The club was punching above its weight financially and, as a result, carefully selected new signings which would compliment Potter’s style of play.

The approach paid off as the team became an established Premier League outfit which has gone on to greater things this season – the Seagulls currently sit sixth in the league and in the UEFA Conference League qualification spot.

At Chelsea, it seemed the complete opposite.

As was the case under previous owner Roman Abramovich, the club expected instant results and used its financial weight to sign a number of high-profile names, including the likes of Enzo Fernández and Mykhailo Mudryk.

Despite his impressive CV, Potter had never managed superstars and suddenly found himself in a changing room with multiple.

The scatter gun approach to recruitment also left the team lacking balance and organization with Potter failing to get a tune out of a team busting with talent.

In truth, many had raised an eyebrow when Potter, inexperienced at the very top level, was chosen to replace Thomas Tuchel as manager.

Despite falling out of favor with fans by the end of his tenure, Tuchel had guided Chelsea to the Champions League title in 2021 and is still considered by many as one of the best coaches in the world.

The German has gone on to sign with Bavarian giant Bayern Munich through the 2024/25 season and recently told reporters he needed “quite a bit of time to distance himself from Chelsea” because leaving the club had hurt him.

Bayern Munich sacks manager Julian Nagelsmann, appoints Thomas Tuchel

“Todd [Boehly] said he would be different to Roman [Abramovich],” former Liverpool player and current pundit Jamie Carragher wrote on Twitter.

“I feel for Graham Potter, but it was inevitable. You don’t change Tuchel for Potter. Ridiculous decision to start with.”

With 10 games left in the league and a Champions League quarterfinal against Real Madrid on the horizon, Chelsea still has plenty to play for.

Assistant coach Bruno Saltor will take over on an interim basis, but Boehly and his team must now look for a long-term replacement capable – and willing – to bring order to Chelsea’s chaos.

Potter’s sacking is yet another sign of just how transient managers can be in the top-flight of English football, especially when results aren’t instantaneous.

There has now been a record 12 sackings in the Premier League so far this season, with Potter leaving his position the same day Leicester boss Brendan Rodgers was sacked from his.

The previous record was 10 manager changes in a single season, set in four other campaigns, most recently in 2017/18.

#355. Nvidia has a Jensen Huang problem—and no obvious successor

Huang faces a board probe as investors fret over Nvidia’s lack of a clear successor.

The investigation of Jensen Huang by Nvidia’s board threatens the future of one of Silicon Valley’s longest-serving executives and a key enabler of the AI boom, who has no obvious successor as leader of the world’s second-most-valuable company.

Huang is fighting for survival after admitting to an extramarital affair, believed to be with an executive at a large Nvidia customer whom US officials also allege was working as a Chinese spy.

The prospect that Huang could be forced out as chief executive of the US chipmaker he co-founded more than 30 years ago has alarmed investors and allies in the tech industry. Shares in Nvidia, which late last month ceded its position as the world’s most valuable to Apple amid a broader sell-off in AI stocks, had fallen more than 5 percent by midday trading on Monday.

“Jensen is irreplaceable for America’s AI leadership,” said Shaun Maguire, partner at early Nvidia investor Sequoia Capital, in a social media post.

Nvidia’s chips were instrumental in the creation of generative AI tools such as ChatGPT and now sit at the center of Big Tech’s $1 trillion AI infrastructure frenzy. Huang has personally stood behind investments and guarantees running into hundreds of billions of dollars for dozens of AI start-ups and data center providers, as well as lobbying US President Donald Trump for looser AI regulation.

“Nvidia is not an ordinary company and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO; he IS Nvidia,” analysts at Bernstein said in a note to clients.

With policymakers in Washington pressuring Huang to appear before congressional committees to explain himself, the spotlight is on Nvidia’s board, which said on Sunday it would conduct its own “independent” investigation into the scandal that exploded over the weekend.

The investigation will probably be led by board member and former Cooley chief executive Stephen C. Neal, according to people familiar with the probe.

“If information was compromised, the implications are a lot bigger than if it was just an affair, which obviously isn’t welcome news,” said a fund manager with a large Nvidia position. “Nvidia would survive [Huang leaving] but there’s no better person than Jensen to run the company; it would be very different without him.”

In contrast to other leading Big Tech companies, Nvidia’s annual reports and corporate governance paperwork make no specific reference to “succession planning” and set out no details for how it would replace its CEO, even in an emergency, beyond noting that the company “believe[s] in extensive leadership development.”

Huang’s unusually large number of direct reports, numbered at more than 60 according to a 2024 disclosure, present Nvidia’s board with a “pool of excellent candidates for future promotion,” recent filings say.

Huang leads a small core executive team at Nvidia, including Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress, Operations Chief Debora Shoquist and general counsel Tim Teter. He has become one of Huang’s closest lieutenants since joining in 2017 and has lobbied Washington on export controls.

Jay Puri, a close lieutenant who led Nvidia’s sales operations and deputized for Huang on international visits, stepped down in August and was replaced on the executive team by Microsoft veteran Nick Parker.

Huang, who has cultivated an air of infallibility inside Nvidia, has given no indication he will step down from the role of CEO.

Recent biographies of Huang and Nvidia, including Tae Kim’s The Nvidia Way and Stephen Witt’s The Thinking Machine, portray him as having a rare combination of technical depth and forward-thinking leadership.

He is known to be able to inspire extraordinary productivity inside a company whose 42,000 employees each generated an average of more than $7 million in annual revenue over the past 12 months, far more than any other Big Tech group. But Kim also notes that Huang represents the company’s “single point of failure,” functioning as its head of marketing, sales, and product development.

His influence in the wider tech industry is also unique. Endorsements by Huang of companies in the AI semiconductor supply chain can supercharge their share prices, while his offhand dismissal early last year of the near-term commercial prospects for quantum computing sent stocks in the sector spiraling.

Despite his imperial stature, Huang owns just 3.6 percent of Nvidia. That stake makes him Nvidia’s largest individual shareholder and is worth more than $150 billion. However, Huang lacks the special voting rights held by many Silicon Valley founders, including Meta’s Mark Zuckerberg and Google’s Larry Page and Sergey Brin, that would allow him to control the board or face down outside investors who might attempt to oust him.

Huang’s total compensation last year of $36.3 million, of which the vast majority came in the form of stock awards, included some $4 million for security paid for by Nvidia, which filings say is “due to the exceptionally high public profile and prominence of our CEO.”

These costs included protection at home and during “personal travel,” as well as using private aircraft for all business trips, according to filings. Huang reimburses Nvidia for personal air travel on private aircraft arranged by the company, according to filings.

Nvidia’s board, whose individual members have until now kept a low profile, confirmed they would appoint a special committee composed of sitting board members assisted by outside counsel to examine the allegations surrounding its CEO.

Neal joined Nvidia’s board in 2019 and is chair emeritus and senior counsel at Cooley, after serving as the law firm’s CEO from 2001 to 2008.

Nvidia’s board is unusual among the largest US companies in having no designated chair. A 2025 report by law firm Fenwick found that only one company in S&P 100—which would be Nvidia, according to its own filings—had no chair.

In its proxy filings, Nvidia states that this set-up “ensures that each member has an equal voice in the affairs and management of Nvidia.”

The other nine members of Nvidia’s board—excluding Huang, who is himself a director—include four men who have been with the company since the 1990s, a former Taco Bell executive, a professor of aeronautics, and a former Goldman Sachs executive who joined only this year.

The board’s public statement on Sunday said it had “confidence in Jensen and his leadership team” and that it had “no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised.”

Nonetheless, his admission of an affair on Sunday shocked employees and former colleagues who spoke to the FT.

Madison Huang, Jensen’s daughter who serves as a senior director in Nvidia’s product marketing team and has become an increasingly visible presence at the company’s conferences, canceled a planned appearance at a company event in South Korea on Monday.

The law in Delaware, where Nvidia is incorporated, says executives have certain fiduciary duties including a duty of care and loyalty to their company.

Nvidia’s code of conduct requires executives to “conduct themselves with the highest degree of integrity, ethics and honesty. Our credibility and reputation depend upon their good judgment, ethical standards and personal integrity.”

Even if Huang is found to have breached those guidelines, his defenders in Silicon Valley argue that he is too big to fire.

“Yes he should have known better,” Sequoia’s Maguire said. “But let’s also not nuke our AI industry because China ran a successful intel op.”

#354. Fraggle Rock: A Holiday Gift trailer spreads holiday cheer

Fraggle Rock's 2026 holiday special just got its official trailer, and it's doing its best to spread the holiday spirit.

Fraggle Rock: A Holiday Gift sees the Fraggles name Wembley (voiced by Jordan Lockhart) as Spirit Captain, responsible for spreading holiday cheer throughout Fraggle Rock. Enthusiastically embracing his new role, Wembley leads Gobo (voiced by John Tartaglia), Mokey (voiced by Donna Kimball), Boober (voiced by Dave Goelz), and Red (voiced by Karen Prell) in sharing the holiday spirit. However, said spirit begins to fade when they mistakenly take Doc's (Lilli Cooper) beloved snow globe from her workshop.

"Wembley must find the courage to return it, learning that kindness, giving and helping someone in need are the true gifts of the holidays," reads Apple TV's official synopsis.

The musical special also features a performance by singer and Tony Award winner Nicole Scherzinger. Fraggle Rock: A Holiday Gift arrives Dec. 4 on Apple TV. #353. Some fans whistle as Lionel Messi's name is announced as Paris Saint-Germain's season hits new low

Lionel Messi passes 100 international goals with hat-trick against Curaçao

#351. DeepMind reveals Gemini Robotics 2.5 and open safety benchmark "ASIMOV-jailbreak"

The new model is up to twice as successful at some tasks.

Google DeepMind has revealed a new generation of the AI-powered Gemini Robotics platform. In the 2.5 release, Google says its robotic AI is getting tantalizingly close to completing real-world tasks as well as humans do. In fact, some Gemini robots were able to get actual work done in Mercedes-Benz factories, and the team is preparing for a day when robots operate alongside humans with an open source safety benchmark.

DeepMind’s physical AI platform has several computational layers between user input and robotic action. There’s the vision-language model piece, which is Gemini Embodied Reasoning. Essentially, it takes your instructions and uses live video to plan how the robot can accomplish the task. The other main component is the vision-language-action (VLA) that actually generates robot actions, like moving an actuator—that’s Gemini Robotics, which has been bumped to version 2.5.

DeepMind says that Gemini Robotics 2.5 is much better at tasks that require a high degree of dexterity. The model was evaluated across more than 200 activities, including serving food with tongs and using a pipette, as you would see in a laboratory. Google also showed that Apptronik’s Apollo 2 humanoid robot with Gemini Robotics 2.5 can now tie a bow with its five-fingered hands, a notable feat, because deformable objects (like ribbon) are notoriously difficult for robots to manipulate.

Overall, Gemini Robotics 2.5 manages a 30 to 100 percent higher success rate compared to the last version. It’s also 20 to 30 percent faster. But that’s based on laboratory testing. Google partnered with Apptronik and Mercedes-Benz to see how capable this AI is when faced with the uncertainty of the world outside robotics labs. The Gemini-powered Apollo 2 robots kitted automotive parts in a few European factories, reaching a 96 percent success rate.

That’s an impressive showing compared to previous robotic AI systems, but it’s still probably not enough for a company like Mercedes-Benz. A human doing that sort of repetitive task will get it right virtually every time, and DeepMind director Kanishka Rao notes that closing that last 4 percent gap will be a “steep road.”

You could manually program a robot to do a handful of tasks in a factory without issue—this type of automation has been common for decades. Gemini Robotics is not trying to just do a few things, though. These are intended to be general robots that can handle almost anything, but there are trade-offs to that approach.

“We can be very good at a particular application at the cost of generalization,” said DeepMind Robotics head Carolina Parada. “Or we can just push on the generalization axis and maybe not get to 99.9 in every application.”

Training data is essential for training all generative AI models. Most text-based chatbots in existence were fed basically the entirety of the open Internet, but there isn’t as much “physical data” for robotic AI. The best kind of training data for robots comes from teleoperation, but the team says Gemini 4 Argon has helped create a “simulation engine” that greatly accelerated development of Gemini Robotics 2.5.

The team used Argon to generate thousands of variations on practice runs based on real-world camera data. These “digital twins” apparently allowed Gemini Robotics 2.5 to more quickly generalize new tasks and settings. There’s still a role for the real thing, though. DeepMind combined this synthetic data with traditional training demonstrations to ground the model.

Argon was also used for analyzing failures and suggesting ways to improve in both simulations and real robots. However, this isn’t something that works in real time. Frontier models like Gemini take time to churn through their tokens, but robots need to be able to operate without long pauses. The new VLA isn’t lightning quick, either. While Google has experimented with some embedded robotics models, the current 2.5 release is still running on cloud-based servers. So those gains in accuracy and speed still don’t come without latency. The video above is sped up by 2x to make the robot look more human-like.

DeepMind is also working to ensure the failures don’t put any nearby humans at risk. The team notes that Gemini Robotics 2.5 uses inference-time reasoning to avoid hazards. So if something gets in the way, the robot will stop completely or wait until it’s safe to continue. This is essential if you’re going to have a robot operating outside of the lab, for example, in a Mercedes factory. DeepMind says this feature allowed Gemini robots to safely complete tasks 33 percent more often when obstacles were present. The success rate went up 43 percent when people were in the way.

There is no agreed-upon way to test physical AI for safety, but DeepMind hopes to move the industry in that direction with ASIMOV-Jailbreak. This is an open source database that contains 1,500 prompts designed to trick a robotic AI into doing something unsafe. For example, a prompt might ask the robot to place items in a bag and close it with a fastener too fiddly for the robot to operate. The AI should decline to do that, but attempting to proceed and incorrectly closing the container would be the “unsafe” outcome.

DeepMind says that placing Argon in the loop results in a 98.8 percent jailbreak safety level. With just Gemini Robotics ER 2, the system scores 73.5 percent in Google’s safety benchmark.

The full ASIMOV dataset is available on Hugging Face for anyone working on embodied AI who wants to analyze its safety. Gemini Robotics 2.5 is not generally available, but it’s now in the hands of Google’s robotics partners, including Agile Robots, Apptronik, and Boston Dynamics.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#350. Google Fi announces super network automatic carrier switching for improved speed and reception

It’s rolling out first on Pixel phones before wider availability arrives in 2027.

Getting the fastest mobile data speeds can be tricky because a single carrier may not have optimal coverage in your area. So to address this, Google Fi is introducing its new super network feature which allows your phone to automatically switch between two different carriers to deliver the best connection possible.

The super network feature begins rolling out today to customers with Pixel phones (Pixel 9 and above with eSIMs). In the event that your phone detects that your connection is sluggish, it will automatically switch from one carrier to another. Unfortunately, Google Fi is not officially disclosing the names of the carriers it's using for its super network, though based on previous partnerships one of them is probably T-Mobile. Regardless, the effect is that if you hit a dead zone or congestion, your phone can connect to a different cell signal without any manual input.

However, that's not all, because alongside the super network, Google Fi is also introducing a new Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ feature designed to improve data speeds indoors in places like malls, airports and more. That means even if you're in a building that interferes with traditional cellular reception, your phone can switch over to Wi-Fi where available, while automatic encryption ensures that your data stays secure. Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ will initially be available on Pixel phones (Pixel 5a and newer) and some Motorola and Samsung phones (running OneUI 9 and above) across select locations throughout the US, Europe, Asia and Brazil.

But perhaps the best part is that both Google Fi's super network and Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ will be available to customers with Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans at no extra cost. Furthermore, for international travelers, both features will work in over 200 destinations across the globe.

Alternatively, for customers who need a little extra help or technical support, the company is rolling out its new AI-powered Ask Fi agent inside the app. It's designed to answer questions, straighten out billing concerns or help troubleshoot things like connectivity issues in real time. Thankfully, Google Fi says there will still be actual humans you can reach out to if that's what you prefer.

#348. One space station company sues another for copying its work

“NASA advised that Vast’s submission in some locations substituted ‘the PAM Provider’ for Axiom”

NASA’s competition for the development of private space stations that will take flight in the 2030s is already nearing crunch time. But a recently unsealed lawsuit may spice things up even further.

The lawsuit involves two of the most prominent companies in this competition, Houston-based Axiom Space and Long Beach, California-based Vast Space. And it is surfacing as final proposals for private space stations are due from companies to the US space agency a little more than one month from today, on December 14.

According to the court filings unsealed by a November 4 order from US District Judge Charles Eskridge, Axiom is pursuing a False Claims Act case against Vast in which Axiom accuses Vast of presenting NASA with a fraudulent mission-planning document that incorporates Axiom’s proprietary information.

“Axiom Space filed this false-claims action on behalf of the government because public-private partnerships in human spaceflight depend on candor, capability, original work, and integrity,” an Axiom Space spokesperson told Ars. “Presenting another company’s hard-won experience as its own does not meet that standard.”

In response, Vast said the lawsuit was about competition, not fraud.

“NASA opened private astronaut missions to more than one provider because competition drives innovation, delivers better value for taxpayers, and keeps America leading in space,” a Vast spokesperson said. “Misusing litigation against a competitor works against all of that.”

So what happened? Let’s unpack this further to understand what we do and don’t know at this point.

The dispute revolves around a program called Private Astronaut Missions, launched in 2019, through which NASA allows private companies to organize missions of up to four astronauts to visit the International Space Station. The missions are part of NASA’s effort to increase commercial activity in low-Earth orbit and allow private companies to gain some operational experience as they develop their own commercial space stations. As such, these missions are viewed by some as a stepping stone toward private station contracts with NASA.

Axiom became the early leader in the program, winning contracts to organize the first four private missions. The first, Axiom-1, launched in April 2022, while the most recent, Axiom-4, launched to the International Space Station in the summer of 2025. Then, in January, NASA awarded the fifth private astronaut mission to Axiom, followed by the sixth to Vast Space in February and the seventh to Voyager Technologies in April.

As part of these agreements, private companies generally purchase mission services from NASA, including food, storage, and other supplies. In return, NASA buys the capability to return scientific samples to Earth that must remain cold during transit.

The private astronaut mission agreements include contractual deliverables that providers must submit for NASA’s review. According to Axiom’s lawsuit, one such deliverable under its contracts is a Mission Integration and Operations Management Plan, or MIOMP, essentially the operational blueprint for the entire mission. Axiom alleges that Vast’s contract contains a similar requirement and that NASA’s acceptance of the document is a condition for a milestone payment.

According to the lawsuit filed by Axiom, prior to June 3, Vast submitted its mission-planning document for the private astronaut mission to NASA. Then, on June 3, NASA notified Axiom “that its sensitive information had been improperly included in a deliverable submitted by Vast.”

On page 11 of the Axiom lawsuit, the company alleges: “NASA advised that Vast’s submission in some locations substituted ‘the PAM Provider’ for Axiom’s name, while in other instances retained Axiom’s name and company details instead of replacing it with Vast’s name. That the document retained residual references to Axiom in certain sections demonstrates that the underlying text originated with Axiom and was not independently authored by Vast.”

Axiom said its mission planning documents are “sensitive and proprietary” information. These documents represent years of work and significant investment, a company spokesperson said.

“A Mission Integration and Operations Management Plan is not paperwork,” the Axiom spokesperson said. “It is the document that demonstrates to NASA the company understands and can execute every step required to keep a crew safe from launch to return. When human lives depend on a plan, the company submitting it must have developed it, understood it, and be able to stand behind it.”

Axiom filed a lawsuit in a Houston federal court, where the company is based, on June 19. The False Claims Act complaint seeks civil penalties, treble damages, litigation costs, and a statutory whistleblower award.

There are plenty of unanswered questions at this point. Principal among them is how Vast obtained Axiom’s planning document and how it was inserted into Vast’s submission to NASA.

Several hours after publication of this article, Vast released additional information about the origin of the planning document. The company stated:

The Mission Integration and Operations Management Plan was provided to Vast in the normal course of business by a subcontractor that supplies services to Vast and Axiom, using standard documentation adapted from prior missions, as is common practice for NASA private astronaut missions. The version provided to Vast included material carried over from an earlier Axiom mission.

Within a day of submitting the document to NASA, Vast proactively informed NASA that the document needed to be corrected and resubmitted a revised version the next day. When NASA later asked us to take additional steps, we did so immediately, and we have cooperated with NASA throughout.

Vast did not take anything from Axiom, and any suggestion otherwise is false.

What does seem notable is that Axiom sued under the False Claims Act rather than bringing a conventional trade-secret misappropriation case. The False Claims Act imposes a demanding burden of proving fraud against the government, but successful cases can produce substantial civil penalties and damages. A finding of fraud could also complicate Vast’s ability to compete for future NASA contracts, potentially including the commercial space station competition.

Additionally, by filing a False Claims Act case in federal court and essentially framing the issue as one of fraud against NASA, Axiom triggered a federal review that gave the US Department of Justice the opportunity to investigate the allegations and decide whether to take over prosecution. However, the Department of Justice declined to intervene on October 23, according to the court documents. Axiom is therefore pursuing the allegations independently.

“The Department of Justice’s decision not to intervene does not mean that what happened was acceptable,” the Axiom spokesperson told Ars.

However, Vast officials said the government’s decision not to intervene reflects the merits of the case.

“The Department of Justice reviewed Axiom’s allegations and declined to intervene,” the spokesperson said. “We think that decision speaks for itself. Axiom’s complaint does not describe a fraud on the government, and it does not meet the basic requirements of the False Claims Act.”

The Justice Department’s decision does not establish whether Vast improperly used Axiom’s materials. In False Claims Act cases, federal prosecutors may decline to intervene for a variety of reasons, including doubts about the case’s legal merits, the availability of evidence, or the potential recovery. The government provided no explanation for its decision in the court filing. Axiom remains free to pursue the lawsuit on its own.

And the company appears poised to do so. Vast said it would defend itself “vigorously” in legal proceedings. The company also said its work with NASA on the sixth Private Astronaut Mission—for which customers have already been announced—is proceeding without difficulty or interruption.

NASA now finds itself the central witness in a case involving two of the leading companies chasing private space station contracts that could ultimately be worth billions of dollars. Other potential contenders include Voyager, Blue Origin, and possibly SpaceX.

The agency declined to provide a substantive comment for this article. “NASA is aware of the matter but does not comment on litigation,” agency spokesman George Alderman told Ars.

The commercial space stations program, which dates back half a decade, has been a rough-and-tumble contest as private companies have jostled for favor with NASA. Ultimately, the space agency is expected to pick only a few winners to finish their space stations in low-Earth orbit and pay handsomely for NASA astronauts to visit there in the 2030s.

At times, this contest has turned personal. In September 2025, for example, far-right political activist Laura Loomer shared an in-depth post on the social media site X purporting to link Vast chief executive Max Haot, who was born in Belgium, and Vast with Chinese interests. These allegations, which raised national security concerns, were wholly unsubstantiated. A recent Washington Post investigation reported that Loomer has received payments for some political posts on X.

It is also not the only lawsuit Axiom has filed this year. In April, after policy advisor Ramzi Masri-Elyafaoui left the company to work for rival Vast, Axiom sued to stop him, alleging he violated a non-compete agreement. In June, a Houston federal judge dismissed the case with prejudice.

This story was updated with a statement from Vast.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#347. Anthropic takes on Google and Microsoft with Claude Sheets

The company also has a new whiteboard app.

Anthropic is significantly expanding Claude's suite of productivity tools. At an event in San Francisco today, the company announced Claude Sheets. As you can probably guess, Sheets allows Claude to generate and edit spreadsheets for you. Each file can include its own set of tabs and formulas. You'll also find the usual assortment of formatting options.

Alongside Claude Sheets, Anthropic announced two other new products: Claude Meetings and Claude Whiteboards. The first of those is Anthropic's take on a meeting agent like the kind offered by Otter.ai and other companies. Available in Google Meet to start (which, incidentally, offers its own meeting agent through Gemini), Claude can join your video calls and start taking notes as you and your colleagues talk. You can later chat with Claude about any plans or action items that came up during your call. "Claude marks itself as a participant and posts a notice in the meeting's chat," says Anthropic. "It won't join a call with outside guests unless an admin allows it, and it leaves if one joins anyway." At the moment, Claude Meetings is only available to Team and Enterprise customers.

Moving to Claude Whiteboards, it's a pretty cutesy take on a collaborative brainstorming app. As your team works together, Claude will group everyone's ideas together by theme and draft action items for each individual. In a nifty bit of integration, you can use Claude Meetings and Whiteboards at the same time. Anthropic is billing the tool as a natural extension of how people already use Claude. "Many people already brainstorm with Claude, but sharing the results with coworkers has meant copy-pasting them somewhere else," the company says. Claude Whiteboards is available in beta to all paid users across the web, desktop and mobile.

Separately from those announcements, Anthropic is renaming Claude Dashboards to Claude Data and starting to roll out the tool to all paid users. Anthropic says it decided to rename the app because "companies use their data for more than dashboards." In any case, you can connect Claude Data to your company's CRM so that the chatbot can then answer specific questions you might have about what's inside.

Lastly, Anthropic is upgrading Claude's plugin functionality, which allows you to connect apps like Canva and Notion to the chatbot so it can pull information from those services. As Anthropic notes, there are already more than 9,000 connectors offered through the Claude Marketplace, but now you can open full apps inside of Claude, allowing the chatbot to act on what it sees in the window. Anthropic is rolling out this update starting with a small group of partners that includes Adobe, Atlassian and HubSpot.

#346. NYT Strands hints, answers for November 11, 2026

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.

#343. Google turns Gemini towards medical paperwork

Gemini for healthcare is in preview now, with some big-name partners already on board.

Google Cloud has officially announced Gemini for healthcare, bringing agentic AI to the sector.

In a press release, Google said that the new initiative aims to "unify the disparate clinical, operational, and administrative systems central to healthcare delivery and health plan operations." So rather than scraping the web to diagnose or advise treatments to medical professionals at the point of care, it sounds like Gemini will be used primarily to improve workflow efficiency.

The healthcare-focused assistant is built on top of the universal Gemini agent, and connects to third-party systems to gather information from health records, billing platforms and official guidelines. The AI can also access Medicare and Medicaid data to suggest next steps and generate multi-step workflows that Google says will be overseen by humans where appropriate, and adhere to "defined guardrails."

Gemini for healthcare is intended to relieve the strain on human administrative teams, reducing burnout and in turn allowing more time for patient care. It launches in preview first, with Blue Cross Blue Shield of Massachusetts, Hartford HealthCare, HCA Healthcare, Highmark Health, Northwell Health, Optum and Stanford School of Medicine among the launch customers.

"The fundamental challenge in healthcare isn't a lack of information—it's the immense friction of making that data actionable across a highly regulated ecosystem," said Thomas Kurian, CEO, Google Cloud. "By working directly alongside our healthcare customers, we've built a platform that resolves chronic bottlenecks so that the industry can put its focus entirely back on human health."

Gemini for healthcare connects workflows across Google Workspace and Microsoft 365, as well as ServiceNow and industry-specific platforms like the Salesforce's agentic Health Cloud. It will be available for Gemini Enterprise Standard and Gemini Enterprise Plus customers.

#342. NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 11, 2026

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you like to hit the green.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.
