#340. High oil prices have led to a dip in global carbon emissions

Bucking recent trends, fossil fuel emissions look to be down 0.4 percent.

Evaluating the annual global temperatures each year requires context. There is a fair amount of natural variability from one year to the next, so it’s important to focus on the trend over time. Famously, global warming did not end in 1998, as some claimed a couple of decades ago—we just had a run of La-Niña-dominated years after a very strong El Niño.

Annual greenhouse gas emissions are much less variable, having increased fairly steadily, but many complicating factors can still muddy the picture. Sometimes those factors conspire to throw a year off the trend. It looks like 2026 will be one of those years.

A new analysis led by Corinne Le Quéré at the University of East Anglia estimates that fossil fuel emissions have fallen about 0.4 percent from last year. This probably isn’t a peak that symbolizes heartening progress on climate change, though; the researchers think it’s a short-term dip driven by the war in Iran and its effect on oil prices.

This team works on the Global Carbon Project, which compiles stats on fossil fuel use and things like deforestation to track carbon emissions globally and by country. In this case, they looked at the stats and tried to pull out key underlying trends that show us what to expect over the next couple of years.

Last year, fossil fuel emissions increased 1.2 percent, a bit above the 2019–2025 average of 0.8 percent per year. This year’s decline featured a 1.9 percent drop in oil emissions, a smaller 0.3 percent drop in emissions from natural gas use, and a 0.6 percent increase in coal emissions.

Unlike the pandemic-driven drop in 2020, this wasn’t driven by a widespread decrease in energy demand. Demand did increase, but additions of electric vehicles and solar and wind generation covered almost 70 percent of the growth. Electric vehicles can account for only about 20 percent of that consequential decline in oil emissions, meaning the bulk is due to high prices.

One simple way to break down emissions is to connect it to economic output. Given a country’s gross domestic product (GDP), energy use per unit GDP, and carbon emissions per unit energy, you can calculate total carbon emissions. GDP might go up, but if that’s due to less energy-intensive industries or a cleaner energy system, carbon emissions might not rise with it.

Between 2019 and 2025, 59 countries cut their emissions despite economic growth, even as global emissions increased by 0.8 percent per year. The researchers estimated that, had the economic growth not been accompanied by the growth of electric vehicles and solar and wind generation, the increase would have been much higher, at 1.9 percent per year.

Given the International Monetary Fund’s projection for GDP through 2028, the researchers extrapolated these trends forward as well. From that, we would expect global fossil fuel emissions to continue increasing. That includes the present year—without the shock to oil prices, we would be seeing emissions increase in 2026 as well.

So while we could be close to peak emissions, we’re probably not there yet. The growth of electric vehicles and renewable energy is offsetting most of the growth of emissions, but not all of it. That would take just a bit more juice.

The researchers write, “If further acceleration of clean technology deployment can be supported through time, a sustained peak and decline in global CO2 emissions could be a few years away.”

Nature Climate Change, 2026. DOI: 10.1038/s41558-026-02821-8 (About DOIs).

#338. Polestar is banned in the US, but we still want its latest concept car

The Formula 2030 has inflatable seats, a pop-up LiDAR sensor and physical buttons.

Polestar's new concept EV understands the assignment: build hype for the brand by showcasing a futuristic car that gets auto enthusiasts drooling. But if you live in the US, you can't buy the Formula 2030. To be fair, nobody can buy it, since it's a concept. However, unless something changes, Americans won't be able to buy the future EVs it inspires, either.

Starting in 2027, the US Bureau of Industry and Security won't let Polestar sell new models. The decision stems from the Connected Vehicle Rule, which restricts the sale of cars with software or hardware from China or Russia. The only exception is if automakers get authorization, which Polestar hasn't received. Polestar is majority-owned by China's Geely; Volvo falls under the same parent company, but it received authorization.

That said, if you're the type to get excited about a car you know you'll never own, the Formula 2030 offers plenty of eye candy. The low, compact four-door sports car stands just 53 inches tall and measures 184 inches long. It has Polestar's Dual Blade headlights, a wraparound windshield and what Polestar describes as a "UFO-esque" rear.

Polestar skipped the full-width light bar common on EVs in favor of Single Blade rear lights, which the company says will appear on future production models. There's even a concealed LiDAR sensor that only appears when the car switches to autonomous mode.

On the inside, there's a 20-inch curved driver display mounted on the steering column, with customizable physical controls (real buttons!) below a 12.2-inch center screen. Four individual seats have inflatable bolsters that adjust to whoever's sitting in them, and the seat backs are made from a combination of European flax and bio-based polypropylene. The car uses Google Gemini for voice control.

Polestar says elements of the design will appear on the Polestar 2's successor (coming in 2027) and the Polestar 7 SUV (expected in 2028).

#337. Steam Deck performance gets a reality check with Valve's new FPS data

Benchmark tools for generic gaming rigs could be coming soon.

Since Valve announced its Steam Deck Verified program in 2021, players have found that a game’s “Verified” status doesn’t always match how well it actually runs on the handheld. Now, new Steam Deck frame rate data being shared by Valve itself can help players dig deeper into the performance they can expect from many Steam Deck games.

Valve has been collecting anonymized frame rate data from Steam players who opt in since February. Yesterday evening, the company announced it has started sharing that data on many Steam Store pages as averages and histograms for both the Steam Deck and Steam Deck OLED.

Valve acknowledges that players enabling VSync or frame rate limits can bias the collected data somewhat and that the measurements don’t account for custom in-game settings that Steam Deck users may tinker with to get a game running. Regardless, Valve says it has nonetheless “found that the framerate data consolidates around relatively narrow ranges.”

This kind of data can be extremely useful for distinguishing between the wide range of performance levels that can exist among “Verified” Steam Deck games. Both Cyberpunk 2077 and Skyrim: Special Edition earn the same Steam Deck Verified badge, for instance, but the former sees average Deck frame rates in the 30–40 FPS range, while the latter usually achieves 50–60 FPS on the hardware.

Valve’s frame rate data is even more useful for games that the company insists are “Unsupported” on Steam Deck because their “graphics setting cannot be configured to run well” on the hardware or because Valve is “still working on adding support for this game.” Despite warnings like these, games like Starfield, Silent Hill 2, and Assassin’s Creed Syndicate, to name just a few, all show extremely playable frame rates reported by actual Steam Deck users. Assassin’s Creed Syndicate in particular approaches a 60 FPS average on the Steam Deck OLED hardware despite its “Unsupported” status.

Frame rate data can also be useful for judging the single-player modes of games whose multiplayer doesn’t work under SteamOS. Watch Dogs 2, for example, is officially Unsupported on Steam Deck because of anti-cheat compatibility issues, but Steam Deck players report averages of 40+ FPS on the game’s single-player modes.

Of course, collecting and averaging frame rate data for the Steam Deck(s) is made simpler by their set hardware configuration. When it comes to communicating expected frame rates for the wide range of hardware in more generalized gaming PCs, Valve says it is “always looking for new ways to help developers understand the experiences customers are having with their games.”

Back in April, JSON files buried in the Steam client pointed to a potential Steam “Framerate Estimator” tool that could let users “Select an App and a PC config to get a chart of estimated framerates, based on the framerates of other Steam users,” according to strings contained within. That could be incredibly useful for Steam users who want to go beyond the listed Minimum and Recommended hardware requirements without relying on shady-looking third-party FPS-estimator websites. In the meantime, is sharing average frame rate data for the fixed-configuration Steam Machine too much to ask?

#336. NVIDIA DLSS 4.5 has presets you should know about

Like it or lump it, there's no getting away from upscalers if you're a PC gamer who loves modern AAA titles. For those on the NVIDIA side of the divide, the company's Deep Learning Super Sampling tech is increasingly needed if you want to run graphically demanding games at high frame rates with ray tracing whacked all the way up. If you regularly deploy DLSS 4.5 in games, there are tweaks you can make through the NVIDIA app to squeeze better performance and cleaner visuals from your favorite titles.

The internet remains very upset about NVIDIA's DLSS 5, and rightfully so. Thankfully, DLSS 4.5 is focused purely on delivering the best balance between image quality and performance, not needlessly yassifying in-game character models. We'll run you through DLSS 4.5's K, L, and M presets, how they compare to each other, and what hardware is best suited to each. We'll also touch on in-app tweaks you can make to reduce visual glitches that may occur if you enable frame generation.

There's no real "best" preset, per se. It's more a case of judging what the most suitable option is for striking the right balance between visual quality and performance, which ties into how powerful your GPU is. To switch between DLSS 4.5's various presets via the app, go to Graphics > Global Settings > DLSS Override – Model Presets.

First up, preset K has been designed to deliver the best image quality, so it should be the go-to for folks who own the highest-end gaming PCs (more on that shortly). If you're gaming on more modest hardware, presets M and L have been tailored towards DLSS 4.5 Performance and Ultra Performance modes respectively. If you're playing on, say, an RTX 4070, opting for preset M should deliver attractive results without hitting your frame rates too hard. Upscaling from an internal resolution of 1080p, this option produces results that can look really pleasing on a 4K screen thanks to strong image stability.

Gamers who want to play the best PC games on either RTX 30 or 20 Series cards would be better off selecting preset L, especially if the title in question has ray tracing. Sure, the image won't be quite as stable as M, meaning you're likely to encounter ghosting issues when the camera is moving around quickly in games. Still, preset L can deliver convincingly high resolution results on 1440p and 4K monitors. That's impressive, considering DLSS 4.5 is upscaling from an internal 720p resolution.

In short, if you own bleeding edge PC components, preset K will produce the most eye-arousing results. The performance-focused M is great for mid-range gaming PCs and does a good job upscaling from 1080p. For aging rigs, preset L delivers the most performance bang for your buck and looks pretty decent doing so.

Whether you own a 4K monitor or have your PC hooked up to your main living room TV like I do, playing AAA games on a beefy rig in Ultra HD is pretty glorious. Provided your GPU and CPU are up to the task, DLSS 4.5's preset K is best placed to deliver eye-rubbing 2160p results. Though preset M does a commendable job upscaling from 1080p, the higher baseline resolutions that K is working with produces image quality that's closer to native 4K output.

Once you choose K from the NVIDIA app's model presets menu, you can further tailor the visual experience you're hoping to obtain by hopping to "DLSS Override – Super Resolution Mode". Here, you're best choosing either DLAA, Quality or Balanced to hit a high level of image quality. However, since DLAA outputs at 100% resolution, you really need an expensive 50 Series GPU with this option if you want to avoid tanking frame rates in demanding modern games.

The Quality and Balanced options are obviously less taxing than rendering games at native output. The former upscales from 1440p (that's around 66.7% of a native 4K image), while the latter scales from a baseline 2227 x 1253 pixels (about 58% of 4K). You can also set a custom input resolution from this sub menu. Whenever possible, I go with DLAA, as it provides the best form of anti-aliasing to all but eliminate most in-game jaggies. That said, I do own an RTX 5090. For most folks with decent GPUs, pairing preset K with Balanced mode will provide strong performance with image quality that should look great on most 4K displays.

Team Green's Multi Frame Generation tech is capable of delivering hugely impressive FPS numbers... with a couple of caveats. Though you can run the likes of Cyberpunk 2077 at over 200 FPS with full path tracing when the x6 multiplier is enabled, latency can increase significantly, while visual glitches may also occur. To reduce the latter issue, it's best to tweak settings in the Frame Generation tab of the DLSS Override – Model Presets section.

Not to be confused with the previously mentioned K, L, and M presets, frame generation has its own unique options to help smooth out image quality. By default, the app chooses the "Use 3D app setting", while also placing an asterisk next to the Recommended preset with the disclaimer this option should provide the best balance between optimal quality and performance in most cases. You also have the choice of presets A and B. In most instances, it's the latter you should go with.

However you feel about NVIDIA's DLSS 4.5 Multi Frame Generation tech and its AI-assisted frames, it is capable of delivering game-changing performance in titles that push path-tracing hard, such as Resident Evil Requiem. However, the frame gen preset you choose can have a substantial impact on what you see onscreen. In my experience, choosing the older A preset can cause obvious visual bugs.

In the case of Requiem or 007 First Light, this most commonly manifests as a distracting shimmering effect around 2D elements, like onscreen icons. When I've encountered these ugly issues, swapping from preset A to the more recent B variant has generally cleaned up these problems completely, or at least made them less visible. Not all games that support MFG are compatible with preset B though, so if you're experiencing issues, it's occasionally worth testing out preset A.

#333. Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out the New York Times

Some VIP Twitter users woke up on Saturday expecting to have lost their coveted blue verification check marks in a previously announced purge by Elon Musk. Instead, Twitter appeared to target a single account from a major publication Musk dislikes and changed the language on its site in a way that obscures why users are verified.

Twitter had said it would “begin winding down” blue checks granted under its old verification system — which emphasized protecting high-profile users at risk of impersonation — on April 1. In order to stay verified, Musk said, users would have to pay $8 per month to join the platform’s Twitter Blue subscription service, which has allowed accounts to pay for verification since December.

But one high-profile account did lose its blue check over the weekend: the main account for the New York Times, which had previously told CNN it would not pay for verification.

William Shatner is mad he may have to pay to keep his blue Twitter checkmark. Elon Musk says too bad

After an account that often engages with Musk posted a meme this weekend about the Times declining to pay for verification, Musk responded in a tweet saying, “Oh ok, we’ll take it off then.” Musk then lashed out at the Times — just the latest instance of the billionaire slamming journalists or media outlets — in a series of tweets that claimed the outlet’s coverage is boring and “propaganda.”

The weekend moves are just the latest example of Twitter creating confusion and whiplash for users over feature changes — and in this case, not just any users, but many of the most high-profile accounts that have long been a key selling point for the platform. It also highlights how Musk often appears to guide decisions about the platform more by whims than by policy.

Although the New York Times’ main account lost its blue check, its other accounts, such as those for its arts, travel and books content, remained verified. (It’s not clear why the New York Times doesn’t have a gold “organizations” check mark, like accounts for other news outlets, including the Associated Press and the Washington Post.) After its blue check was removed, a spokesperson for the New York Times reiterated to CNN that it does not plan to pay for verification.

Twitter, which laid off most of its public relations staff last fall, did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a separate puzzling move, Twitter’s blue bird logo at the top of the site was replaced on Monday with doge, the meme representing the cryptocurrency dogecoin, which Musk has promoted. The price of dogecoin shot up 20% on Monday.

Musk has been threatening to take away “legacy” blue check marks from users verified under Twitter’s old system since shortly after he bought Twitter last fall.

In early November, Twitter launched the option for people paying for its Twitter Blue subscription service to receive blue checks. The program was quickly put on pause after being plagued by a wave of celebrity and corporate impersonators, and was relaunched in December.

Twitter also rolled out a color-coded verification system with differently colored marks for companies and government entities, but Musk continued to say that individual users would eventually have to pay for blue checks.

In the days leading up to the blue check purge that wasn’t, prominent users such as actor William Shatner and anti-bullying activist Monica Lewinksy pushed back against the idea that, as power users that draw attention to the site, they should have to pay for a feature that keeps them safe from impersonation.

By muddying the reason accounts are verified, the new label could risk making it easier for people to scam or impersonate high-profile users. Experts in inauthentic behavior have also said it’s not clear that reserving verification for paid users will reduce the number of bots on the site, an issue Musk has raised on and off over the past year.

Musk, for his part, has previously presented changes to Twitter’s verification system as a way of “treating everyone equally.”

“There shouldn’t be a different standard for celebrities,” he said in a tweet last week. The paid feature could also drive revenue, which could help Musk, who is on the hook for significant debt after buying Twitter for $44 billion.

Musk last week also said that starting on April 15, only verified accounts would be recommended in users’ “For You” feeds alongside the accounts they follow.

–CNN’s Oliver Darcy contributed to this report.

#332. Here's what to look for so you don't get scammed by a fake GPU

When it comes to the arms-race of GPU scams, an ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure.

RAM prices are completely out of control. The AI rush has made even old GPUs worth more than ever before, so it's no surprise that affordable GPUs have become a hot commodity and fertile ground for scams. As more and more false listings hit both the online world and the physical streets, a keen eye may be all that stands between you and a con.

The overwhelming demand for GPUs in the AI sector, both from major companies and individual hobbyists, has led to crazy price spikes. This has, in turn, fueled a deluge of scams. Buyers have reported receiving bags of pasta and rice instead of a GPU or even getting just a picture of the GPU they ordered instead of a physical product. Physical repair shops have also been victimized, with some traded-in GPUs being nothing more than empty shells with their memory dies removed (and sold to other consumers).

The first thing you need to avoid scams like this is a sense of caution. While it's not impossible to find an affordable GPU in 2026, any deals on Facebook Marketplace or eBay that are too good to be true, probably are. Not only should you avoid taking a chance on what feels like an unreal price, but you should also avoid placing your trust in stock photos when buying from these sites. Even with an in-hand photo, try searching for the picture on Google Images to check if it's pulled from elsewhere. In addition, ask the seller to provide additional photos from niche angles, and don't let the seller avoid questions or rush you into buying the product. These are telltale red flags.

Buying in-person (whether through an arranged meeting or a physical vendor) is perhaps the best way to avoid getting conned, as this will allow you to test the GPU. Arrange with the seller beforehand to meet somewhere you can set up a test rig, such as a library or a local tech shop. After inspecting the GPU for damage, stress test the GPU using an application like HWiNFO. Simply open the program in full mode, click on Sensors, and click on Stress Test (which looks like a red flame in a blue square). During the test, you'll be able to use the info in the window to view peak temperatures, clock speeds and check other readings. If everything's in order after about 15 minutes, you should be good to go.

You may have noticed that many of these pitfalls relate to online purchases, such as scan listings and the inability to test the actual product in advance. Though in-person dealings aren't always perfect, and you should always be careful when meeting a stranger for any arranged purchase, they at least give you an opportunity to get a hands-on look at what you're buying. The best you can do in a purely online transaction is ask thorough questions of the seller, request videos of stress tests from the seller and check the seller's reviews and buyer feedback.

If you are buying a GPU in 2026 from a stranger online, you should at least be doing so through a site with buyer protection, such as eBay. However, you shouldn't throw caution to the wind just because of this safety layer. Scammers have developed ways to make getting your money back a major hassle. In the case of GPUs, some con artists have devised ways to snag tracking IDs going to your area and to give those IDs to the platform they used to sell to you, making it harder for you to prove that you didn't receive what you ordered. You should be able to eventually get a refund if you're able to convince your courier service to provide proof of a scam, but it's still better to avoid the situation in the first place.

#331. Free video-streaming service Tubi lets users share curated watchlists now

User-based recommendations could make it easier to find stuff to watch on Tubi.

Tubi users can now create and share playlists of movies and shows available on the streaming service.

Tubi is a free ad-supported streaming television (FAST) service that has both live channels and on-demand content.

Today, the Fox-owned company announced “Mixtapes” as a way for users to make watchlists from Tubi’s “nearly 400,000 movies and TV episodes,” Tubi’s announcement said, adding:

They can give each mixtape a title and description and arrange the entertainment inside in whatever order feels right to them. Viewers can build one entirely on their own, or describe what they’re looking for and let Tubi offer a starting place that they can edit, rearrange, or rebuild entirely. When their mixtape is finished, they can publish and share it with other people or keep it for themselves.

Tubi’s Mixtapes website already features numerous playlists, including: “Millennial Memory Lane”—with movies like Cruel Intentions (1999) and The Fifth Element (1997) and shows such as Everybody Hates Chris (2005–2009) and Degrassi: The Next Generation (2001–2015); and “Bald Baddies”—which includes Fast & Furious Presents: Hobbs & Shaw (2019) and Central Intelligence (2016).

Watching a Mixtape doesn’t require a Tubi account, but making one does.

Tubi’s Mixtapes comes at a time when streaming service providers are seeking a way to address the content fragmentation that’s frequently cited as a frustration among streaming users, as well as users struggling to find something to watch. Gracenote’s “2025 State of Play” report, for instance, surveyed 3,000 consumers in the US, Brazil, France, Germany, Mexico, and the UK and found that “nearly 33 percent of streaming users feel content and service fragmentation negatively impacts their TV experience.”

Users are also likely to stop using a streaming service if they struggle to find something they want to watch, per the survey. Forty-nine percent of users said they “are willing to cancel a service based on difficulty finding what to watch.” Additionally, 19 percent of respondents said they would “abandon a viewing session if their content search is not successful.”

Although Tubi users don’t have paid subscriptions to cancel, making it easier to find relevant content could help Tubi grow its user base and increase the time people spend watching, which supports the advertising business Tubi relies on.

Sharable watchlists are rare among streaming services. Mubi has a similar feature where users can make lists of movies and shows that people can follow, but the lists can feature content that’s not available on Mubi.

Plex and JustWatch also let people create and share curated watchlists with other users.

Still, it’s nice to see a streaming service, especially a free one, add new capabilities at a time when many other streaming services are consistently increasing subscription prices. And while some streaming service companies are turning to AI to help with content recommendations, a human connection may appeal more to certain users, while keeping costs lower for Tubi.

VisionQuest brings James Spader's evil Ultron back to the MCU, and at first, I wasn't complaining. Spader's gleefully villainous performance is one of the sole bright spots of Age of Ultron. It holds up just as well in VisionQuest, with Spader chewing the hell out of every withering one-liner.

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

Episode 7 revealed that the show's main villain, the Client, was also played by James Spader, meaning Ultron's human avatar is modeled after him. Turns out, the Client is Virgil Horton, grandson of Phineas Horton (Bettany), creator of the world's first synthetic man.

Virgil was once the partner of Josephine Carrington (T'Nia Miller), whose consciousness was used to create the synthezoid Jocasta (Miller). As the two worked on their revolutionary AI tech, they coded themselves into their work, explaining why Ultron's human avatar looks like Virgil. Now that Virgil has apprehended both Vision and Jocasta, he plans to put his mind in Vision's body so he and Jocasta can live together forever, using their nanobot tech to rule over all of humanity.

Neither Vision nor Jocasta wants this future, so they team up with Tommy Maximoff (Ruaridh Mollica) to defeat Virgil. In the chaos, Virgil shoots Jocasta, who subsequently throws Ultron's drive into the nanobot swarm, unleashing him in his physical form. His first move? Killing Virgil in a feat of Spader-on-Spader violence.

In one fell swoop, Ultron dispatches VisionQuest's Big Bad, leaving a villainous void ready to be filled. That's a role Ultron is all too happy to step into, and so begins VisionQuest's endgame: Ultron's nanobot swarm flying towards London, ready to implant itself in its citizens and kill them from the inside out.

For a series that tried to twist and bend the rules of what a Marvel show could be, this final showdown is frustratingly familiar, right down to Vision and Ultron duking it out in their synthetic forms. Yes, Ultron can dissipate into a cloud of nanobots now, but that doesn't change the fact that this feels too much a rehash of an Avengers film that came out 11 years ago.

Under this sheen of familiarity, even the best parts of VisionQuest's climax feel like a drag. In a callback to WandaVision, Vision tries to use the power of sitcoms to help Ultron process his grief over losing Jocasta. Ultron hijacks the program, resulting in a 500-episode montage that comes with its fair share of clever sight gags, along with a catchy theme song. ("Don't kill the world!")

Yet all the montage really does is remind us of how perfectly WandaVision was able to parody sitcoms. More than that, it reminds us of the power of good episodic television, something every Marvel show has tried to capture, before almost always falling short and feeling like a truncated eight-hour movie instead. With its Age of Ultron-meets-therapy climax, VisionQuest is no exception, even if it has its fair share of highs. (Tommy's awkward running around the table in the finale will live in my head for a while.)

VisionQuest is the last new MCU show on Marvel's docket until 2028's Zodiac, and aside from Loki and Daredevil: Born Again, live-action MCU series don't have the best track record of renewal. Still, that doesn't stop VisionQuest from signalling to a future for its characters. Tommy connects with his long-lost twin Billy (Joe Locke) and heads back to New Jersey with him by the season's end. Could the pair be part of a young Avengers team down the line, or will the MCU leave them by the wayside, along with other potential young Avengers like Kamala Khan (Iman Vellani) and Kate Bishop (Hailee Steinfeld)?

Tommy also asks Vision if there's any way they could potentially bring the late Wanda (Elizabeth Olsen) back, reuniting the entire WandaVision family. Meanwhile, Ultron is still out there, along with Jocasta's hard drive. Both sequences suggest that neither women are truly gone from the MCU — if only VisionQuest gets the opportunity to explore further. In the meantime, though, its final impression is a mixed bag: a combination of some truly inventive world building, marred by the insistence on replaying a decade-old story.

#328. Google's impressive Rambler dictation tech is now available on Pixel 10 devices too

November’s Pixel Drop also brings other AI features to older Pixel phones, while the Pixel 11’s HiLight feature now works with Google Messages.

Google is allowing its Gemini-powered dictation tech to trickle down to the Pixel 10 series, meaning those who haven't yet upgraded to the Pixel 11 series get one of its standout features.

Rambler is a text input feature that takes your natural language ramblings and turns them into coherent, grammatically correct text. It also strips out the "umms" and "ahhs" from your dictation, as well as unnecessary words, and if you self-correct while speaking, Gemini will smoothen it all out in the final passage.

Despite by his own admission not being much of a voice texter, Engadget's Nathan Ingraham praised the keyboard-native feature in his Pixel 11 review, so it's good to see it coming to more devices. And it isn't the only one. Gemini Intelligence's rollout to the Pixel 10 family also includes personalized suggestions and new multi-step automation tools for tasks like ordering groceries or booking a taxi.

Beyond the Pixel 10 goodies, Google is rolling out new AI functionality for a number of its device families. The Create My Widget tool, which lets you use natural language prompts to build custom widgets, will now be available on phones too, with Pixel Watch support coming soon. New AI-powered icon styles also arrive this month, while the HiLight camera module notification system, which previously only worked for incoming calls, now supports Google Messages too. If you have a Pixel 11 Pro, the module will glow when you receive a message from an important contact.

Google has also announced that step-by-step workout routines are now available on Pixel Watch 3 and newer, as well as phones. These track your live metrics on your watch or phone's display, and can log your reps as you go.

And finally, Google's long-overdue AirTag-alike Pixel Tag is now available to buy. Announced back in August, this pill-shaped tracker can be paired with devices running Android 9 or later, though to use its full suite of features you'll need a device that supports Bluetooth 6.0 and is running Android 16. The Pixel Tag costs $29 or $99 for a four-pack.

#327. VisionQuests mid-credit scene, explained

Like many an MCU movie before it, VisionQuest's finale continues Marvel's proud tradition of a mid-credits scene teasing the franchise's future.

VisionQuest's mid-credits scene does not gesture toward the next confirmed Marvel release, the highly anticipated Avengers: Doomsday. Instead, it offers a look at what might be next for one of the leads of VisionQuest, should the series get a Season 2.

Want more film and TV news and reviews delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for Mashable’s Top Stories newsletter or get Mashable push alerts today.

VisionQuest returns to the site of the show's final battle: Horton Manor. There, Phineas Horton (Paul Bettany) created the world's first synthetic man, all those decades ago.

In the final battle, Horton's grandson Virgil (James Spader), aka VisionQuest's sinister Client, perished at the hands of Ultron (Spader). Before he died, Virgil shot synthezoid Jocasta (T'Nia Miller), who was based on the consciousness of his former partner Josephine Carrington (also Miller). But Jocasta may not be gone for good.

The mid-credits scene shows Ultron residing at Horton Manor, now in his human form thanks to Josephine's nanobot technology. (He's trimmed the beard, though.) Since his human form mirrors Virgil's, has Ultron assumed Virgil's identity to live among humans? Or is he remaining fully himself?

One thing's for sure: Ultron is still holding onto Jocasta. Literally: He's holding the drive that contains her code.

As he holds it, he takes a contemplative beat. The music swells menacingly. Then he walks off screen. To do what? VisionQuest certainly doesn't tell.

Perhaps he's getting ready to rebuild and reboot Jocasta, so the pair can finish their dream of ruling the world. Whatever his goal, the foreboding music suggests that it may have been a mistake to let Ultron hang around.

So far, Disney has not renewed VisionQuest for a second season.

Aside from Loki and Daredevil: Born Again, it's rare that Disney renews the MCU's live-action series. Based on that precedent, the chances for more VisionQuest are very slim.

However, that doesn't mean we won't see these characters again. WandaVision spawned two "sequel" series in Agatha All Along and VisionQuest, so the story of Ultron or the second-generation Maximoff twins (Ruaridh Mollica and Joe Locke) could continue in a new series.

Currently, the only live-action MCU series in development is 2028's Zodiac. So, it's unlikely we get any further Ultron updates before then.

#326. How much did the ASUS ROG Zephyrus G14 cost in 2020 vs 2026?

It's only natural to wince when you think back to the spring of 2020. But among all the chaos and uncertainty of the time, you could've found solace in bagging an ASUS ROG Zephyrus G14 for the low low price of $1,050. That might look like a typo when considering today's "cheapest" model costs $3,600 when you buy directly from ASUS, but it's true. Even at Best Buy, you're talking $3,200 at the time of writing, with both prices representing more than a three-fold increase over the launch price.

That number looks all the more grim when you consider that inflation only accounts for about $320 of the increase. ASUS upgraded the G14's screen, chassis, and graphics between 2020 and 2025, adding about $750 to the sticker price. It then jumped by another $1,400 in a single generation as memory prices went to the moon and cheaper configurations were dropped from the U.S. lineup. Almost two-thirds of the price increase came this year alone.

In 2020, the base G14 paired a Ryzen 7 4800HS with 8GB of RAM, a GTX 1650 and a 60Hz 1080p display. Meanwhile, the $1,450 review configuration (which we, at the time, called ultraportable gaming done right) had a Ryzen 9 4900HS, an RTX 2060 Max-Q, 16GB of RAM and a 120Hz screen. It weighed roughly 3.5 pounds and came in at 0.7 inches thick. The Zephyrus line had only been around for three years at that point, starting in 2017 with the 15-inch GX501. That was a $2,699 machine, and the G14 brought it down to less than half that price.

In 2022, ASUS rebuilt the G14 around AMD Radeon graphics, inflating the starting price to around $1,650 for the RX 6700S model. A year later, NVIDIA GPUs returned, and an RTX 4060 config retailed at around $1,600. The top model with an RTX 4090 and a mini-LED screen had more than double the price tag at $3,299. Another redesign in 2024 brought a 3K 120Hz OLED panel and cut the weight to 3.31 pounds, with the price starting again at $1,600 with a Ryzen 9 8945HS, 16GB of RAM, and an RTX 4060.

A year later, the 2025 model launched at $2,499 with an RTX 5070 Ti and twice the usual RAM at 32GB, topping out at $3,200 with an RTX 5080. ASUS did quote a $2,199 starting price at January's CES 2025 event, but no configuration at that price went on sale at launch. A cheaper RTX 5060 version was eventually announced in May 2025 for $1,800. That left the cheapest G14 costing $750 more than the OG, though it came with an OLED screen and 16GB of RAM.

This year's G14 pairs Intel's Core Ultra 9 386H with an RTX 5070 Ti, with the $3,200 config sporting 16GB of RAM, a 1TB SSD, and a 2880 x 1800 OLED panel. The version with 32GB RAM is $3,600 directly from ASUS. The RAM here is soldered, so you're stuck with 16GB in the cheaper model, and its thickness and weight are almost exactly the same as the original. Interestingly, in its benchmarks, GamesRadar found that the 2026 RTX 5070 Ti model sometimes outran last year's RTX 5080 version.

This dramatic price increase largely comes down to the AI-fueled memory crisis. In January 2025, ASUS told its partners that it would raise prices on some products, citing memory costs driven by AI demand. Unfortunately, a thin gaming laptop like the G14 with 16GB or 32GB of fast soldered memory and a 1TB SSD is exposed to both DRAM and flash pricing. While it's true that ASUS does make a cheaper 2026 G14 with an AMD Ryzen AI 9 465 and an RTX 5060, it isn't currently available from the U.S. store. That leaves the RTX 5070 Ti model as the entry point for U.S. buyers, and at $3,200, it now costs what the top-spec RTX 5080 model did at launch. The same chip and GPU in the dual-screen ROG Zephyrus Duo, which we called outrageously expensive yet totally awesome, will set you back $4,500.

When we account for inflation, the 2020 G14's $1,050 is about $1,370 today, and no model in the G14 lineup comes close to that price. The nearest thing is last year's RTX 5060 model, which tops our list of the best gaming laptops for 2026. It's still available at Best Buy, and if you want the cheapest G14 going, that's the one to buy while you still can.

#325. Pixel phones getting new icon styles, Create My Widget, and more in November Pixel Drop

Spruce up your home screen with expanded custom icons and AI-generated widgets.

Google’s Pixel phones get an update every month, but most are just security patches and bug fixes. The bigger changes are bundled into quarterly Pixel Drops, and one is rolling out today. The update brings a few new things, and many of the Gemini features previously exclusive to the latest Pixel phones will also expand to Google’s Pixel 10 lineup.

The Pixel 11 was Google’s first foray into “Gemini Intelligence” branding, something we’ll see a lot more of going forward. Some of the features under that heading were limited to Google’s newest phones (and in some cases, Samsung’s phones). The November Pixel Drop will expand these AI features to the Pixel 10 lineup, but importantly, that doesn’t include the budget-oriented Pixel 10a.

For starters, the Pixel 10 series will get the improved personalized suggestions (previously known as Magic Cue) that debuted on the Pixel 11. This enables things like sending grocery lists to Google Keep from other apps and generating summaries from the context menu. Rambler, Google’s AI-assisted voice input system, is also coming to the 2025 phones. Likewise, Gemini app automation will roll out on Pixel 10 with this update. This feature is still limited to select apps in rideshare, food delivery, travel, and a few other categories.

The AI-powered icon theming that Google added to Pixels back in March is also getting an update. Initially, this feature just matched icon colors to the system theme when developers didn’t include the necessary theming assets. With this update, Google says it has moved the feature to a more powerful Nano Banana model, unlocking 10 new icon styles for your home screen.

The update also brings Create My Widget to Pixel phones. Google announced this earlier in 2026, and it shipped on Googlebooks a few weeks ago. The idea is that you describe a home screen widget you want, and Android builds it for you on the spot. However, the use cases are pretty narrow. You’re limited to data accessible in search (e.g., stock prices, sports scores, and weather) and select Google account data points like Gmail or your calendar.

So that’s a lot of AI. Google does have some non-AI additions with this update, but don’t get your hopes up. One of the headlining additions is (drumroll) HiLight support for Google Messages. Yes, Pixel 11 phones will be able to illuminate HiLight when you get a message from important contacts. Ignoring that we had more functional notification LEDs 20 years ago, this is a good and necessary improvement for Google’s least useful hardware feature.

If you have a Pixel Watch, Google says this update will also improve your workout routine. The Pixel Watch 3 and newer will be able to display step-by-step guidance for your workouts. The wearable can also log reps and weights as you go without interrupting live metrics. While Create My Widget is not yet available on the watch, Google promises it’s also coming soon.

The update is beginning its rollout today and should be available widely in the coming days.

#324. This boiled bag of offal is banned in the US. In Scotland it's a fine-dining treat

Anthony Bourdain loved haggis. But even the late, great American chef, writer and television host recognized that Scotland’s national dish, with its “sinister sheep parts” wrapped in a shroud of mystery and half-invented history, could be a hard sell.

“Don’t let them tell you otherwise, that’s really one of life’s great pleasures,” Bourdain said on one of his gastro-curious pilgrimages to Glasgow. “There is no more unfairly reviled food on Earth than the haggis.”

A mash-up of diced lung, liver and heart mixed with oatmeal, beef suet, onion and assorted spices, haggis was traditionally made by stuffing these raw ingredients into the stomach of a recently slain sheep and boiling the lot to a state of palatability.

Instagrammable is not the word that immediately comes to mind. In our 21st-century world, where “clean” eating and processed pap overlap, haggis can seem like an “Outlander”-style outlier from another age.

Yet, by some alchemy, once cooked to its required “warm-reekin’ (steaming)” state, it adds up to much more than the sum of its modest parts. It’s offaly charm has kept nose-to-tail eating alive among a younger generation of Scots that has largely turned its back on the tripe, liver and kidneys their predecessors enjoyed (or endured).

Carefully prepared, haggis tastes both oaty and meaty; it is dark and crumbly, a little crispy at the edges but still moist; earthy but also savory and spicy; deep-tasting and profoundly warming, the perfect foil for its traditional garnish of floury mashed potatoes and orange bashed turnip.

“It’s like a cuddle for the stomach,” says Nicola Turner, a 35-year-old office administrator from Helensburgh, a town on western Scotland’s Firth of Clyde.

For children of the 1960s and ’70s, like crime novelist Ian Rankin, haggis meals were a choice between the classic meat-and-two-veg plate and the battered and deep-fried, chip-shop iteration loved by both his friend Bourdain and his quintessentially Scottish detective character, Inspector John Rebus.

Now myriad other treatments have blossomed.

“I’m pretty sure the first time I dined with AB in Edinburgh we had haggis in filo pastry with a jam-style – maybe blackcurrant – sauce,” Rankin recalled. “He was a big fan of haggis and of chip shops. Rebus will have enjoyed the occasional haggis supper from his local chip shop. He was definitely a fan, as am I.”

“It is all about the spicing and the texture,” says the Scottish food writer, novelist and cook Sue Lawrence, a champion of haggis’ adaptability for use in other dishes. “If you didn’t know what was in it, you wouldn’t think ‘oh that tastes of liver or whatever.’ It is all nicely chopped up and the oatmeal gives it a lovely texture. It could easily be a nice, big mince dish.”

These US restaurants and chefs are James Beard Award finalists

Lawrence uses haggis as an alternative to beef and pork ragù in lasagna and in her pastilla, a version of the North African dish in which a hand-made haggis from the Isle of Mull substitutes for the traditional poultry or seafood filling. The filo pastry savory is flavored with the spice blend ras el hanout, apricots, chile, orange zest and almonds before being sprinkled with cinnamon and icing sugar.

Such cultural crossovers serve as a reminder that haggis could easily be a dish with nothing specifically Scottish about it at all. Records of similar quick and portable preparations of the fast-perishing innards of sheep and other animals date back to ancient Rome and Greece.

Haggis-like combinations of offal and grains are part of the culinary history of several countries. Spain has chireta, Romania drob and Sweden polsa, while chaudin, or ponce, is a rice and meat-stuffed pig stomach that is a staple of Cajun cooking.

In neighboring England, recipes for “hagese,” “hagws of a schepe,” “haggas” or “haggus” pop up in recipe books published between the 15th and 17th centuries, probably preceding written records north of the border.

Etymological evidence points to the term “haggis” having its roots in Old Norse, suggesting an early version of an oat-and-offal sausage might have arrived in Britain and Ireland on a Viking longboat.

But ever since it was first optioned by the poet Robert Burns in the late 1700s, the haggis backstory has been monopolized by Scotland and the Scots, sometimes mischievously.

It is, according to the kind of lore that Burns engendered, the dish a doughty Highlander would carry with him as he drove cattle through the glens to the markets of the central belt or the perfect picnic for a whisky smuggler plying his illicit trade by moonlight.

From such romantic notions it was a short step to turning the haggis into a wee wild beastie, one with longer legs on one side that was thus condemned to run round and round whichever hill it lived on. In 2003, a poll of American tourists in Scotland found that one in three of them believed they might encounter such a confused creature on a Caledonian vacation.

Bourdain, a native New Yorker, may have qualified as haggis’ biggest admirer since Burns, but his compatriots at the US Department of Agriculture remain unconverted to offal-filled paunch. Haggis imports into the United States were prohibited in 1971 as part of a ban on the consumption of all livestock lungs. Authentic versions of old school haggis remain culinary contraband in the US, as hard to lay your hands on as Cuban cigars.

Across the rest of the world, it’s a different story. According to leading producer Simon Howie, haggis is more widely appreciated and consumed now than it has been since Burns improvised his “Address to a Haggis” for the entertainment of well-to-do Edinburgh acquaintances.

Firmly tongue-in-cheek, the poem lauds the “Great chieftain o’ the pudding race,” as exactly the kind of unpretentious, hearty fare required to nourish a nation of braveheart warriors.

In comparison to the enfeebling foreign muck enjoyed by the capital’s claret-quaffing elites of the time – the olio, fricassée or ragoût that would “sicken a sow” – Burns urges his readers to wonder at the magical impact of haggis on his fellow sons of Scotland’s soil.

As the English translation of the original Scots language version puts it:

These days synthetic casings have largely replaced stomach but ovine and porcine innards remain at the core of most of the haggis produced in its homeland, said Howie, who estimates that his company Simon Howie Butchers, accounts for around 60% of the roughly two million haggises produced every year.

For Howie, versatility, value for money and convenience explain why this staple of the Scottish larder is thriving. Typically haggis retails in Scotland, which accounts for half of global consumption by volume, for around £6, or $7.70 per kilogram ($3.36/pound). That’s around half the price of less expensive cuts of beef or a third of the price of Scotch lamb while enjoying a fairly similar nutritional and calorific profile.

“You can give your kids a meal that is not full of things you don’t want to feed them – for a few pounds you can feed three strapping lads,” Howie said.

“From a kitchen perspective, it is very simple because when it leaves our factory it is already cooked. So when you or a restaurant owner gets it into the kitchen all you have to do is heat it up to be piping hot. It couldn’t be more basic: a student with no cooking skills or a Michelin-starred chef do exactly the same thing to put it out on the plate.”

Its texture means haggis can also be usefully deployed in fine dining alongside leaner meat like venison or as a stuffing for poultry and game birds. Its spicy intensity means it is also finding uses in canapés and as a crouton-borne garnish for soups.

Buoyant sales are also underpinned by the increasing consumption of haggis in forms inspired by Scotland’s ethnic minorities.

Glasgow’s Sikh community pioneered haggis pakora in the 1990s and samosas, spring rolls and quesadillas have followed in its wake, often using a vegetarian version of the protein in which the offal is replaced by a mix of vegetables, pulses and mushrooms.

Such dishes are more than culinary twists. They are badges of belonging, and an indication that, two centuries after Burns grabbed it for the nation, haggis is as intimately entwined with Scots identity as ever.

Just ask Ross O’Cinneide, a promising 14-year-old fly-half in the junior section of Stirling County rugby club.

“Most of my friends and I like haggis,” he says. “Mum makes it for us sometimes after rugby and it’s got a very nice warming feeling. And it’s nice because it’s purely Scottish.”

#322. New York will independently test AI models

The state’s research center will check AI companies’ work instead of taking their word.

As the federal government essentially leaves us all at the mercy of the AI industry, New York is taking matters into its own hands. On Tuesday, Governor Kathy Hochul launched the nation's first state-run AI testing program. Instead of trusting AI companies to police themselves, New York's system will task independent researchers with checking their work. "The stakes are too high to simply take AI companies at their word," Hochul stated in a press release.

The work will be handled by the New York Center for AI Responsibility and Research at Binghamton University. More than 80 New York scientists will contribute. They'll use computing power from Empire AI, the state's $500 million public-private AI partnership.

Hochul's office said the center will test advanced AI systems, study their risks and provide evidence to inform public officials' decisions. It will also look at how AI can be used responsibly in areas like medicine and scientific discovery.

One potential hole in the program is that it can only study what AI companies disclose publicly. New York's RAISE Act, signed by Hochul last year, requires large AI developers to register with the state by January 1, 2027. Starting then, companies will have to publish safety frameworks and transparency reports and report critical safety incidents within 72 hours. The RAISE Act won't plug the hole on unreleased models, but it will at least force companies to disclose more.

#320. Google Fi adds support for a second 5G network, won't say which one

Fi has always supported T-Mobile, but the new one? Google won’t say.

Google has announced a “super network” upgrade for its Fi cell service. For the past few years, Google Fi has operated exclusively on the T-Mobile network, but now it’s adding support for a second 5G network. Oddly, Google refuses to say which one.

Google’s MVNO (mobile virtual network operator) launched in 2015 as Project Fi with support for both T-Mobile and Sprint service. The service initially only worked on Google’s Nexus phones, which would switch between networks based on signal strength. After T-Mobile absorbed Sprint, Fi switched to being a single-network service in the US while continuing to hop between networks for international travel.

With the super network, Google Fi is again a dual-carrier service stateside. Phones connected to Fi will be able to hop between T-Mobile 5G and a different 5G network to provide the best possible connectivity. Google’s blog post announcing the change conspicuously doesn’t mention either network, saying only that it supports “two of America’s best 5G cellular networks.”

We know one of them is T-Mobile, and the other must be either AT&T or Verizon. When reached for comment, a Google spokesperson declined to name the second network, which is odd. Looking at the Fi coverage map, my best guess for the super network partner is Verizon. The shape of unserved regions is a closer match than AT&T, but carriers do tend to fudge the fine details of these maps.

MVNOs don’t always advertise which network they use, and that information is getting harder to find in FAQs and support pages. Google’s Fi FAQ no longer even confirms T-Mobile as a network partner. Google used to be very upfront about its partnership with T-Mobile, pointing out that, unlike most MVNOs, it gets top prioritization on T-Mobile’s towers. Network information like this is really something consumers should have when shopping for plans—you probably know which carriers work best where you spend your time.

There are a few caveats in the super network rollout beyond the mysterious new partner. Currently, network switching is only supported on the Pixel 9, Pixel 10, and Pixel 11 series. Other devices will get access in 2027. You also won’t be able to enjoy traditional dual-SIM functionality if you want Fi to switch networks. This will only work if you have Fi configured as an eSIM with all non-Fi SIMs disabled.

If you meet those requirements, super network support is rolling out now at no additional cost on Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans.

#319. The best-selling game console of all time is over 25 years old

Sony shipped over 160 million PlayStation 2 units over its 13-year run.

You might expect newer consoles to dominate lifetime sales lists. After all, the industry is several times bigger than it was two decades ago. But all-time console sales are a blend of old and new, with Sony's second home console, the PlayStation 2, hanging onto the top spot.

However, the PS2's lead is shrinking. Based on the latest figures, Nintendo's original Switch is only a few million units behind. And unlike the PS2, it's still being sold.

A note on the figures: Sony and Nintendo report their totals as consoles shipped to retailers, which is a little different from consumer sales. Also, Microsoft doesn't publish official totals, so those numbers are analyst estimates.

Sony says the PS2 sold over 160 million units, more than any other console. How did it take the crown? For starters, it launched with a built-in DVD player, right when that market was taking off. As wild as that sounds today, this was back when physical media ruled. Come for Austin Powers, stay for Kratos.

Timing also worked in the PS2's favor. When it launched, Sega's Dreamcast was the only other new home console on the market. By the time the Dreamcast hit the US in 1999, Sony was already hyping the PS2, leading many a gamer to hold off on Sega's new console. (The fact that Sega bailed quickly on the Sega CD, 32X and Saturn didn't help with consumer confidence.) And the GameCube and original Xbox didn't launch until late 2001, after the PS2 had already gained a foothold.

To top it off, the PS2 stayed on the market for ages. Sony didn't stop making it until 2013, the same year its "grandchild," the PS4, launched. The PS2 also benefited from price cuts, dropping from $299 at its US launch to $199 in 2002, and eventually to $99 in 2009. By then, it was an attractive option for people who wanted a still-capable gaming system on the cheap.

All those hardware sales led to staggering software numbers: more than 1.5 billion PS2 games sold. Its best-selling titles were Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas at 17.3 million units, Gran Turismo 3: A-Spec at 14.9 million, and Grand Theft Auto: Vice City at 14.2 million.

Kicking off the era of the hybrid console, the Switch launched in 2017 with a novel concept: a mobile gaming device that you could also connect to a TV. It didn't take long to see Nintendo had a winner on its hands after the Wii U's disappointment. Helping out was the fact that its flagship launch game was an open-world classic, with The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild making the Switch's proposition instantly clear.

The result? Nintendo has shipped over 156 million Switch consoles as of June 2026, including all variants. The original model was followed by the cheaper and portable-only Switch Lite and the higher-end Switch OLED, which added a bigger and better screen.

The Switch has already beaten the PS2 in game sales, at nearly 1.6 billion units. Its top-selling titles are Mario Kart 8 Deluxe at 71.5 million units, Animal Crossing: New Horizons at 50.3 million and Super Smash Bros. Ultimate at 38.1 million.

Nintendo has said it expects to ship around 2 million original Switch consoles in the current fiscal year, which ends in March 2027. If that rate holds, the Switch could pass the PS2 in 2028 at the earliest.

The best-selling fully portable console was the Nintendo DS, which arrived in 2004. The system had a series of features that were novel at the time: two screens, a stylus, a built-in microphone and wireless play. Nintendo ended up shipping over 154 million units across all DS models.

The first versions were backward compatible with the Game Boy Advance's library, which likely helped with early sales. Later hardware iterations included the slimmer and lighter DS Lite, the DSi (which added two cameras, an SD card slot and a digital shop) and the larger-screened DSi XL.

The DS, which sold about 949 million games, was known as much for quirky titles — like Nintendogs and Brain Age which took advantage of the unique hardware — as it was for its tentpole franchises. The platform's best-selling games were New Super Mario Bros. at 30.8 million units, Nintendogs at 24 million and Mario Kart DS at 23.6 million.

The original Game Boy was an exercise in restraint. Rivals like the Atari Lynx and Sega Game Gear had color screens, but they cost more and ate up batteries at a faster clip. The Game Boy had a greenish screen, was relatively cheap and durable and ran for hours on four AA batteries. In North America and Europe, it also came with the ultimate pick-up-and-play game, Tetris.

Like most Nintendo consoles, the Game Boy had several iterations. The Game Boy Pocket arrived in 1996, with a smaller, lighter build and a sharper black-and-white screen. The Japan-only Game Boy Light added a backlight to the Pocket.

But the most notable hardware update was the Game Boy Color, which Nintendo lumps together with the original model in lifetime totals. It arrived nearly a decade after the original in 1998, soon after Pokémon took off, adding a full-color screen and compatibility with most older Game Boy games. Together, the Game Boy family sold over 118 million consoles.

The Game Boy family's top-selling games illustrate the console's pocketable appeal. On top was Pokémon Red, Green and Blue at 31.4 million units, followed by Tetris at 30.3 million and Pokémon Gold and Silver at 23.1 million.

The PS4's early success arguably marked the beginning of the end of the PlayStation-Xbox rivalry. Microsoft's initial Xbox One pitch, which included a mandatory daily online check-in and a mandatory Kinect camera, drew near-universal scorn. Although Microsoft reversed most of its plans before launch, the damage was done — and Sony was all too happy to reap the benefits, to the tune of 117 million PS4s shipped.

Sony learned from its mistakes with the PS3. The PS4 was much easier on developers, with a PC-esque architecture from AMD. Sony also continued to build on its growing reputation for first-party exclusives, including The Last of Us Part II and Uncharted 4: A Thief's End. The console got two hardware revisions: a slimmer model (known unofficially as the PS4 Slim) and the PS4 Pro, which added 4K support.

The system's best-selling games were Grand Theft Auto V at an estimated 24.7 million units, Marvel's Spider-Man at 22.7 million and God of War (2018) at 21 million.

Rounding out the top 10 are the original PlayStation at 102.4 million units shipped, the Nintendo Wii at 101.6 million, the PS5 at over 95 million (and counting) the PS3 at 87.4 million and the Xbox 360 (Microsoft's only entry on the list) at an estimated 84 million.

One of the reasons the PS2's record has held up is that the industry changed. The gaming business is much bigger today, but mobile gaming, PCs and subscription services now fight for a piece of the pie. That makes it harder for any single system to rack up PS2-like numbers. And current-gen hardware tends to get more expensive, not cheaper — especially in the age of RAMageddon. Good luck finding a current console for the PS2's final $99 price, or even the roughly $150 that works out to today, adjusting for inflation.

The OG Switch could take the crown within the next two or three years, assuming Nintendo doesn't discontinue it before it crosses the line. But until then, the 26-year-old console that made its bones spinning The Matrix DVDs stays on its perch.

#317. What is the 'Dead Internet Theory' and should humanity be worried?

When spending time online today, do you ever get the feeling that digital life has become eerily less human? Perhaps social media posts seem a bit off, or you get the sneaking suspicion the customer service representative you are chatting with is actually a bot. If this sounds familiar, you aren't alone: a paradigm called the "dead internet theory" has been gaining momentum in the last several years, positing that the internet is increasingly dominated by non-human activity, including bots and AI-generated content.

Since the theory first started making the rounds on internet forums in the 2010s, AI has only gotten more powerful. In September 2026, OpenAI reported that it had found a solution to a famously difficult mathematics puzzle, the Navier-Stokes problem. Meanwhile, a 2026 report from cybersecurity group Thales found that automated traffic continues to overtake human activity online, with bots accounting for 53 percent of all web traffic, up from 51 percent the year before. And bots are now talking to each other without humans in the loop at all: In March, Meta acquired Moltbook — a social network on which AI chatbots communicate with one another, often discussing humans. So as the internet landscape potentially gets less human every day, what exactly is dead internet theory, where does it come from, and should we be worried?

Dead Internet Theory — the idea that the majority of the internet is propelled by bots and other non-human agents – has been floating around for quite some time. It first emerged as a conspiracy theory on internet forums in the late 2010s, but gained a more concrete framework following a post in 2021 on online community Agora Road titled, "Dead Internet Theory: Most of the Internet Is Fake," which argued "the Internet feels empty and devoid of people." Followers of the theory argued that the internet "died" around 2016 when search engines and social media feeds started surfacing more AI-generated content.

The popularity of chatbots and AI-generated content has only further expanded since then. In 2024, only 33 percent of Americans said they had used chatbots like ChatGPT, Gemini or Copilot, a study from Pew Research found. By 2026, that number had increased to nearly half of Americans at 49 percent. That same report found that 25 percent of Americans use chatbots every day. In many realms of society we are now facing chatbot loops: in schools, students are writing essays using AI, which teachers then grade with AI. In the job search, employees use chatbots to spit out optimized resumes and cover letters which are then analyzed by AI systems. Increasingly, humans are cut out of the conversation entirely, our voices replaced with bots.

On a personal level, this chatbot loop can feel uncanny. Those script-like posts we see on LinkedIn or a soulless AI-generated poster at your local cafe can induce a feeling of existential dread, but the consequences go beyond personal discomfort. Bots can spread disinformation that causes real harm. AI chatbots are increasingly a source of election disinformation, and little is being done legislatively to regulate this new frontier, though government agencies have issued warnings on deepfakes and other AI-powered risks to free elections.

As AI use grows, so has the backlash: 40 percent of Americans say they think AI will have a negative impact on society in the next 20 years, according to a Pew Research study. AI detection is becoming increasingly popular, with company Pangram receiving $9 million in backing in July 2026 for its AI detection system. Following the passage of the EU AI Act, OpenAI has started watermarking AI-generated content. With measures like these, people opposed to AI will hopefully be able to more easily spot and avoid the artificial slop in favor of the human-centric connection they initially came to the internet to find.
