#315. What happens to mathematics when AI becomes an oracle?

Mathematician Steven Strogatz discusses his new book, Big Math: The Hidden Codes That Run Our World.

Our modern world runs on math, from social media algorithms, package delivery, and weather prediction to medical imaging and machine learning. The rise of computers helped fuel its dominance, enabling us to work with increasingly large numbers that would otherwise be beyond the capacity of our finite human brains. But with the rise of large language models, AI chatbots, and, soon, quantum computing, so-called “big math” is beginning to outstrip its creators entirely. What that means, and how we might adapt, is the focus of a new book, Big Math: The Hidden Codes That Run Our World, by applied mathematicians Steven Strogatz and Alex Townsend.

Strogatz is perhaps best known in his field for his co-authorship of a seminal 1998 paper with Duncan Watts on the collective dynamics of small-world networks. He has also studied chaos theory as applied to physics, the geometry of supercoiled DNA, and the biological collective oscillators, such as swarms of synchronously flashing fireflies and crickets that chirp in unison. In 2005, Strogatz extended his study of synchronicity to the crowds that caused the wobbling of London’s Millennium Bridge.

An award-winning communicator, Strogatz wrote three series of columns on mathematics for The New York Times in 2010, 2012, and 2025 and hosted a podcast for Quanta magazine from 2020–2021. He has written four popular books: Sync (2003), The Calculus of Friendship (2009), The Joy of X (2012), and the New York Times bestseller Infinite Powers (2019).

This time around, Strogatz has found a well-matched co-author in Townsend, a younger colleague recently hired at Cornell who studies numerical analysis and deep learning. As senior faculty, Strogatz was assigned to mentor Townsend, and the two men began regularly lunching together in Risley Hall. Strogatz was intrigued by the popular book potential of Townsend’s course on the top 20 algorithms of the 20th century and suggested co-authorship. That concept slowly morphed into a book about big matrix calculations, and then into what became Big Math.

“There are so many books about pure math, as if computers didn’t exist,” Strogatz told Ars. “Alex and I are both very applied mathematicians. We thought there should be a book on computers and math, especially the class of really big problems with billions of variables or parameters that you just can’t approach in your head. A lot of the math we’ve had for centuries perfectly scales up to this computer moment. What the abacus did for arithmetic, the fangcheng (“rectangular array”) board did for systems of linear equations. It was a matrix you could manipulate with your hand and bamboo sticks. So this externalizing of math outside of our heads into our tools is a 2,000-year-old idea.”

“The mathematician in each of us will always want to know more,” Strogatz and Townsend wrote. “The human being must insist that knowledge serves something larger than itself. That might be the work of our century: letting our power grow without losing our soul. Big Math keeps getting bigger. So must we.”

Ars caught up with Strogatz to learn more.

Ars Technica: Let’s start by defining “big math,” because it’s such a broad umbrella term.

Steven Strogatz: Operationally, big math is “big” relative to human cognitive capacity. So it can be surprisingly small and yet big. We also wanted to expand it to things that people would think of as computational engineering—in the building of the Burj Khalifa, that’d be civil engineering or design architecture. It’s there in medical imaging. It’s in getting us to the Moon. The lunar module was controlled by a tiny computer that only had to think about 20 or 30 variables at a time to steer the LEM down to the Moon’s surface. But it’s not something a person could do in their head.

Big Math is not simply big in the ordinary sense of big numbers. There’s an undercurrent in the book about the importance of linearity and sparsity. When problems are linear, they can be scaled up to immense size, and they stay tractable. If they’re non-linear, you don’t have any hope of dealing with them with pure math. You can do them with computers. But for the people who want rigorous theorems, we’re in terra incognita when we deal with anything that’s non-linear and big.

Small math can be very hard. Historically, we had small math problems with one or two variables, arithmetic, algebra that you learn in high school. Even to this day, a lot of pure math is relatively small. It doesn’t concern itself with enormous numbers of variables simultaneously because it’s totally intractable. Cryptography can be small, but it’s so complicated with its non-linearities and other algebraic difficulties. So big is one way to be difficult, but there are a lot of other ways.

Nowadays, trillions of parameters are typical in the biggest of the large language models. But infinity is not big from this point. Infinity, which you would think of would be the biggest of big math, is not because calculus fits perfectly nicely in a human head. Infinity turns out to be very tractable in a way that a billion is not.

Ars: One of your real-world examples of big math is particularly relevant at the moment: mathematics as it relates to the gerrymandering issue.

Strogatz: It’s an interesting question of, what’s a fair map and what’s a reasonable redistricting? The legal system gives us certain criteria, but they are vague. The districts should not be too snakey-looking, not look like salamanders, for instance. The term in the law is ‘compact.’ They should be reasonably compact. But what does that mean mathematically? The districts are also supposed to be contiguous; you don’t want to have separate islands and call that one district. It’s supposed to respect natural boundaries like rivers or other landscape features. Also, each district is supposed to have roughly the same population.

So how to make maps that respect all of the criteria? It turns out there are a lot of ways to make them. Using past voting information, you can stack the deck. That’s what gerrymandering is all about, trying to redistrict in favor of whatever party is in power. There has been a big debate about whether certain maps violate people’s rights. The principle should be one person, one vote. If you do things that dilute somebody’s power so that their votes are wasted, so to speak, that’s traditionally thought to be unfair or maybe even illegal.

The Supreme Court has heard all kinds of cases, but the court is very resistant to the use of math. I quoted [Chief Justice] John Roberts talking about the efficiency gap as one measure of gerrymandering. He referred to it by its Greek letters and said something like, “The average person on the street is just going to think this is gobbledygook, that this is not a criterion that the law should pay any attention to.”

Nevertheless, Moon Duchin and her colleagues showed this very down-to-earth way of generating a big ensemble of reasonable maps that satisfy all the legal criteria so that you have a baseline, you could do the control experiment. What the math does is give you a principled way of generating maps at random that respect the criteria that the law requires, but otherwise are just a random sample of reasonable maps. How often would a map produce the result that the gerrymander intended? If you can show that it’s an extreme outlier, that’s a measure of something. Does it prove malicious intent? Not really. It does prove it’s not typical.

There was an Alabama voting rights case where Roberts actually voted with the plaintiffs, who said that there was real harm being done to Black voters in Alabama with one of Alabama’s maps. The state was claiming there’s no way we could get a reasonable map to have two districts with Black majority voters. Moon Duchin showed that was wrong. You could generate thousands of maps that would do that. So it just wasn’t true what the state had claimed.

After the book was copyedited and ready to go to press, a Supreme Court decision was made that, in fact, you can partisan gerrymander as much as you want. It doesn’t violate the Voting Rights Act. We wrote a more optimistic story.

Ars: How has math changed over time from a philosophical standpoint?

Strogatz: I think a crisp way to encapsulate it is the role of the computer. For a long time, a computer was a servant to mathematicians and engineers and scientists. It was no more philosophically threatening than a microscope or a telescope. It could extend our senses. It’s a chisel, it’s an anvil, it’s a tool. It helped us do calculations faster; things that would be too hard for people to do at scale you could do on a machine. We had mechanical calculators. They became electronic computers in the 1950s with the invention of the transistor and got much faster to give us today’s supercomputers. We program them; they do exactly what we tell them. So they’re servants.

The first philosophically big change is in the 1950s, when computers start to be used as more than servants; they become scouts. You could send the computer out into unknown, unexplored territory in the mathematical landscape. The clearest example was work by Enrico Fermi and his colleagues (Pasta, Ulam, and Tsingou) that we wrote about in the chapter on simulation. Mary Tsingou was the one who did the calculations in what was traditionally called the Fermi-Pasta-Ulam numerical experiment.

What distinguished it from earlier uses of computers is that Fermi and colleagues had no idea what the answer was going to be. It’s one thing to use a computer to calculate the power released by an atomic bomb blast or to calculate an artillery shell’s trajectory. We didn’t know the exact numerical values, but we knew what was happening. With this experiment, they were trying to simulate a simplified model of how atoms could, in the jargon, formalize as chemical bonds jiggle. You want to see the decay to entropy. How do systems degenerate to greater and greater disorder if they’re just left to their own devices?

Fermi thought that the non-linear aspect of chemical bonds—meaning if you’d stretch a chemical bond by twice as much, it doesn’t pull back exactly twice as hard, so it’s not strictly proportional—might account for the so-called second law of thermodynamics, the decay to entropy. Pencil and paper math was not up to the job because you couldn’t calculate these non-linear interactions far enough to see the decay into entropy.

So they ran it in the computer, and a shocking thing happened: It didn’t decay. It showed a strange echo. You started all the energy in one of the modes of vibration of this system, and then it started to degenerate. But after a while, 98 percent of the energy came back into the original mode. That was the echo that they called a recurrence phenomenon. It led us to a whole new branch of math that we call soliton theory. It was an early indication that once you walk into the nonlinear jungle, human intuition breaks down. We couldn’t walk there, but we sent our scout, this computer probe, into the wilderness of the mind, and it was the only thing capable of doing the calculations and reporting back to us what it found.

Ars: The rapid rise of AI in mathematics has alarmed many in the field, particularly in light of OpenAI’s release last month of 722 AI-generated math papers, dubbed the “mathocalypse.” Technically, AI is also a new tool, but it seems fundamentally different.

Strogatz: So from servant to scout was a new thing, and computing was our partner. At first, it was all very thrilling. It was like having a new Ferrari or something. You could race ahead and then discover things. That’s what Fermi and Ulam said at the time. That was truly amazing. They didn’t know what to expect. The computer was now teaching us by showing us things, but we could still keep up. We could develop these new theories like soliton theory, so we could rationalize what it was showing us.

What’s different now is that it’s going from servant to scout to oracle, where it’s showing us things that we don’t understand and it’s not articulate enough to explain itself. It seems to be right. A clear example would be something like Google Translate, where it’s fantastic at translating one language into another, but it has not discovered new principles of linguistics, or if it has, it can’t tell us what they are.

Likewise, protein folding with AlphaFold that won the Nobel Prize in chemistry a couple of years ago for the people who built it. We now can solve a thousand times more proteins than we had before, but we have not done it by learning new physics or chemistry. It just recognized patterns, but it can’t tell us how it’s doing it. So that’s where we are, and it’s going to get worse.

Ars: In your book, you ask if insight still matters, citing Stephen Wolfram as an example of someone who might answer that question in the negative.

Strogatz: Wolfram’s position is tricky. He pushes computational irreducibility. I love these two words, perspicuous and perspicacious. Systems can be perspicuous; you can see into them. Science has, for thousands of years, concerned itself with systems that are perspicuous, meaning you can get insight into them because you’re so perspicacious. But the question from Wolfram would be, have we been the famous drunk that’s looking under the lamppost for his keys because it’s dark everywhere else? You wouldn’t have any hope of finding them except under the lamppost.

So is it that we’ve artificially restricted ourself to systems that are transparent enough that we can develop science that our insight illuminates? I think Wolfram would claim that most of nature is computationally irreducible in the sense that there is no simple insightful theory into what’s going on. The best you can do is just run a simulation and watch what happens, similar to just letting nature unfold and watch what happens in reality.

In the jargon people use nowadays, you shouldn’t expect a compressed description, a simplified model that will account for what you’re seeing. We have seen those. Newton could explain the motion of the planets from a few simple principles and that worked, but that’s, I think in Wolfram’s take, artificially simple. Most of science is not going to end up being like that. We don’t know if Wolfram is really right about that. Wolfram, I think, is demonstrably right that many computer simulations are computationally irreducible, but nature might not be.

Ars: If you can’t see into the models, and they can’t explain what they’re doing, how do you know they’re right?

Strogatz: Let’s stipulate that we don’t understand them, and that they are not at the present able to explain themselves to us. Let’s put a little asterisk next to that part, though, because it could be that they’ll become very good teachers. Or maybe we don’t understand Google Translate or AlphaFold right now, but we will in 10 years. I do believe that there will be systems that are truly opaque because of human limits, that we just won’t be fast enough or smart enough or have enough memory. AIs will show us something that we just won’t be able to understand even with their help. What happens then?

Then I think math starts to be more like science or medicine, or engineering. At the FDA, for a new drug to be approved, it has to be shown to be safe by doing a lot of controlled studies and randomized trials. There’s a whole methodology for opacity that people in medicine have worked out. But the doctors do not require knowledge of the mechanism of action. You don’t have to know how aspirin works. You don’t have to know how electroconvulsive therapy works for people with intractable depression. Even if we don’t understand, we can still live with these technologies or drugs by these methods. You don’t have to have first principles understanding. If the AI is an oracle, you can still check it by seeing how often it is right.

Strogatz: It is a loss. I don’t want to pooh-pooh the severity of the—to use a fancy philosophical word—epistemic loss. What we have prided ourselves on in pure math for so long is that we’re the only subject that has absolute proof. We have certainty. We’re as close to certainty as anything that humanity can do. We can write a proof. Euclid’s proofs are still correct today, even though he wrote them in 300 BC. There’s nothing in the science or medicine of 300 BC that is still accepted, but all of the math is still true.

So there is something eternal and very special about math. Even though we’re just primates, somehow we’re tapping into something really deep. And so to say, “Well, science and engineering are the models for math,” would be a big sacrifice to the purest of pure mathematicians. They don’t want to give up the certainty that we’ve always been so proud of.

Ars: There’s a sadness among many mathematicians, not just for the epistemic questions, but also for the impact on why we do math in the first place. You write about that “aha!” moment when you’re struggling with a problem and suddenly you see the pattern. Are we losing that?

Strogatz: There’s so many things that mathematicians are in grief about, those of us who feel grief. I have been through the whole sequence of the five stages of grief. I’m joking with you a little. But the young students don’t think this is funny. I’m an old person at the end of my career, so it’s one thing for me to be philosophical about it. But imagine being 25 years old and you love this subject and you’ve dreamt about being a mathematician, and now people are saying the subject is over. There are even people gloating about it. Some of the people in the tech community are very cruel about what’s happening to math.

We talked about the epistemic aspect, but there’s also the culture of productive struggle. The pure math people like Terry Tao have been emphasizing understanding. That’s our idea of insight, that when you try hard to understand something, even if you don’t solve it, you often are so resourceful in thinking of strategies or other simplified versions of the problem. You learn a lot on the journey. You also have all this companionship and human joy of being with other people who love the thing you love. This is not restricted to math. The people who love art or weaving—my colleagues in creative writing are bereft over what their students are experiencing now, handing in AI [generated work]. They want writing with soul, with human individuality.

Ars: That is the opposite of what AI does. I guess I worry that it will do the same thing to math. There is a beauty to math, an aesthetic element. If all you’re focused on is getting the answers and ignore the journey, those are qualitative losses. Mathematicians have individual voices, too.

Strogatz: Totally. There are people whose style is very visible in the questions they ask, in the approach they take to them, in the way they explain themselves. I have cried on camera for several interviews over the past few months because there are lots of things maybe being lost. But I want to put an underline on the fact that we don’t really know. For example, what if AI gets really good at teaching us? I find when I interact with it, that it can be a very effective explainer.

And why do we have to feel sad? Maybe we can understand math better than ever with the help of this machine. We may not get to make the greatest discoveries because the machine will probably be making them, but we can still make little discoveries. I never made a great discovery. I’m not at the level of a Fields medalist, but I can still enjoy math and so can people way below me. There are recreational mathematicians who take pleasure in discovery, whatever they figure out on their own. Just like when a person solves a puzzle, it’s not like a jigsaw puzzle or Sudoku is important. You just have the pleasure of thinking.

There are people who love the workout part of it. There are people who like the competition. We shouldn’t pretend. There are people who are thrilled by the idea of being the first to make a big discovery. That’s animating for some folks, and it will be hard for them.

Strogatz: So far the AIs are not good at asking questions. I think they could be trained to ask good questions. A graduate student is often not good at asking questions when they start because in college you’re not taught to ask questions, you’re taught to give answers. The hardest thing in training a new PhD or postdoc is helping them figure out how to ask questions. I think we could do that with AIs, too.

There is a feeling of loss, but I don’t think we should shortchange the feeling of excitement. There’s so many practical, important benefits that could happen in energy technology and climate and medicine, theoretical physics, pure math. I mean, we’re making astonishing discoveries through this machine. So for a lot of people, it’s a very joyful time. It’s very confusing. That’s the only honest assessment.

We end the book with Faust. Usually people hear “Faustian bargain” as, “You have sold your soul to the devil, now you’re going to pay.” Goethe’s version is more ambiguous: that if we keep striving to build something bigger than ourselves, maybe we can be redeemed. But it’s still a very dangerous game that we have chosen to play.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

→ Baca artikel asli

#314. Kwame Brathwaite, photographer of 'Black is Beautiful' movement, dies at 85

Editor’s Note:  This article was originally published by The Art Newspaper, an editorial partner of CNN Style.

Kwame Brathwaite, the pioneering activist and photographer whose work helped define the aesthetics of the “Black is Beautiful” movement of the 1960s and beyond, died on April 1, aged 85.

His son, Kwame Brathwaite, Jr, announced his father’s death in an Instagram post that read in part, “I am deeply saddened to share that my Baba, the patriarch of our family, our rock and my hero has transitioned.”

Brathwaite’s work has been the subject of resurgent interest from curators, historians and collectors in recent years, and his first major institutional retrospective, which was organized by the Aperture Foundation, made its debut in 2019 at the Skirball Cultural Center in Los Angeles before touring the country.

Brathwaite was born in 1938 to Barbadian immigrants, in what he referred to as “the People’s Republic of Brooklyn” in New York, though his family moved from there to Harlem and then to the South Bronx when Brathwaite was 5 years old. He attended the School of Industrial Art (now the High School of Art and Design) and, according to profiles of Brathwaite in T Magazine and Vice, was drawn to photography by two moments. The first was in August of 1955, when a 17-year-old Brathwaite encountered David Jackson’s haunting photograph of a brutalized Emmett Till in his open casket. The second was in 1956, when — after he and his brother Elombe co-founded the African Jazz Arts Society and Studios (AJASS) — Brathwaite saw a young man taking photos in a dark jazz club without the use of a flash, and his mind became alight with possibility.

Using a Hasselblad medium-format camera, Brathwaite attempted to do the same, learning to work with limited light in a manner that enhanced the visual narrative of his imagery. He would soon also develop a darkroom technique that enriched and deepened how Black skin would appear in his photography, honing the practice in a small darkroom in his Harlem apartment. Brathwaite went on to photograph jazz legends performing throughout the 1950s and ’60s, including Miles Davis, John Coltrane, Thelonious Monk and others.

“You want to get the feeling, the mood that you’re experiencing when they’re playing,” Brathwaite told Aperture Magazine in 2017. “That’s the thing. You want to capture that.”

A 106-year-old from the Philippines is Vogue's oldest ever cover model

By the early 1960s, alongside the rest of AJASS, Brathwaite began using his photography and organizing prowess to consciously push back against whitewashed, Eurocentric beauty standards. The group came up with the concept of the Grandassa Models, young Black women whom Brathwaite would photograph, celebrating and accentuating their features. In 1962, AJASS organized “Naturally ’62”, a fashion show held in a Harlem club called the Purple Manor and featuring the models. The show would go on to be held regularly until 1992. In 1966, Brathwaite married his wife Sikolo, a Grandassa Model whom he had met on the street the year prior when he asked if he could take her portrait. The two remained married for the rest of Brathwaite’s life.

By the 1970s, Brathwaite’s focus on jazz shifted to other forms of popular Black music. In 1974, he traveled to Africa with the Jackson Five to document their tour, also photographing the historic “Rumble in the Jungle” boxing match between Muhammad Ali and George Foreman in what’s now the Democratic Republic of Congo that same year. Commissions in this era also saw Brathwaite photographing Nina Simone, Stevie Wonder, Sly and the Family Stone, Bob Marley and other music legends.

Creating for community: The Black designers placing identity and heritage at the center of their brands

Throughout the ensuing decades, Brathwaite continued to explore and develop his mode of photography, all through the lens of the “Black is Beautiful” ethos. In 2016, Brathwaite joined the roster of Philip Martin Gallery in Los Angeles, and he was continuing to photograph commissions as recently as 2018, when he shot artist and stylist Joanne Petit-Frère for The New Yorker.

T Magazine’s 2021 profile, published on the occasion of Brathwaite’s retrospective traveling to the Blanton Museum of Art in Austin, Texas, noted that the photographer’s health was failing such that he was unable to be interviewed for the article. A separate exhibition, “Kwame Brathwaite: Things Well Worth Waiting For,” is currently on view at the Art Institute of Chicago, where it will remain until July 24.

Top image: Kwame Brathwaite, “Untitled (Sikolo Brathwaite, Orange Portrait),” 1968

#310. Meta warns some of its AI glasses may get 'too warm' if they get wet

The company is offering free replacements to affected devices.

Meta is rolling out a software update for its AI glasses to fix an issue that causes some devices to become "too warm" when they get wet. The company describes it as a "rare issue" affecting only a "small number" of its glasses, but is nonetheless urging everyone with a pair of Meta Glasses to update their software and offering free replacements to those affected.

The company has begun notifying users about the forthcoming update which will roll out to everyone with a pair of Meta-branded frames, which includes Adventurer, Fury, Starfire, Nova and Capri models. Oakley and Ray-Ban glasses are not impacted, according to Meta.

While Meta doesn't use the word "overheating," the company compares its glasses fix to how smartphones automatically shut down when they get too hot. "We've implemented a software update to fix a rare issue that caused a small number of Meta Glasses to get too warm when moisture entered the device," a Meta spokesperson said in a statement. "It works similarly to a phone shutting off automatically when it needs to cool down. If people experience this, they can contact Meta Customer Service for a replacement."

In an email to users reported by 9to5Google, Meta says that the heat-related issue can be triggered by "excessive moisture or sweat" entering the glasses. That suggests that at least some of the company's own AI glasses may not be as water resistant as they're supposed to be. According to Meta, all of its AI glasses come with IPX4 water resistance which "means your glasses are safe if you get caught in light rain while you wear them."

The company hasn't indicated how many pairs of glasses might be affected, but says that those who experience the moisture-related shutdowns will be able to get a free replacement pair of glasses. Affected glasses will show a push notification if they do turn off unexpectedly, and the alert will direct them to Meta's customer support.

In the meantime, the company is urging everyone with a pair of Meta Glasses to update to the latest software, version 130, to ensure "the best performance." You can update your glasses from the Meta AI app by heading to Settings > Glasses > Updates. The version 130 update is still rolling out so it may take a day or so to hit everyone.

#309. Grok Build coding agent leaks oodles of confidential info, some belonging to SpaceXSI

Researcher finds 35,000 Grok Build apps wide open to anyone who looked for them.

Grok Build, the vibe-coding agent from SpaceXSI, is leaking data—some of it proprietary business information and other sensitive secrets—from thousands of its users, including the very same Elon Musk-owned company that offers the platform, new research shows.

The information is revealed through links allowing Grok Build users to share their apps with others. Grok takes steps to randomize the URLs so only people who know the exact URL can access the data stored there. A design decision by developers of the platform, however, completely torpedoes that objective and makes it possible for anyone to enumerate thousands of live Grok Build projects. Except in rare cases, clicking those links opens the app and reveals all data associated with it.

Research from security firm Claroc shows that the exposed data includes things like internal business plans, ad proposals, medical exam results, galley checklists from a celebrity cruise line, and employee names and ID numbers. Ironically, some of the sensitive data comes from two-dozen apps built by SpaceXSI (the name of the now-merged SpaceX and xAI) itself. A small sampling of the documents revealed in the SpaceXSI apps—Claroc found 24 of them—include an onboarding plan, marked confidential, for a new member of SpaceXSI’s enterprise sales team, a sales territory map of 594 accounts, internal system addresses for sales team tools, and advertising proposals.

“We were shocked,” Claroc said in a Tuesday post. “The company that makes Grok had its own internal material open to anyone holding the link, and we had never been given it. Had the sales team bought a tool for this, it would have sat behind company sign-in, open only to the team. Built with AI, it went live open to anyone with the link.

Claroc found sensitive data belonging to other Grok Build users, some working for Fortune 500 companies and a defense contractor. Some of the affected organizations and data revealed includes:

Asaf Aprozper, the co-founder and CTO of Claroc, said he’s in the process of notifying companies. After speaking to the CISO of the luxury cruise company, it “pulled in their whole security team as soon as we shared the findings and are already working to contain the Grok exposure.”

Link sharing for Grok Build is intended to streamline the process of developing and deploying apps. A user can input speech or text and have a working app ready for internal testing within minutes. The links are designed to give developers a way to get the app in front of authorized users quickly. The very left of the URLs contain two or more “dice words,” meaning randomly generated phrases such as “failing chassis traders,” although users can modify them. The rightmost portion of the link is grok.me. The URL for an X ads campaign plan was: https://cinder-crane-sand-frost.grok.me.

The scheme is intended to prevent links from being easily guessable. When done correctly, a phrase comprising three dice words has roughly 38 bits of entropy, amounting to billions of possible combinations. The idea—sometimes called capability-based access control—is that possession of the URL serves as authorization to the resources behind it. Google has long used this technique to make URLs easy to share but hard to guess. A URL share on that platform might look like: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1BxiMVs0XRA5nFMdKvBdBZjgmUUqptlbs74OgvE2upms/edit

There’s a fatal flaw in the way SpaceXSI has implemented the mechanism. Instead of being to the right of the domain—as is the case with Google links—the randomness for Grok Build links is positioned to the left of it. That allows the links to be easily enumerated using passive DNS services. These services monitor central DNS lookup servers. When a server lookup requests the IP address of a previously never-before-seen URL, the transaction is captured in a publicly searchable database. Had Grok followed Google’s approach, the links would have been invisible to passive DNS searchers. Muse, the Meta agent that CEO Mark Zuckerberg said was built from the ground up for security, recently suspended a link-sharing feature after it too was found leaking sensitive data.

Aprozper said a passive DNS search returned 43,601 links ending in grok.me. Of them, more than 35,000, or nearly 95 percent, were public, meaning that clicking on the link opened the corresponding app and all data it stored. He said that tests he conducted over the past week showed that, by default, Grok sets links to “Anyone with the link.” Users have the option to change the setting to “invited users” or “only you.”

A representative with Cursor, the AI coding service SpaceXSI recently acquired, said in an email that Grok Build “apps are private by default and nothing goes public unless the creator chooses to share it.” The representative said that competing coding agents from ChatGPT, Lovable, and Bolt also give users the ability to generate shareable links.

Aprozper’s tests contradict the representative’s statement. In all cases, Grok Build required users to change anyone with the link to one of the other two options.

The Cursor statement regarding other AI coding platforms is correct. Aprozper said that app builders, including Lovable, Bolt, and Replit, “also give each app its own web address, so the same DNS records expose them, too. However, Grok is where we found by far the most sensitive company data.” That means that people using these other platforms are also at risk of exposing sensitive data through shared links. Interestingly, AI platforms that follow the correct sharing link practice include Claude’s published artifacts (claude.ai/public/artifacts/), ChatGPT’s shared conversations (chatgpt.com/share/), and Gemini (gemini.google.com/share/). For all three, the secret randomness is in the path, so DNS never sees it.

The representative didn’t respond to follow-up questions, including if SpaceXSI intentionally exposed the proprietary internal information, why the company removed the data after Ars asked about it, and if it planned to notify customers who also exposed similar types of information. Anyone using shareable links with Grok Build should choose the “invite others” option unless they intend to make an app widely available.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#308. Study finds slightly higher risk of autism diagnosis in areas with more lithium in drinking water, but experts say more research is needed

A new study found a moderately higher risk of autism spectrum disorder in children born to pregnant people exposed to tap water with higher levels of lithium, but experts caution that this association does not show a direct link between the two.

About 1 in 36 children in the US is diagnosed with autism spectrum disorder (ASD) each year, according to data from the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Scientists still don’t know the exact cause of autism, a developmental disorder. Genetics may be a factor, but some have been looking at potential environmental causes, too.

Autism diagnosis has become more common, but pandemic disrupted early detection, CDC says

Cases may be on the rise, but that is also unclear. One study published this year on cases in the New York-New Jersey area found that autism diagnosis rates tripled among certain age groups between 2000 and 2016. A 2021 report found similar increases in cases, but the CDC says the increased number of cases is most likely linked to more doctors screening for the condition.

Lithium is an alkali metal that can be found naturally in some food and ground water. It’s used in batteries, grease and air conditioners, as well as in the treatment of bipolar disorder and some blood disorders. Its levels in US drinking water are not regulated, according to the US Geological Survey.

A new study, published Monday in the journal JAMA Pediatrics, found a small association between lithium and autism diagnosis in Denmark, where the researchers say the level of lithium in drinking water is similar to that in American water systems.

The researchers checked a database of people with psychiatric disorders for children born between 2000 and 2013 to find information on 8,842 cases of ASD and 43,864 participants who did not have ASD. They then measured the concentration of lithium in 151 public waterworks that served more than half of the Danish population and mapped out where pregnant people lived in relation.

For many neurodiverse people, 'The Sims' has been a lifelong comfort

As lithium levels in water increased, there was a modest increased risk of an ASD diagnosis. Specifically, compared with people at the lowest exposure level, those who had the second and third highest exposure during pregnancy had a 24% to 26% higher risk of ASD diagnosed in children. The group with the highest exposure had a 46% higher risk than those at the lowest level of exposure.

The researchers could not tell how much water the pregnant people drank, but they picked Denmark in part because residents there consume some of the lowest amounts of bottled water in Europe.

Experts say it’s important to note that the research can’t show that lithium exposure leads directly to an autism diagnosis.

Further study is required, said study co-author Dr. Beate Ritz, a professor of neurology in the David Geffen School of Medicine at UCLA, and a professor of epidemiology and environmental health at the UCLA Fielding School of Public Health.

“Any drinking water contaminants that may affect the developing human brain deserve intense scrutiny,” Ritz said in a news release. She added that the research would need to be replicated in other countries to look for a similar connection.

The implications of the findings are complex as far as public health policy is concerned, according to an editorial published alongside the study. Lithium levels in water, at concentrations that the study associated with a potential ASD risk, have also been linked with health benefits such as lower rates of hospitalization for psychiatric disorders and suicide.

“If all these of associations are valid, the wisdom of Solomon will be required to develop guidelines for lithium in drinking water that are maximally protective of the entire population,” wrote Dr. David C. Bellinger, a professor of neurology and psychology at Harvard Medical School. “Until the basic biology of ASD is better understood, it will be difficult to distinguish causal from spurious associations.”

'Heartbreaking' delays in autism diagnosis and treatment got even worse during pandemic

Dr. Max Wiznitzer, director of the Rainbow Autism Center at University Hospitals Rainbow Babies and Children’s Hospital in Cleveland, points to other research on the effects of lithium on pregnant people who take it for mental health disorders. Those studies – which look at people exposed to much higher levels than are found in drinking water – do not show a connection with autism spectrum disorder.

“It’s an interesting association, but causation is definitely not proven,” said Wiznitzer, who was not involved in the new research. “We have to see if there’s a viable and biologically plausible mechanism by which a small amount of lithium in the water supply can somehow do this, yet pharmacologic dosing of lithium in women with bipolar disorder has not been reported to be causing increased risk of ASD.”

Other studies have also suggested connections between ASD and environmental exposures to things like pesticides, air pollution and phthalates. But none of them points to any of these factors as a direct cause of the disorder.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

A link between environmental exposure and ASD is hard to prove, Wiznitzer said. With research showing that increased exposure to air pollution raises the risk of giving birth to a child with ASD, for example, he often wonders whether pollution is the determining factor or if it’s just the populations who live in more polluted areas.

“There’s a lot of speculation about about environmental factors, but how many of them are truly causally associated?” Wiznitzer said. “We are bombarded with a variety of environmental stressors in our everyday lives. We have to figure out how to basically safely navigate them, and this is probably not one that’s high on our list.”

Xiaomi's new software has started rolling out in China, but older phones are being left behind.

Xiaomi also claims that HyperOS 4 leaves 27.2 percent more memory free after eight hours of use and opens apps 17.5 percent faster after long sessions. Meanwhile, its voice assistant, Super XiaoAI 2.0, now runs on Xiaomi's own MiMo AI model and adds an Expert Mode that can carry out multi-step tasks across apps, though most of the AI features are limited to China for now. HyperOS 4 is reportedly based on Android 17, but Xiaomi's newest phones already got Android 17 in July 2026 through an interim release called HyperOS 3.3.

#302. How SCSKiller is trying to fix shader stuttering in PC games

It's difficult to state just how much I hate shader stutter, even in the best PC games. I'm probably the world's biggest arachnophobe and absolutely loathe mushrooms, yet I'd rather have a trained tarantula serve me deep-fried shiitake than endure the infamously stutter-heavy Star Wars Jedi: Survivor. At least, that was until I downloaded Shader Compilation Stutter Killer (SCSKiller).

This free app promises to heavily reduce or even completely get rid of shader compilation in modern PC titles. But can this open-source software actually deliver? I've tested it out with a handful of games that are notorious for stuttering, and when it's working at its best, SCSKiller is ingenious. Are you a frame-obsessed PC gamer? You need to download this app ASAP, and here's why.

Before explaining why SCSKiller is great, let's address what shader stutter actually is. In short, it's a brief visual hiccup that looks like a frame rate drop to the untrained eye. Why is this a PC-specific issue and not a console one? Because PC development isn't based on fixed hardware, whereas studios can pre-compile shaders focused on exact PS5 or Xbox Series X specs pre-release.

While frame dips and shader stutters can occur at the same time (here's how to improve FPS for better gaming), they're actually separate issues. Frame drops normally happen because of hardware-related bottlenecks; where either the GPU or CPU can't render certain visual elements or physics tasks quickly enough.

Shader stuttering is different. These little programs tell your GPU how to draw various in-game effects, like shadows, reflections or lighting. In many modern PC titles — especially Unreal 5 Engine games — shader stuttering occurs because the rendering pipeline is having to produce effects in real-time. As the next frame waits for the shader to compile, a brief frame spike may easily occur. Once you're aware of them, in-game stutters can be so distracting that they ruin immersion.

Common variety stutters can sometimes be largely overcome if you own a VRR display. Variable Refresh Rate screens (like NVIDIA G-Sync and AMD FreeSync models) dynamically match the refresh rate of your panel to the number of frames your GPU is currently drawing, substantially smoothing out visual performance. VRR displays can also help fix screen tearing in PC and console games.

CPU-based compilation stutters can't be fixed by screen or GPU tech. In these cases, stuttering can last for a couple of seconds, dropping in-game action to 0 fps, thus completely breaking gameplay. This happens because your PC is seeing a particular effect for the very first time. That's why compilation stutter is at its worst when initially entering new areas.

SCSKiller can vastly reduce stutter in the PC games I've tested it with, but it can't fix all stuttering. Instead, this app has been designed specifically to target and eliminate shader compilation stutter. In that regard, it's hugely successful.

I picked out a few games on my rig that are infamous for compilation stutter. In all but one instance, SCSKiller slayed this horrible PC defect. Once you install it, the tool automatically reads your game libraries, instantly flagging the titles that can benefit most under a "Recommended to compile" list.

By pre-compiling shaders into my graphics card's driver cache before I launched each title, I found compilation stutter was more or less eradicated in Silent Hill f, Silent Hill Townfall, Elden Ring and The Witcher 3: Wild Hunt — Remastered. Before I discovered SSCKiller, I found both Townfall and Wild Hunt to be unplayable due to shader comp stutter. For context, I'm super sensitive to frame spikes. After I let this app pre-compile the shaders for both, the ruinous hitches I experienced before disappeared. SSCKiller is a complete game-changer in the titles I've just mentioned.

My results from Silent Hill f have proven equally impressive. Before I pre-compiled the Japanese spookster's shaders, SSCKiller's advanced stats informed me my previous 10-minute session had been blighted with precisely 25 compilation stutters (highest being 246 ms), 67 shader hitches, with a 1 percent low of just 3 fps. After using the app, comp stuttering went down to zero, with my G-Sync monitor making any smaller hitches incredibly difficult to spot. As I said, total game-changer.

The pros are abundant and obvious; the cons relatively few. SSCKiller is free, intuitive to use, boasts handy before and after compilation stats, and that instant library-reading feature I mentioned means you don't have to go about digging for specific game inventories. When SSCKiller's mojo is cooking, it comprehensively fixes this loathsome defect.

The app is refreshingly upfront about its current limitations. As I type this, most of the games I have installed on my custom-built rig show as unsupported via the in-software filter. There's a handy "Why?" button that explains this limitation. At time of writing, a lot of titles throw up a "Not supported yet" error, explaining "shaders are compressed or packed: needs an engine reader".

Thankfully, you can get around this fairly easily via the built-in recorder feature. Simply click the "Record while I play" slider located in the bottom right corner of the app after you select a game and SCSKiller can analyze the rendering pipeline and pre-configure shaders after 10 minutes or so of gameplay. Be warned though: Using the app can trigger bans when playing games with anti-cheat software, so make sure you set Elden Ring to launch offline when using this tool.

Pre-compilation cycles can take a while, too. Though most of the games I configured only took a few minutes to process, even my mighty AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D needed 17 minutes to compile Hogwarts Legacy's 291,688 shaders.

On that note, SSCKiller can't fix Hogwarts. Even on my NVIDIA RTX 5090 rig, Portkey Games wizarding open-world has always been a horribly optimized port. It threw up six major compilation hitches during a 10-minute run despite the app's best efforts. For games blighted with traversal stutter and CPU spikes (like Hogwarts and 2023's Dead Space remake), unless you use a VRR display, stuttering will remain visible. For a gratis piece of software though, SSCKiller does one heck of a job at smoothing out compilation stutter.

→ Baca artikel asli

#301. Democrats oppose plan to stack FCC with Republicans, plan probes of Chairman Carr

Democrats accuse FCC nominee of evading questions about Carr, her current boss.

Senate Democrats today opposed President Trump’s nominee for an open Federal Communications Commission seat, and criticized Republicans for moving to confirm a new commissioner during the period before Democrats take control of Congress in January 2027. Democrats also plan to use their majority next year to launch probes of FCC Chairman Brendan Carr.

“After the American people last week overwhelmingly rejected the MAGA agenda, there is no justification for this committee to advance partisan nominees during the lame-duck period,” Sen. Ed Markey (D-Mass.) said today at the Commerce Committee’s nomination hearing for Danielle Thumann Severs, Trump’s pick to fill an empty commissioner’s seat.

Thumann Severs, an attorney and senior counsel to Carr, faced questions from Democrats about whether she supports Carr’s attacks on TV broadcasters.

“Do you support Chairman Carr’s illegal campaign to remove Jimmy Kimmel from the nation’s airwaves?” Sen. Ben Ray Luján (D-N.M.) asked the nominee.

Thumann Severs responded that she has not advised Carr on media-related topics and declined Luján’s request to identify any specific policy issues on which she disagrees with Carr.

Luján predicted trouble for Carr when Democrats take the majority in both chambers of Congress. He seemed to suggest that Carr will quickly grow tired of having to testify in Congress multiple times a month under the Democratic majorities.

“I don’t think Chairman Carr is going to be around more than another three or four months because when we have the gavels in here, he will be here once a month,” Luján said. “And Mr. Carr, if you’re listening now, know you’re going to be in this body once a month, if not more, and in the House of Representatives as well, answering questions, and you don’t have the protection of the gavel any longer.” Luján said Democrats will use “subpoena authorities that you have to respond to so we can get to the bottom of the nonsense and the cover-ups that have been taking place.”

Markey accused Thumann Severs of evading questions about Carr threatening and taking actions against broadcasters on behalf of Trump. “Anyone seeking a seat on the Federal Communications Commission must be willing to stand up to such threats and to defend the First Amendment, and you’re not willing to do so here in your confirmation hearing,” Markey said.

Democrats similarly criticized Thumann Severs for not giving a clear answer on whether the FCC has authority to eliminate E-Rate, a $2 billion-a-year program that was created by Congress to connect students and library visitors to the Internet. Carr has proposed eliminating or scaling back the program. Thumann Severs said she helped Carr work on the E-Rate Notice of Proposed Rulemaking, which seeks public comment about the program and the FCC’s authority to change or eliminate it.

“I can’t prejudge the open proceeding [on E-Rate] at the FCC right now,” she said today.

Sen. Tammy Duckworth (D-Ill.) criticized Thumann Severs’ answers. “If you’re confirmed, you’re going to be one of the votes that decides whether the FCC stays an independent regulator, or becomes an arm of the White House press office. We deserve to know where you stand, and I am not comfortable with your answers today,” Duckworth said.

Duckworth told Thumann Severs that the FCC cannot eliminate E-Rate without Congressional approval. “We never directed the FCC to eliminate the program and frankly, at a time when the price of gas, groceries, and utilities is skyrocketing, the last thing the FCC should be doing is making it harder for schools to stay connected,” Duckworth said.

The Senate Commerce, Science & Transportation Committee held today’s hearing for Thumann Severs and three other nominees for federal positions. Senators have until Thursday to submit additional questions for the record, and nominees will have until November 16 to respond.

Thumann Severs needs approval from the committee and then the full Senate to be confirmed to the FCC. Committee Chairman Ted Cruz (R-Texas) is trying to get her approved before Republicans have to give up their Senate majority.

Thumann Severs would give Republicans an unusual 3-1 advantage on the FCC, which usually operates with a 3-2 split. Democrats criticized Republicans for not pairing her nomination with a Democratic one. The commission has only one Democrat, Anna Gomez, and she would have to leave at the end of 2027 if she is not given another term.

“For many decades, the Senate has paired commission nominees: A Republican and a Democrat moving together,” Sen. John Hickenlooper (D-Colo.) said. “Congress established the FCC as an independent, bipartisan agency… a bipartisan FCC is essential to avoid things like large-scale media consolidation, and I think also more fundamentally to protect a free and diverse press.”

Because US law requires the FCC to have three commissioners for a quorum, Gomez’s “presence in the current 2-1 Republican majority is necessary for the FCC to conduct business,” Hickenlooper said. The Communications Act prohibits a single party from holding more FCC seats than is necessary to form a majority, but Hickenlooper predicted that Trump will fire Gomez to give Republicans a 3-0 stranglehold.

“If today’s nominee is confirmed, President Trump could then fire Commissioner Gomez and have an FCC with no Democratic voice at all,” Hickenlooper said.

Hickenlooper questioned whether Thumann Severs is sufficiently independent from Carr. “Ms. Thumann Severs, your career has largely been spent serving Brendan Carr,” Hickenlooper said. “[You are] currently Chairman Carr’s senior counsel; previously, you were legal advisor to Commissioner Carr. Your official duties include near-daily meetings with Chairman Carr and working to advance his agenda, amplify his messaging.”

Markey asked Thumann Severs if it was appropriate for Carr to use the FCC’s regulatory authority to pressure broadcasters into taking a comedian off the air. Markey was referring to an incident last year in which Carr threatened to revoke ABC broadcast TV licenses over remarks made by Kimmel, who was then suspended by ABC owner Disney.

“We can do this the easy way or the hard way,” Carr said at the time, adding that Disney must “take action” on Kimmel “or there’s going to be additional work for the FCC ahead.”

When asked about Carr’s comments today, Thumann Severs said, “Those were his words. They were not my words, and I have not advised him or worked with the chairman on these particular issues. I can say that if I were to be confirmed as commissioner, I would make all of my decisions [based on] the facts and the law and the record.”

Senate Democrats tried to get Thumann Severs to discuss Carr’s decision to order an early review of ABC station licenses. “Do you think the FCC should revoke broadcasters’ licenses because the president does not like their coverage?” Duckworth asked.

Thumann Severs answered that the FCC “has a responsibility to fully investigate any complaint… and to evaluate that complaint on the merits. I don’t think a decision should be made purely based on someone’s discontent with the content being aired.”

Although the ABC license review is widely seen as retaliation against the network for its news coverage and other programming, Carr’s proceeding is ostensibly based on an allegation that Disney’s diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI) practices are discriminatory.

“Based on publicly available material, which is all I have seen on that particular matter, the licenses were called for early renewal as part of an investigation into potential rule violations,” Thumann Severs said today.

Cruz mentioned the Carr FCC’s review of ABC licenses and noted that “ABC argues [the review] is actually in retaliation for the network’s programming.” Cruz also alleged that in Democratic administrations, “the FCC has previously used its public interest standard as a weapon against conservatives.”

“If you are confirmed, will you commit to basing licensing decisions on the law and the record and to not using the FCC’s authority to pressure or punish a broadcaster for protected speech?” Cruz asked the nominee. She answered, “Yes.”

Cruz then asked Thumann Severs if she supports the JAWBONE Act, a bill that Cruz introduced with Sen. Ron Wyden (D-Ore.) to enable lawsuits against federal officials who try to coerce broadcasters or tech platforms into restricting speech.

Thumann Severs answered that she supports the bill. “I think it’s incredibly important that federal officials not use their position… to coerce against protected speech,” she said.

Cruz seemed satisfied with her answers. While Democrats recognized that Republicans have the votes to advance the nomination, they spent much of the hearing criticizing Thumann Severs’ answers and the Republican plan to confirm her without a paired Democratic nominee.

“We should not be confirming a Republican nominee without also renominating and confirming Anna Gomez, the Democrat, and filling the other vacant Democratic seat,” Markey said. “There is a potential next year where there will be three Republicans and no Democrats on the Federal Communications Commission… that is wrong, and that is why I do not believe we should proceed with this nomination at this time.”

#300. Syrian refugee elected mayor of German town, years after fleeing war

A Syrian who arrived in Germany as a refugee in 2015 has won a mayoral election in the southwestern German state of Baden-Württemberg.

Ryyan Alshebl, who left his hometown of As Suwayda in Syria eight years ago, ran as an independent in the municipality of Ostelsheim. He won 55.41% of the votes on Sunday, beating two German candidates, Marco Strauss and Mathias Fey.

Locals cheered the 29-year-old when he welcomed his win, a victory he described as “sensational,” German local broadcaster SWR reported Monday.

“Today, Ostelsheim sent an example for broad-mindedness and cosmopolitanism for the whole of Germany,” he said, according to to German public broadcaster ZDF. “That’s not something that can be taken for granted in a conservative, rural area.”

Alshebl’s first call after his victory was to his mother in Syria, who was thrilled with the news, SWR reported.

The Association of Municipalities of Baden-Württemberg said Alshebl is the first man with Syrian roots to run for and win a mayor’s office. He will start his role in June.

'You are not a refugee.' Roma refugees fleeing war in Ukraine say they are suffering discrimination and prejudice

Ostelsheim residents have welcomed their incoming mayor. “The fairy tale has come true, and the right man has become our mayor,” Annette Keck, who lives in the village, told SWR.

Strauss, one of his opponents, congratulated Alshebl. “I wish you good luck and at the same time ask for support for Mr. Alshebl, for our shared Ostelsheim,” he said on Facebook.

The state’s Integration Minister Manne Lucha said that Alshebl’s victory showed that diversity is a natural part of Baden-Württemberg. “I would be very pleased if Ryyan Alshebl’s election encourages more people with a migration history to run for political office,” he said.

Not everyone has been so warm to the 29-year-old. ZDF reported the Syrian received hateful comments on the campaign trail.

The young politician went from house to house, promoting his election program, and “the experiences were predominantly positive,” but there was also a minority of far-right fringe voters in Ostelsheim that did not want to accept him due to his Syrian roots, Alshebl told ZDF.

Born to a schoolteacher and agricultural engineer in Syria, Alshebl described his life as carefree until the age of 20, according to his campaign website.

At the time, protests against the Syrian government that began in 2011 soon devolved into chaotic war. The fighting and later rise of ISIS forced 10.6 million people from home by late 2015 – about half of Syria’s pre-war population.

Alshebl faced the dilemma of being drafted for military service with the Syrian army or leaving the country, according to his website.

While many Syrians were displaced internally or fled to countries in the region, others like Alshebl made the dangerous journey to Europe. He was 21 years old at the time, and said he crossed from Turkey to the Greek island of Lesbos in a rubber dinghy.

Former German Chancellor Angela Merkel had implemented a brief open-door policy in 2015 that saw the country take in about 1.2 million asylum seekers in the following years, including Alshebl.

They were welcomed into British homes. Celebrating their first Christmas together, Ukrainians wonder if that hospitality will last

The move sparked a backlash in Germany and the sudden growth of the far-right, anti-immigration Alternative for Germany (AfD) in the wake of summer 2015.

Once in Germany, Alshebl lived close to Ostelsheim and said at the time he felt “there is only one thing you can do: get back on your feet quickly and start investing in your own future quickly.”

For the last seven years he worked in the administration of Althengstett town hall, in a neighboring town. He drew from his experience, he said in his campaign, and made digital access to to public administration services one of priorities. Flexible childcare and climate protections are also on his agenda.

Alshebl, who is a member of the Green Party and now has German citizenship, pledged during his campaign that once elected as mayor he would move to Ostelsheim.

#298. NVIDIA shareholders are suing the company and CEO Jensen Huang

The company’s shares have plummeted in response to the exec’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The unfolding drama around NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang's affair with a woman now accused of being a spy for the Chinese government may be getting its first courtroom chapter. A shareholder in the computing giant has sued accusing NVIDIA and Huang of misleading investors about the nature of the CEO's relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at the Taiwanese company Megaspeed who is now suspected of being an espionage asset for China. Huang is a common surname; The pair are not related.

Mark Kistenmacher filed the complaint and is seeking class action status for anyone who purchased NVIDIA shares between October 9 and November 8. The first date is when The New York Times published a story on NVIDIA and Megaspeed detailing a connection between the pair, which was rebuffed by NVIDIA as a routine business relationship; the second date is when the NVIDIA founder admitted to an affair and the company publicly disclosed that it was launching its own investigation into whether its top exec had been targeted by a foreign intelligence operation.

In the interim, The New York Times published an in-depth feature on October 29 claiming that the NVIDIA CEO had become the subject of a suspected Chinese intelligence action. The piece cites NVIDIA representative John Rizzo, who said, "Jensen's interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year." This statement, the lawsuit alleges, was not true.

The company's shares have dropped in the fallout of the allegations, with NVIDIA on track to lose more than $470 billion in market value over the past two days. "As a result of Defendants' wrongful acts and omissions, and the precipitous decline in the market value of the Company's securities, Plaintiff and other Class members have suffered significant losses and damages," the complaint states.

#296. How to get this Siri camera upgrade for your iPad before its public release

You’ll be able to access Visual Intelligence from a new tab in the tablet's Camera app.

Apple users willing to install beta versions of its OS updates can get their hands on upcoming features sooner than most, as long as they're comfortable contending with the possibility of buggy software or apps breaking. And one such feature is the Siri Mode that's already available in the camera app on supported iPhones. It's also coming to compatible iPads as part of iPadOS 27.3 and will let you ask Siri AI questions about things you point your tablet's camera at.

We got our first look at this mode when Apple properly unveiled Siri AI at its Worldwide Developers Conference in June this year. It basically brings the company's Visual Intelligence tool for asking questions from photos to the camera app. But there's one distinguishing feature that's unique to iPads which isn't available on iPhones.

On iPads, Visual Intelligence includes Apple Pencil support. So, much like with Google's Circle to Search feature, you can snap a photo of something with your iPad, use your Apple Pencil to draw a circle around an item and learn more information about the object with the help of Siri AI.

It's worth noting that Visual Intelligence is already available on compatible tablets — iPad Pro and iPad Air with the M1 chip and newer, and iPad mini with the A17 Pro or A20 Pro chipset. As things stand, you can access it in the Camera app by long-pressing the Camera Control button or pressing the Action button if you've set it up to activate the feature (and your iPad has an Action button, of course).

In iPadOS 27.3, you'll have another way to access Visual Intelligence in the Camera app. All you'll have to do is swipe through the options in the viewfinder (i.e. photo, video and portrait modes) until you get to the Siri tab.

The company is expected to release the public version of iPadOS 27.3 in December. However, you can try it on your tablet right now, because Apple released the first beta in early November.

To install the iPadOS 27.3 beta, open the Settings app on your iPad, then tap General > Software Update. If you haven't already done so, you'll need to enable beta updates. Tap the Beta Updates option, then iPadOS 27 Public Beta. After you go back to the Software Update tab, you should be able to install the iPadOS 27.3 beta.

Before you install the beta, it's best to create a full backup of your iPad using iCloud, a Mac or a Windows PC. Beta builds aren't the finished product, meaning they're more susceptible to bugs and errors. Backing up your data should be standard practice anyway, but it's especially advisable to do that before installing a beta version of an operating system, just to make sure your files and other information stay safe.

If you don't feel comfortable enough to install a iPadOS beta, that's perfectly fine! The public version of the OS update is likely coming soon. You can check out the Siri tab on your iPad's Camera app at that point.

#294. Ring cameras go mobile as Amazon debuts AI-powered security drone and robot

Amazon’s roaming Ring Robotics devices will debut in late 2026 and early 2027.

The Ring Rover Cam and Ring Drone Cam are expected to reach customers starting at the end of 2026 and in early 2027, respectively, according to an Amazon announcement posted on November 10. Amazon describes the devices as relying on an AI Pilot system that uses each device’s cameras along with “specialized vision models, on-device sensors and cloud processing” to plan patrol routes while autonomously navigating around obstacles such as furniture.

Both devices also use that AI Pilot system to digitally map the floor plan of the customer’s home or business. This setup process occurs after the customer carries the Ring drone through their indoor spaces or allows the Ring Rover to do some autonomous exploration.

The AI Pilot can supposedly use that guided- or self-tour information to identify specific rooms and suggest patrol points near side doors, windows, or even sensitive locations such as a cash register in the case of a business.

Amazon’s Ring Drone appears to be a direct successor to the company’s Ring Always Home Cam that was only temporarily available for purchase by invitation as a “Day 1 Edition” product in September 2021—even having the same starting price of $250.

But the Ring Drone still requires authorization from the Federal Communications Commission before going on sale. Assuming that comes through, Amazon aims to make the drone available on January 27, 2027, with customers able to register for email updates through the product page.

Onboard camera capability includes a 148-degree field of view and 360-degree rotation with a 2K video resolution. Two LED lights also activate in low-light conditions to help illuminate the scene for the drone video camera.

However, the drone is limited to just 8 minutes of flight time when operating on a “new, fully-charged battery under controlled conditions” according to the product description. It comes with a docking station for charging.

Another caveat in the product description is that the Ring Drone Cam may experience degraded performance when navigating in environments with reflective surfaces such as “polished marble or wet tile,” blank matte walls without any features, or transparent surfaces like frameless glass and mirrors.

Still, Amazon touts the drone as providing an “autonomous way to check on your home that has never existed before,” whether the customer is away or at home. The Ring Drone can be controlled directly from the Alexa Home Map interactive map that shows all of a customer’s Amazon-linked devices.

Meanwhile, Amazon has pitched the Ring Rover Cam priced at $1,000 as “an autonomous security robot designed for business.” This product is already available for preorder for those so inclined and scheduled to start shipping to customers on December 16, 2026.

The company described the Ring Rover as being inspired by customer feedback involving another invite-only beta product, the Amazon Astro robot designed for home monitoring.

The Ring Rover can livestream and record in up to 4K video resolution when patrolling or while doing a 360-degree scan at a designated location. Videos triggered by doorbell rings, motion events and “Live View” events are stored in the cloud for sharing and replaying for up to 180 days.

Like the Ring Drone, the Ring Rover can automatically respond to alerts from connected Ring devices by following its preprogrammed patrol points. But the wheeled robot is only designed to operate within a single-floor, indoor environment because it lacks stair-climbing capability.

Amazon’s product description also warns that the Rover is not compatible with “curved edges, sunken areas, flooring transitions greater than 1.375 inches (3.5 centimeters), and black glossy floors.”

To help compensate for its ground-based view, the Ring Rover comes with an automatically adjustable mast to peer over counters, shelving and other obstacles. It can supposedly cover up to 5,000 square feet (465 square meters), according to Amazon’s product description.

The Ring Rover also comes with additional security-related features such as a siren and light tower to loudly announce its presence, along with a microphone for two-way audio communication if the customer wants to address someone near the robot.

Ring was previously charged in a 2023 Federal Trade Commission investigation with allowing employees and contractors to watch Ring video recordings of customers’ private spaces. The FTC also described Ring as having lackluster safeguards that allowed hackers to access 55,000 US customers’ accounts.

Videos taken by the new Ring Drone and Ring Rover will be protected with Ring’s Throw Away the Key Encryption that was introduced in September 2026, according to Amazon’s announcement. The digital rights nonprofit Electronic Frontier Foundation (EFF) has described this as an improvement over Ring’s former default settings, but still well short of end-to-end encryption.

There is currently no mention of the new Ring Robotics products implementing facial recognition or similar AI-powered technologies for identifying people. But starting in December 2025, Ring has allowed owners of fixed Ring doorbell cameras to turn on a “Familiar Faces” feature that uses facial recognition technology on visitors—and that is already the subject of a class action lawsuit.

#293. iFixit teardown shows how Apple's glowing PROLED MacBook logo works

The new laptop goes the extra mile to recreate the iconic feature.

iFixit has shared its teardown of Apple's first touchscreen MacBook Pro and besides bemoaning the company's half-hearted commitment to user repairability, it also showed off the physical components that make the laptop's glowing logo work. The new lid design is one of the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display's many charms, alongside its tandem OLED display and lighter weight.

Older LCD MacBooks featured a physical cutout that let the display's backlight illuminate the logo, but since OLED screens don't require backlights, Apple had to come up with a different solution to bring the design element back. iFixit found that the company uses a light strip with four LEDs layered behind the display, whose light is reflected through a diffuser to illuminate the logo. The Apple logo itself is also coated "like a two-way mirror," according to iFixit, so that it can appear metallic and reflective when the logo is off, but still let light through when the LEDs are on.

Apple ditched the glowing logo in 2016, so it's a bit ironic that the company waited until it stopped using backlit displays to bring it back. It used to be a cool bit of branding before, but now the company may see the glowing logo design as an important way to distinguish its most expensive laptop from the kind we peons are forced to use.

The rest of the internal changes to the laptop are more expected. Apple was able to achieve the new MacBook Pro's lighter weight by using less aluminum in the base and lid of the laptop and including a slightly smaller trackpad. The laptop's hinge was also reworked to make the display wobble less when you tap the screen. Those changes don't make it any easier to fix, though. The laptop is still complex to disassemble: For example, iFixit says that you have to remove the laptop's logic board to switch out its USB-C ports. Plenty of other components, like the MacBook's memory and the HDMI port, are also soldered in place. Anyone dropping up to $15,000 on a laptop probably isn't worried about doing their own repairs, but Apple's thin and light (and glowing) design comes with some tradeoffs, as always.

#292. These are the animals people most want to see photographed

Editor’s Note:  Call to Earth is a CNN editorial series committed to reporting on the environmental challenges facing our planet, together with the solutions. Rolex’s Perpetual Planet initiative has partnered with CNN to drive awareness and education around key sustainability issues and to inspire positive action.

Originally a term used by trophy hunters in Africa, the “Big Five” described the animals most challenging to shoot and kill: the lion, elephant, leopard, rhino, and buffalo. Nowadays, it is more loosely used to refer to some of Africa’s largest and most iconic animals.

However, British photographer Graeme Green has reclaimed the narrative, creating a global “New Big Five” for wildlife photography. In 2021, 50,000 people around the world voted for the five animals they would most like to photograph, or see photographed, in the wild. Five animals were crowned the winners: the elephant, polar bear, lion, gorilla and tiger.

This week, sees the publication of “The New Big 5” photography book, which features images of those animals and other at-risk wildlife, from photography legends such as Ami Vitale, Steve McCurry and Paul Nicklen, and essays from famous conservationists and activists including like Jane Goodall and Paula Kahumbu.

Green says that the book celebrates wildlife and is a global call to action on issues impacting wildlife, including habitat loss, poaching, pollution and climate change.

Green was on assignment in Botswana at least a decade ago when he came up with the idea for a project to encourage people to “shoot with a camera, not a gun,” he says.

“I thought this would be a way to get people really focusing on wildlife, thinking about the wildlife they love, thinking about the animals that are at risk.”

Ten photographs that made the world wake up to climate change

In total, the book includes the work of 144 globally renowned wildlife photographers from Ecuador to India. Curating the images took nearly two years of work, says Green.

“I think these are some of the most beautiful and creative images that I’ve seen put together in one book,” says Green. “These are the species that we are at risk of losing.”

According to the UN, nature is declining at an unprecedented rate, with around one million of the planet’s animal and plant species facing extinction. The “New Big 5,” all of which are threatened, act as ambassadors for what is happening in the natural world, says Green.

As well as being a powerful reminder of what we stand to lose, the book also points people towards potential solutions. Featured essays explore the benefits of rewilding and the importance of indigenous communities in conservation.

A chapter on endangered species from bees to blue whales illustrates the alarming threat climate change poses to animals outside of the “New Big 5.” “That’s only the tip of the iceberg – I could have included thousands of pictures because that’s how serious the situation is,” says Green.

Why a wildlife photographer took 40,000 photos to capture these extraordinary birds

Jane Goodall, a leading conservationist who also wrote the afterword to the book, said in a press release that “we have a window of time during which we can start to heal some of the harm we have inflicted on the natural world, but only if we get together and take action now.

“I hope the photos will lead people into the wonderful worlds of these iconic species. Then, perhaps, other people will become involved in helping to create a world where wildlife can flourish for future generations to enjoy,” she said.

“The New Big 5: A Global Photography Project For Endangered Wildlife,” by Graeme Green, published by Earth Aware Editions, is on sale from April 4, 2023.

This story has been updated to reflect the range of issues covered by the book.
