#290. The US military seems to be getting nervous about the launch supply crunch

“Launch is extremely constrained and that is exactly the situation we predicted.”

The US military is taking a new approach to prop up commercial launch companies as SpaceX looks to wind down its Falcon rocket program, suggesting defense officials see the workhorse rocket’s retirement as a potential national security risk.

The Pentagon’s Office of Strategic Capital has twice in the last two weeks announced it was sending cash to two of SpaceX’s competitors in the launch business. First, on October 26, the office announced a $150 million conditional loan commitment to Stoke Space. Military officials revealed a second $150 million Pentagon loan Tuesday for Rocket Lab.

Stoke Space and Rocket Lab are the first two launch companies to receive significant support from the Office of Strategic Capital (OSC), an organization established in 2022 to infuse companies developing “vital” national security technologies with capital. OSC has been on a loan spree in recent weeks, committing hundreds of millions of dollars to companies specializing in batteries, drones, and semiconductors. In the space sector, the office also provided a $150 million loan to LeoLabs, a commercial satellite tracking company, for production and deployment of radar units.

SpaceX, the Pentagon’s leading launch provider, has stopped taking commercial orders for Falcon 9 launches after 2028 as the company shifts its focus to the next-generation, super-heavy-lift Starship rocket. Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches for government customers, including NASA and the US Space Force, are expected to continue a while longer. The Space Force’s launch contract with SpaceX allows the military to place Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy orders through 2029, with the actual launches extending through 2033.

Then, if Starship is fulfilling its promise, SpaceX plans to retire Falcon for good. Starship may eventually be capable of launching national security payloads, but SpaceX’s near-term priority is using the rocket’s massive cargo capacity for its own Starlink Internet satellites and Starmind orbital data centers.

Military space officials have held to a policy of assured access to space since the 1990s, with the Pentagon paying to support at least two US rocket programs capable of launching national security satellites. The support previously came in the form of direct subsidies, development funding, and launch contracts at premium prices to give the government technical and financial insight into their launch providers, schedule priority on their launch manifests, and, in some cases, to simply keep them in business.

The Space Force’s mix of launch providers today consists of SpaceX’s Falcon family and United Launch Alliance’s Vulcan rocket, which has been grounded twice in the last two years by problems with its solid rocket boosters. ULA’s woes with Vulcan forced the Space Force to shift several national security missions to SpaceX.

Other launch providers aim to compete for their own slices of the growing pie of Space Force launch contracts. Space Systems Command, which oversees launch procurement for the Space Force, now forecasts the military will require up to 249 medium-to-heavy-lift launches through the early 2030s from its roster of launch contractors, more than double the command’s expectation just two years ago.

Alongside SpaceX and ULA, Space Systems Command has tapped Blue Origin, Rocket Lab, Stoke Space, Relativity Space, and Impulse Space for eligibility to compete for some of those national security launches. Blue Origin is the only company that has flown a rocket that could meet the Space Force’s requirements. Rocket Lab, Stoke Space, and Relativity Space are developing their own medium- and heavy-lift launch vehicles, while Impulse Space is working on in-space propulsion.

The loans from the Pentagon’s Office of Strategic Capital aim to get new medium-class rockets from Stoke Space and Rocket Lab ready to meet the military’s needs.

Stoke Space’s loan is tailored to support the buildout of the company’s second launch pad at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida. The second launch site would support flights of Stoke Space’s Nova Block 2 rocket, an upgraded version of the company’s fully reusable Nova Pathfinder, which is set to debut sometime next year.

“By supporting the construction of additional launch infrastructure, this commitment would enhance the overall capacity and resilience of the commercial space industrial base,” said Jordan Sebold, senior managing director of investments at OSC.

The loan to Rocket Lab will support the company’s efforts to increase the flight rate of its partially reusable Neutron rocket, which Rocket Lab says is also scheduled to make its first launch next year.

“The proposed loan would support faster vehicle delivery and processing and allow Rocket Lab to expand its fleet of maritime vessels with additional equipment for Neutron recovery and transport,” OSC said in a statement. “It would also enable manufacturing upgrades to increase the production of composite structures and engines, and support infrastructure that would reduce turnaround time between launches once the reusable rocket makes its operational debut.”

Both conditional loans are subject to “various financial, legal, technical, investment, due diligence, and other requirements,” OSC said.

Adam Spice, Rocket Lab’s chief financial officer, said Monday the Pentagon loan is on “very attractive terms” and “underscores the importance” of the military’s appetite for launch capacity.

“We always take it when we can get it,” Spice said in a quarterly earnings call. “It gives us additional capital to scale key parts of our business, Neutron in particular … I think this is a helpful indicator, a very positive indicator, about where everyone sees the demand constraint and how it can be unlocked, and capital is certainly a part of that.”

Rocket Lab also unveiled new pricing for the Neutron rocket—an increase from $55 million to $70 million per launch—in response to what the company said was a surge in bookings for the still-unproven launch vehicle. Rocket Lab said it now has a backlog of 117 launches on contract, including orders for Neutron missions and the company’s smaller Electron rocket.

“Launch is extremely constrained, and that is exactly the situation we predicted,” said Peter Beck, Rocket Lab’s founder and CEO.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#288. Why your Roku might be plugged into the wrong HDMI port

Your Roku might lose a few features if you plug it into any old HDMI port on your TV.

Depending on your smart TV, an add-on media streaming device isn't necessarily essential, but they can still provide a lot of value. Perhaps your TV's processor can't keep up with the latest apps and features, or you might prefer a specific ecosystem. That's what Apple is banking on to sell the 2026 Apple TV 4K, which is now an extremely pricey box at $299.

Roku streamers are far cheaper, but can still come with other concerns. You might be worried about your Roku monitoring what you watch, for example, or the implications of Fox's pending takeover. On a more mundane level, there's an issue applicable to any connected device: getting the best possible performance from your TV.

To do that, it's vital to you select the right HDMI port and cable. It's hard to go too far wrong with Roku devices, but a mistake could still mean getting inferior video or sound.

The reason it's hard to go wrong is that for the most part, Roku devices don't exploit any features unique to HDMI 2.1 or later, and all modern TVs support at least HDMI 2.0. Standards like 4K, HDR10+, Dolby Atmos and Dolby Vision can all work over 2.0, if not necessarily in their optimal form. Optimal would include 120Hz-plus refresh rates — but even Roku's top-of-the-line Ultra is capped at 60Hz, regardless of which port you plug it into.

That's acceptable, since 24fps is the most common playback speed for TV and movies while 30fps is used for news. 60fps content (equaling 60Hz) is common on YouTube, Twitch and many live TV streaming services; any modern Roku will support this. 120Hz would simply enable clearer motion, which is mostly relevant for gaming.

The Roku Ultra does support an HDMI 2.1 feature called Quick Media Switching (QMS). This allows refresh rates to shift up or down seamlessly, like if you're flipping from the 30fps evening news to the 24fps cinematic standard. Without QMS, you're going to run into temporary blackouts when a frame rate shift occurs.

In some cases, only certain HDMI ports on your TV will support HDR standards like Dolby Vision. Or if that barrier doesn't exist, you might have to go into your TV's input settings to flip a port over to the appropriate "enhanced" option. Ports are often set to reduced capabilities by default for the sake of compatibility. But since it's unlikely that you'd run into conflicts, you might as well enable modern functionality across the board. Check your manufacturer's website to figure out which ports support HDR and which menus you might need to navigate.

Another important point here is that you shouldn't plug a Roku into your TV's ARC or eARC port. While that technology is essential for some audio features like Dolby Atmos, labeled ports are intended for compatible output devices like soundbars, receivers and other speaker systems. If a TV supports ARC or eARC, it's automatically pulling audio from every HDMI 2.x input, regardless of how it's marked.

Along with the right ports, it's also important to choose a matching cable. If your Roku does support HDMI 2.1 features, a 2.0 cable will prevent them from working. If there's any doubt about what you have, HDMI 2.1 and even 2.2 (also known as Ultra96, which is not in wide use yet) cables are easy to find at low prices.

Computers and game consoles should always get priority for available HDMI 2.1 or 2.2 ports, since they can fully take advantage of high refresh rates and VRR (variable refresh rate). Streamers like Rokus are further back in the line, at least until manufacturers start taking advantage of everything newer TVs are capable of.

#285. Giving Marjorie Taylor Greene a platform isn't good for America

Editor’s Note:  Dean Obeidallah, a former attorney, is the host of SiriusXM radio’s daily program “The Dean Obeidallah Show.” Follow him @DeanObeidallah@masto.ai. The opinions expressed in this commentary are his own. View more opinion on CNN.

Last year, GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene of Georgia spoke at a white nationalist event organized by Holocaust denier Nick Fuentes that caused Republican leaders to denounce her.

Last week, Greene’s Twitter account was temporarily suspended by the Elon Musk-headed platform over a tweet with a graphic referring to a “Trans Day of Vengeance,” as she denounced a planned transgender rights rally.

And come Tuesday, Greene has announced plans to protest in New York City when former President Donald Trump is expected to be arraigned on an indictment of more than 30 counts, calling the proceedings against him an “unconstitutional WITCH HUNT!”

But on Sunday, Greene was featured on CBS’ “60 Minutes” in an interview the long-running show promoted on Twitter with the tease: “Republican Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, nicknamed MTG, isn’t afraid to share her opinions, no matter how intense and in-your-face they are. She sits down with Lesley Stahl this Sunday on 60 Minutes.” The images attached to this tweet by “60 Minutes” include Greene and Stahl walking through the US Capitol, taking a stroll outside and Greene showing Stahl something on her phone.

It's time for Marjorie Taylor Greene to go

In the segment that aired Sunday night, Stahl noted the congresswoman had moved from the fringe to the GOP’s front row in two years despite a “sharp tongue” and “some pretty radical views” as well as “over the top” comments such as “the Democrats are a party of pedophiles.” Stahl also referred to video of Greene chasing a Parkland, Florida, school shooting survivor, still maintaining that the 2020 election was stolen and failing to criticize Trump over spending. (The interview was conducted before news of his indictment.)

But Stahl didn’t mention Greene spoke at a white nationalist event a year ago while a member of Congress or her extreme anti-Muslim views and her defense of January 6 rioters.

Criticism of CBS for amplifying Greene has been swift and well-deserved even before the program aired. Former GOP Rep. Adam Kinzinger of Illinois tweeted beforehand: “Wow. Insane that 60 min would do this.” (Kinzinger is a CNN senior political commentator.)

Journalist Molly Jong-Fast also slammed “60 Minutes” with the tweet: “Attention is currency and 60 minutes is spending its currency on the Jewish space lasers woman.” (Jong-Fast was apparently referring to Greene’s past claim that a massive California wildfire was started by “a laser” beamed from space controlled by a prominent Jewish banking family.)

David Hogg, who survived the 2018 horrific school shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and has since become an activist against gun violence, responded, “I look forward to your questions about why she thinks school shootings are fake and why she’s supported QAnon.”

Greene amplified the conspiracy theory — two years before being elected to Congress — that the Parkland shooting that took 17 lives was staged. And in 2019, Greene appeared on video confronting Hogg outside the Capitol when he was championing laws to save lives from gun violence, screaming that the then-teenager was a “coward.” She also called Hogg “#littleHitler” on social media.

Greene’s own tweet promoting Sunday’s segment was not filled with her typical smears of “fake news” when she doesn’t like the coverage. Rather, Greene urged people to tune in as she praised “60 Minutes” host Stahl (and misspelled her first name): “It was an honor to spend a few days with the legendary icon Leslie Stahl and talented crew @60Minutes.” Greene added, “Leslie is a trailblazer for women in journalism. And while we may disagree on some issues, I respect her greatly.”

Now, it is true that “60 Minutes” over its 50-plus-year history has featured what the show has dubbed “controversial” guests. These include a 2000 interview with Oklahoma City bomber Timothy McVeigh, whose 1995 terrorist attack left 168 dead, including 19 children. And the show did a 1979 interview with Iran’s Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini.

Perhaps “60 Minutes” views Greene — who has repeatedly downplayed and even defended the January 6, 2021, attack plus called for the GOP to become the party of “Christian nationalism” — in the vein of its long list of controversial guests. (CBS had not responded to a request from CNN for comment at time of publishing.)

However, the choice of Greene as a guest instantly recalls the comments of then-CBS CEO Les Moonves during Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign when he admitted that Trump’s candidacy “may not be good for America, but it’s damn good for CBS.” Moonves added, “The money’s rolling in and this is fun. … (T)his is going to be a very good year for us,” concluding, “Sorry. It’s a terrible thing to say. But bring it on, Donald. Keep going.”

Moonves may be gone from CBS, but it appears his mindset continues at “60 Minutes.” And that may be good for “60 Minutes,” but it’s definitely not good for America.

#284. What is the average lifespan of recordable Blu-ray discs?

They can last you quite a while, but there are several factors to consider.

At this point, Blu-ray is a niche format, having mostly been supplanted by downloads, streaming and flash storage. But it's easily the format of choice for cinephiles, owing to higher ceilings on picture and audio quality. And if you want to archive files for a long time, Blu-ray may be more affordable, what with flash memory prices spiraling out of control.

How much can you trust recordable Blu-ray discs (BD-R), though? It's not an academic question if the files you're stashing are irreplaceable. If you make a bad decision, you could potentially lose your only digital copies of home videos, say, or a history of all your artistic projects.

Generally speaking, the news here is positive. BD-R may legitimately be your best option. There can be mitigating factors though, such as manufacturing quality, or simply having something to access the content on several years down the road.

Short of accidents, the main threat to Blu-ray discs is something called disc rot. This is actually an umbrella term for various forms of decay. The reflective layer can gradually become oxidized, for example. Moisture might attract mold, or cause the layers of a disc to separate. Then there's the matter of exposure to UV (ultraviolet) light, though that's more likely to cause labeling to fade than harm your data. You can potentially notice rot before popping in a disc — look for signs like discoloration or spots.

According to Canadian Conservation Institute data, the cheapest BD-R discs will last five to 10 years on average. These should be avoided for any kind of archival project, since an external SSD is liable to last at least that long, and possibly several times as much while storing a lot more data. Remember, even the biggest BD-R discs top out at 128GB.

That said, manufacturing quality can have a huge impact. In better BD-Rs, a dye recording layer is replaced by a silicon and copper alloy, which you can tell if a disc appears dark gray or grayish-brown. Likewise, these discs might switch to a gold metal layer. The result, the CCI says, is double the lifespan at 10 to 20 years.

It gets better. BD-RE (rewritable) discs can conceivably last you as much as 20 to 50 years. That's so long that you might be able to pass them down to your children or grandchildren. On those timescales, however, other factors should probably discourage you from relying on Blu-ray.

The most obvious reason is that discs are fragile. While storage in a relatively dry, dark and cool environment can deter rot, it's all too easy to accidentally crack or scratch a disc in a way that leaves it unreadable. Conversely, many external SSDs use tough metal enclosures that make physical destruction unlikely. All it might take to ruin your disc collection is forgetting that it's at the bottom of a moving box you're hauling, not the top.

Ultimately, the bigger issue might be industry support for Blu-ray. Optical drives have vanished as a default feature on computers, and companies are slowly pulling out the market for both external burners and BD-R discs, even Sony. At the moment, it's not that difficult to find burners and discs if you shop online. But there's no telling whether anyone will still be making this hardware 10 years down the road, much less 20. You could end up with an immaculately-preserved collection and no way of accessing it. Even your current drive might become useless if its software drivers cease to be compatible with later operating systems.

Whatever route you take, it's best not to be completely reliant on one storage medium for critical files. The general rule of thumb these days is to keep one copy in the cloud, and two others on separate forms of local storage. That's not always realistic — at the least, however, you should always be in a position to migrate your files to something fresher.

→ Baca artikel asli

#283. Seagate's new hybrid drive raises some old questions

The FireCuda Forge revisits an old idea, with some familiar questions.

Seagate doesn’t think hybrid drives are done just yet. On Tuesday, the company announced the launch of the new FireCuda Forge line of storage drives. The new drives combine the high capacity of a traditional plattered HDD with the speed and responsiveness of a user-supplied solid-state drive (SSD). The new hybrid storage solution is Seagate’s first to be powered and managed by the new Alkemi intelligent storage platform.

The FireCuda Forge is available right now through Seagate’s web store, and comes in 8TB ($699) or 20TB ($899) HDD configurations—neither of which includes the required SSD. Users will have to bring their own SSD, with some specific restrictions. The FireCuda Forge is compatible with M.2 2280 NVMe drives that are PCIe Gen3 or higher. The maximum supported NVMe capacity is 1TB, which means that’s as much data as you’ll be able to dedicate to storage on the “fast side” of the hybrid setup. The NVMe will require minimum speeds of 2,400MB/s read and 2,000MB/s write, and a unit with DRAM cache is recommended. Seagate claims 2,500MB/s read and 2,000MB/s write speeds for the combined system, along with USB4 and support for Windows 11 and macOS 26+.

Credit: Seagate Credit: Seagate

After dipping to historical lows earlier in the decade, storage pricing per TB has exploded amid the AI boom. According to data collected by Tom’s Hardware, some solid-state drives now cost two to three times their historic lows, and it’s not just SSDs. Mechanical drives haven’t been immune either. According to PCServerParts, a Seagate Exos 24TB drive that sold for $298.99 in the summer of 2025 was priced at $699.99 by August 2026.

Hybrid drives had largely fallen out of fashion since their heyday in the early to mid 2010s. The promise then was similar to what Seagate is offering now. A large HDD is combined with a smaller SSD and handed to the user as a single storage pool. Apple introduced its own hybrid solution back in 2012 with the Mac mini’s Fusion Drive. When Ars opened up a unit, the Fusion Drive system was found to write in 128KB tiered blocks. Information was generally written to the SSD first, and was then migrated to the HDD as needed. If the files were frequently used, they might find a more permanent home on the SSD side of things.

As solid-state storage continued getting cheaper, the hybrid approach was largely abandoned. Fast-forward to our current storage cost predicament in 2026, and Seagate’s line of thinking that everything old is new again starts to hold water. All storage costs have exploded, but the 2012 math still works today. NAND flash is still fast, but expensive, and traditional mechanical drives are at least comparatively cheaper per TB for higher capacity storage.

The problem with Apple’s Fusion solution was its fragility. If one of the two drives failed, that was likely it. Recovery depended on both drives coming together as a single volume. If one of those two disks experienced a physical failure, recovery became difficult since the remaining working drive depended on the now broken drive’s data to rebuild the full system picture.

According to Seagate’s press release, the Alkemi intelligent storage platform takes a different approach. Where Apple’s Fusion Drives used tiering managed by macOS, Alkemi manages the storage pool directly from the enclosure. Seagate says, “Alkemi learns and adapts over time to keep high-demand content fast and accessible on a user’s SSD, while the HDD provides space for growing libraries. Behind the scenes, Alkemi manages data placement, giving users the simplicity of a single storage drive.” The product data sheet PDF from the FireCuda Forge website describes the Alkemi system as a two-tier storage system, but doesn’t elaborate any further on whether the system uses SSD caching, automated tiering, a combination of the two, or something else entirely.

The announcement doesn’t address how the Alkemi platform manages the data between the two drives. The old Intel Smart Response Technology kept failure risks low by mirroring data from the mechanical HDD to the faster SSD, with the HDD retaining the authoritative copy. The optional write-back mode could still hold new data on the SSD, though. Apple’s Fusion Drive instead tiered data in 128KB blocks across the storage pool, storing data on both drives. The distinction matters, and is probably why Ars asked the same question after Apple’s hybrid announcement 14 years ago.

Seagate’s announcement speaks of “intelligent data tiering” without further explanation. The data system promises that your most accessed files will be quickest to access via intelligent storage management, but stops short of explaining whether one drive going down means failure for the whole system. There’s also no mention of how the Alkemi system distributes data on first boot. Does all the new written data go directly to the SSD until the 1TB cap is crossed, and then the system starts determining which files are important enough to remain on the speedier drive? Seagate seems to be keeping things mum for the moment, but as the drives have already launched for sale, there should be answers to all of these questions coming soon.

Seagate hasn’t published technical documentation regarding how the Alkemi system handles drive failure or repair. Until it does, the new FireCuda Forge drives offer the same appeal as Apple’s old Fusion Drives: SSD speed with HDD capacity, along with the same questions Ars was asking about hybrid storage years ago.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#275. Adobe brings AI agents and new creative tools to Photoshop, Premiere, and Firefly

Adobe is pouring more AI into its products, including Photoshop, Premiere, Illustrator, and InDesign, in a major product feature announcement at its MAX event in Miami. The company is also expanding AI capabilities in its creative tool, Firefly, and releasing a Model Context Protocol (MCP) that lets other chatbots like ChatGPT, Claude, or Gemini use Adobe features.

Adobe’s Firefly app is moving into Figma’s territory with a new prototyping tool in private beta called Builder, which lets users create designs to show off ideas. There is also a new tool to create on-brand graphs, along with an image-editing tool that lets you change part of an image without changing other objects.

A new AI-powered video tool in Firefly will allow users to assemble a video from clips and other elements like images and sound effects. The company is making its agent proactive and always on so that it can get more context about work and be present for any action. Adobe is also making it easier for people to take quick action, like removing the background or generating a thumbnail for a video.

The company has already added a ton of AI features across its apps like Photoshop, Express, Premiere, and Illustrator. At the MAX event, Adobe is continuing the trend. Photoshop is getting a new relighting feature and sliders for prompt-based generative styles; Premiere will have a new color grading system for professionals and an object removal feature for video; and Lightroom has a way to correct blinking eyes and a blemish removal tool.

Adobe already offers a lot of third-party models from companies like Google, Runway, ElevenLabs, Black Forest Labs, OpenAI, Kling, and ByteDance. One of its newer acquisitions, Topaz Labs, is releasing two image models, called Wonder 4 for upscaling and denoising, and ImageHDR, to turn standard images into HDR.

The company already has ChatGPT and Claude integrations to let users access some of its product functions. Now, Adobe is releasing a local MCP to expose more features of its Creative Cloud apps to users.

Adobe vice president Deepa Subramaniam told TechCrunch that the company is still figuring out what capabilities to expose and will rely on user feedback.

“We are still figuring out where and when and how to draw the line. What we’re putting out there is a number of endpoints and capabilities across Illustrator, Photoshop, After Effects, and Premiere. We’re just going to see how people use it, what they’re trying to do, and keep showing more. We don’t have any need or desire to sort of limit what the MCP endpoint can expose. What we really want to do is to support that creative workflow, how you choose to do it,” said Subramaniam.

Last month, Adobe rival Figma co-founder Dylan Field said at an event in India that MCP is not a perfect standard and benefits frontier labs more.

#273. New WhatsApp head wants to make the app easier to use, give its AI more chops

WhatsApp has grown into one of the most popular messaging apps in the world, used by over 3 billion people. But with that growth, the app has also become complex, packing in features like communities, channels, stories and business features, and a lot of people find it hard to navigate.

Kunal Shah, who took over leadership of the app’s development from Will Cathcart earlier this year, now wants to make the app and actions within it simpler to use. In a new note shared on Tuesday, Shah said the company wants to make voice notes, groups, stickers, and video “simpler and more expressive.”

WhatsApp has long also tried to develop an ecosystem that lets users explore products and make purchases, and Shah’s note indicates more of that is coming, hinting at partnerships with payment companies and telecom providers to enable bill payment, loans, and eSIM purchases.

Shah also said the company wants to use AI to surface important information to users.

“It should quickly find what you need, get things done, and make sure you never miss a booking or an appointment. The best technology saves you time, so you can get back to the connections that matter,” he wrote.

WhatsApp currently offers an AI assistant that can summarize messages and create drafts. It’s not clear if the company will use its Muse agent to collate information and send reminders to people.

I think of WhatsApp like a shared language, spoken by 3bn+ people. Sometimes we use words, and sometimes it’s a voice note to a parent, a sticker that ends an argument, or a “home safely” at 2am. Language is at the heart of everything we as people do. We write letters, tell stories, send hard messages and make plans for the future. A WhatsApp chat is often where we start.

Like any language, the people who use it shape it. They don’t wait for permission. They find new things to say, and new ways to say them. Often, our users show us what should come next based on how they’re using the app and communicating in the world.

I’ve spent the last few months listening and understanding what our users need and want from us and, and what I heard is simple: Help people stay close to those who matter to them, and get more done on WhatsApp.

First, we want to make connections richer.  Sometimes hearing someone’s voice, sending a joke, agreeing on a plan or knowing they’re safe is the whole point. People send over 10 billion voice messages and over 4 billion stickers on WhatsApp every day. As the way people communicate evolves, we will evolve these experiences with them. We’ll make voice notes, groups, stickers and video simpler and more expressive, because how people feel close keeps changing.

Second, we want anything you do to be as simple as sending a message. Too many things start in a chat and end with many more steps somewhere else: paying a bill, borrowing money, staying connected when you travel. Each handoff to another task costs time. We’re working with financial services and telecom partners so that finishing something feels as easy as starting it.

Third, we want to make sure AI makes it all more efficient. It should quietly find what you need, get things done and make sure you never miss a booking or an appointment. The best technology saves you time, so you can get back to the connections that matter.

None of this matters without trust. Trust takes years to earn and a moment to lose, and I don’t take it lightly. Whatever we build, WhatsApp must remain simple, reliable and private. These principles we’ve had for years aren’t changing. Your personal messages and calls stay end-to-end encrypted. That isn’t changing, and neither is our quality bar.

What happens inside WhatsApp only matters for what it makes possible outside it: more time together, stronger communities, and fewer barriers between people and the lives they want to live.

#272. What OPEC's surprise oil cut means for gas prices

OPEC and its allies’ surprise move to slash oil production will soon be felt at US gas pumps.

The group known as OPEC+ announced Sunday it would cut oil production by more than 1.6 million barrels a day starting in May, running through the end of the year. The news sent both Brent crude futures, the global oil benchmark, and WTI, the US benchmark, up about 6% in trading Monday.

The production cut announcement also had an immediate impact on gasoline futures, which will be passed onto US drivers much more quickly than the spike in oil prices. RBOB, the most closely watched wholesale gasoline price, was up about 8 cents a gallon, or about 3%, in morning trading.

“I think OPEC is reawakening the inflation monster,” said Tom Kloza, global head of energy analysis for OPIS, which tracks gas prices for AAA. “The White House has to be shocked and major-time pissed. It certainly alters the calculus for a while.”

The national average for US gas prices stood at $3.51, on Monday, according to AAA. Kloza said he could see it getting up to $3.80 to $3.90 in relatively short order thanks to the move by OPEC.

“We’re not going to get back to $5 a gallon. I don’t think we’re even going as high as $4,” he said. But he said by the end of the summer US drivers could be back above year-earlier prices, especially if there is a hurricane or other storms affecting production along the Gulf Coast.

The average US regular gas price a year ago stood at $4.19 a gallon in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and the disruption that caused to world’s energy markets. Prices eventually reached a record $5.02 a gallon on June 14, before starting a slow but steady decline over the course of more than three months during which the average price fell every day. The decline was partly driven by the release of oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, and partly by concerns that there could be a US or global recession that reduced the demand for gasoline.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Even at $3.51, US gas prices were just below the $3.53 average on Feb. 23, 2022, the day before Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Kloza said one thing keeping prices from getting anywhere near the record levels of 2022 is that the US plans additional releases from the SPR, and US oil production and refining capacity are both up. But a cut of 1 million barrels a day of oil by OPEC+ will not be easy to make up.

“They have ability to cut production and they seem motivated to do so,” he said.

#269. Google’s Pixel Tag tracker is now available

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Pixel Tag launches today for $30 each or discounted to $100 for a four-pack.

The Pixel Tag launches today for $30 each or discounted to $100 for a four-pack.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After years of third-party Android trackers from companies like Samsung, Motorola, and Pebblebee, Google’s Pixel Tag is now available for purchase. First announced in August, the Pixel Tag is designed to work with Google’s tracking network and app that was renamed the Find Hub last year. The Pixel Tag is priced similar to Apple’s AirTag 2. A single tracker costs $29.99 while a four-pack is discounted to $99.99.

At 0.4 ounces, the Pixel Tag is slightly lighter than the AirTag 2 and while its oblong design is longer than Apple’s tracker, Google’s is a bit thinner. It doesn’t include a hole to attach it to keys, luggage, or other items you want to track, but you can pair it with accessories that make it easy to tether to objects. Unlike Apple’s AirTags, the Pixel Tag has a button that can be pressed to trigger a sound should your phone get misplaced instead of the tracker.

The Pixel Tag is slightly thinner than Apple’s AirTag but has an oblong shape.

Google says the Pixel Tag will run for over a year on a single CR2032 coin battery, and it has an IP67 rating so it’s dustproof and can survive a shallow dunking. The tracker also features ultra wideband (UWB) support and Channel Sounding through Bluetooth 6.0. Android phones with a UWB chip can display the exact distance and direction to the Pixel Tag in the Find Hub app once you’re close to it, while Bluetooth Channel Sounding is limited to just distance.

The Pixel Tag is compatible with smartphones running Android 9 or later, but you’ll need devices that specifically include a UWB chip or support Bluetooth 6.0 to take advantage of Find Hub’s precise locating features.

#267. Reddit is turning user conversations into AI-powered product reviews

Shortly after announcing a plan to transition its site to a logged-in experience, Reddit is debuting a feature that could help make it more of a destination for people doing product research via the web. The company on Tuesday introduced “Reddit Reviews,” a feature that organizes conversations about products into dedicated reviews that include the highlights and key takeaways from users across its communities.

The company notes that the new experience builds on one of the ways people are already using Reddit: to learn from others what products are really like, and how they fit into their everyday lives. These first-hand accounts and recommendations can help consumers determine whether or not a particular product is right for them.

However, given the rise of generative AI and bots, it’s an area that’s also ripe for “astroturfing” — a term that refers to when a brand fakes public support. With Reddit now effectively turning what was casual online research into more of a structured product, it could become a larger target for these types of fake reviews and recommendations than before.

To address this, Reddit’s announcement points to its rules and its latest measures to combat automated abuse and spam, as well as its practice involving community moderators, as tools it will use to keep the reviews on the site legit.

The Reddit Reviews feature itself taps into AI to organize the conversations people are having where they describe their product experiences. These posts will often see users discussing how a product works for them, or why it fails, or what the trade-offs are that someone should consider before buying.

The company has been testing the product reviews feature since February, initially with U.S. users in areas like Electronics, Video Games, and Health & Beauty products. In September, Reddit expanded the test to include the Home & Garden and Fashion & Apparel categories as well.

Now, the company says Reddit Reviews is rolling out to all U.S. users on iOS, Android, and web, with an initial selection focused on around 10,000 products. Over time, it will roll out to more categories and expand beyond the U.S.

To use the feature, Reddit users can search for a product or category in the app’s search bar or click on a highlighted product name they see in someone’s comment. From there, the user is taken to a screen where important conversations are summarized under a “what people are saying” heading. This will also include a visual breakdown that shows how many of those comments are positive, negative, or mixed, and what sort of things people said they liked or disliked about the item.

For instance, a user considering buying an espresso machine might want to know about things like how much counter space it takes up or whether it’s easier for beginners to understand how to use it, says Reddit. Users can then browse the comments, filter them by topic, or click to be taken to the original conversation, where they can ask more questions or get advice.

Users can also move from one product review back to the full category to weigh other, similar products that have been discussed to compare their options, the company said.

The feature arrives just ahead of the busy holiday shopping season, when people spend a lot more time doing online product research ahead of their bigger purchases.

#265. The US is winning the AI race. Jensen Huang could have blown it

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nvidia’s chips are powering America’s AI boom. Of course Chinese spies are interested.

Nvidia’s chips are powering America’s AI boom. Of course Chinese spies are interested.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

As espionage targets go, Jensen Huang is perhaps one of the least surprising on Earth. The Nvidia CEO runs one of the world’s most valuable companies, controls the in-demand technology fueling the AI boom, and is cozy with presidents, CEOs, and all manner of important people. He also wears a cool leather jacket.

Which makes Huang’s admission that he had an affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy — reportedly while her spying was known to US intelligence and even President Donald Trump — all the more shocking. The fallout goes well beyond his marriage, his reputation, or Nvidia itself. Experts tell The Verge the incident threatens to disrupt the entire US AI industry, potentially jeopardize national security, and could undermine America’s efforts to maintain its fragile technological lead over China in AI.

“With those chips, the US lead could vanish in a matter of weeks or months.”

Nvidia is the undisputed leader in advanced computer chips, among the AI industry’s scarcest resources. While many ingredients go into building AI models — including talent, energy, capital, and data — top-tier chips are one of the most important, and Nvidia is one of a handful of companies with the expertise to design or manufacture them at the cutting edge. That makes it a gatekeeper to hardware that has become a key bottleneck in global AI development. US companies — including Meta, Anthropic, and OpenAI — are racing to develop chips of their own, in part to reduce their dependence on Nvidia, even as they spend billions securing ever more compute.

For China, the US’s main rival at the frontier of AI, that bottleneck is even tighter. To maintain its technological lead, the US has restricted the sale of the most advanced AI chips to the country in the hopes of slowing its progress in the sector, which has been impressive and is thought to trail the US by a matter of months. In response, China has made developing domestic alternatives a strategic national priority, though its leaders still lag Nvidia at the bleeding edge. At the same time, a thriving black market for restricted Nvidia chips has emerged.

Experts tell The Verge that China could exploit access to Nvidia to accelerate its own development, or to impede the US development of AI.

Getting its hands on more Nvidia chips is the most obvious win for Beijing, says Emily Kilcrease, a senior fellow and director of the energy, economics, and security program at the Center for a New American Security (CNAS) think tank. China has advantages in other parts of the AI race — including abundant energy and a solid ecosystem of open weight models — but without the latest Nvidia chips, it “cannot close the gap with US frontier capabilities,” Kilcrease tells The Verge. “With those chips, the US lead could vanish in a matter of weeks or months.”

It is not clear how successful the alleged spying operation has been on that front. There is no public evidence indicating that Huang himself has helped direct restricted Nvidia chips toward China or that sensitive information about them was obtained from him. But companies linked to the alleged spy, Alice Huang — no relation to Jensen — have reportedly enabled at least $7 billion of Nvidia chip sales to China.

Brookings fellow Kyle Chan tells The Verge that a concern for US industry could be that companies with alleged ties to China may have had priority access to Nvidia’s latest GPUs. “In a time of chip scarcity,” that would make it much harder for American companies to access them, Chan says.

Huang is also a prominent critic of American restrictions on chip sales, and Nvidia spends a formidable amount lobbying in Washington. Last year, he successfully secured White House approval to start exporting Nvidia’s H200 AI GPU chips to “approved customers” in China and other countries. Crucially, Huang also has the ear of President Trump as well as other world leaders, giving him great influence over policy decisions.

Hardware is not the only thing of value China could get from close access to Huang, Sunny Cheung, a fellow for China studies at the Jamestown Foundation think tank, tells The Verge. “I actually think too many people are focused on whether somebody managed to steal Nvidia’s trade secrets,” Cheung says, calling it a “very narrow way” of looking at espionage. “I would hope Jensen Huang isn’t foolish enough to hand over the blueprints for Nvidia’s next-generation chips to a suspected Chinese intelligence operative.”

The Nvidia CEO’s relationships with other tech executives, senior policymakers, and Trump himself give him an unusually intimate view into how some of the most powerful people in the US are thinking and planning. Beijing could use information on how American companies are planning major infrastructure expansions, their compute requirements, or broad industry trends to inform its own investments and industrial strategy, Cheung says.

Huang’s intelligence value could extend well beyond AI, too. “That information alone could be extremely valuable,” says Cheung, pointing to insights Beijing could glean into White House thinking about export controls or the private views of Silicon Valley executives on China and Taiwan.

“When so much of America’s AI infrastructure depends on one company, any significant security or governance problem at Nvidia can create uncertainty across the industry.”

But helping China advance is only one side of this equation. Disrupting Nvidia — as this scandal clearly has — could destabilize America’s entire AI industry. More than most companies, Nvidia is remarkably closely tied to the identity and reputation of its rockstar CEO. Huang cofounded the company more than three decades ago, and ever since then he has served as its president, chief executive, and a member of the board of directors. Despite holding a relatively small stake in the company, in many ways Nvidia is Jensen Huang. Nvidia shares have slumped since news of the affair broke.

“When so much of America’s AI infrastructure depends on one company, any significant security or governance problem at Nvidia can create uncertainty across the industry,” Cheung says.

Kilcrease echoes those concerns, warning that the scandal could “destabilize” the company. At the very least, she says the various investigations already underway “will consume an immense amount of bandwidth within the company,” creating a very real risk of weighing down Nvidia’s day-to-day operations. If those distractions begin to affect Nvidia’s ability to execute — slowing decisions, product rollouts, or the allocation of already scarce chips — the consequences could quickly reach customers. The AI labs and hyperscalers such as OpenAI and Anthropic “could have a real issue accessing the vast amounts of compute they need,” Kilcrease says, adding that switching to other hardware “takes time.”

In a race to build increasingly powerful AI, time is a precious resource. Experts say China trails the US closely, meaning that even modest delays for American companies can help Chinese rivals catch up. Nvidia is almost certain to face further disruption as investigations continue, though the extent to which the turmoil will roil the rest of the industry may be difficult to measure.

For China, however, the scandal could be about to backfire spectacularly now everything is out in the open. “The odds of AI chip control legislation being passed in Congress just went through the roof,” Kilcrease says.

Correction, Tuesday November 10th: A sentence in this article misstated that Anthropic is the leader in advanced computer chips. It is Nvidia.

#264. Meta’s AI agent Muse lands on Apple Watch and Android Wear OS

Meta is bringing its Muse AI agent to the Apple Watch and Android’s Wear OS, the company announced on Monday. With the launch, users can now interact with Meta’s AI assistant through voice calls, text messages, or dictation directly on their smartwatch.

Works around the clock, and now on your wrist. Muse is now on Apple Watch and Wear OS ⌚️ pic.twitter.com/97QRap5XKz

The smartwatch launch comes just a month after Meta introduced a dedicated iPad app for Muse. First launched on the App Store in September, the AI assistant’s expansion across Apple devices signals Meta’s growing ambitions for its consumer AI product and its focus on making Muse a central part of its ecosystem.

Muse is now one of dozens of consumer-facing AI agents competing for users’ attention. Like many of its competitors, it allows users to connect their accounts to manage email, meetings, bills, and more, while also handling tasks such as booking reservations, ordering groceries, setting goals, and making purchases.

Users can also personalize their Muse avatar to their liking and change it into something that resembles a person, an animal, or another type of character.

Despite being a relatively new product, Meta has wasted little time introducing new capabilities to Muse.

A few days ago, Meta launched Spaces, a new way to communicate and collaborate with Muse. Spaces function as dedicated conversations around specific topics, which Meta describes as “custom experiences” as they’re mini apps that are built on the fly. They appear in the sidebar of the Muse interface and bring up a focused conversation where the AI agent works toward a particular goal on the user’s behalf.

Last week, Meta rolled out the ability for users in the U.S. to video chat with their Muse AI avatar. Meta said Muse will use “subtle expressions and gestures” that reflect the tone and context of the conversation as it speaks. It’s worth noting that Muse doesn’t actually see users, but can hear them and respond in real time.

Shortly after its launch, Muse became one of just 12 mobile apps to have surpassed 10 million U.S. downloads within their first 40 days in the App Store. Only 11 other apps have achieved this milestone, with Meta’s Threads being the most recent in July 2023.

#263. Scientists film deepest ever fish on seabed off Japan

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Cruising at a depth of 8,336 meters (over 27,000 feet) just above the seabed, a young snailfish has become the deepest fish ever filmed by scientists during a probe into the abyss of the northern Pacific Ocean.

Scientists from University of Western Australia and Tokyo University of Marine Science and Technology released footage of the snailfish on Sunday filmed last September by sea robots in deep trenches off Japan.

Along with the filming the deepest snailfish, the scientists physically caught two other specimens at 8,022 meters and set another record for the deepest catch.

Previously, the deepest snailfish ever spotted was at 7,703 meters in 2008, while scientists had never been able to collect fish from anywhere below 8,000 meters.

“What is significant is that it shows how far a particular type of fish will descend in the ocean,” said marine biologist Alan Jamieson, founder of the Minderoo-UWA Deep Sea Research Centre, who led the expedition.

Scientists are filming in the trenches off Japan as part of a 10-year study into the deepest fish populations in the world. Snailfish are members of Liparidae family, and while most snailfish live in shallow water, others survive at some of the greatest depths ever recorded, Jamieson said.

During the two-month survey last year, three “landers” – automatic sea robots fitted with high-resolution cameras – were dropped into three trenches – the Japan, Izu-Ogasawara and Ryukyu trenches – at varying depths.

In the Izu-Ogasawara trench, footage showed the deepest snailfish hovering calmly alongside other crustaceans on the seabed.

Jamieson classified the fish as a juvenile and said younger deep sea snailfish often stay as deep as possible to avoid being eaten by bigger predators that swim at shallower depths.

Another clip shot at between 7,500 and 8,200 meters in the same trench showed a colony of fish and crustaceans munching at bait tied to an undersea robot.

Images of the two captured snailfish – identified as Pseudoliparis belyaevi – provide a rare glimpse of the unique features that help the deep sea species survive the extreme environment.

They have tiny eyes, a translucent body, and their lack of swim bladder, which helps other fish float, works to their advantage, Jamieson said.

The professor said the Pacific Ocean is particularly conducive to vibrant activity due to its warm southern current, which encourages sea creatures to go deeper, while its abundant marine life provides a good source of food for bottom feeders.

Scientists would like to know more about creatures living at extreme depths, but cost is the constraint, Jamieson said, adding that each lander alone costs them $200,000 to assemble and operate.

“The challenges are that technology has been expensive and scientists don’t have a lot of money,” he said.

#261. N-able takes its remote access service offline after researcher publishes dozens of zero-day bugs

Remote access company N-able has confirmed it took down its cloud-hosted offering to fix a slew of security vulnerabilities that were published online this week, giving the company no advance notice to fix them.

In a status update on its website, the company said its hosted N-Central offering for customers is now offline “while we take the necessary actions to secure the environments and implement appropriate remediation.” The company also urged customers to take down their own self-hosted deployments of N-Central, fearing that attackers could use the publicly disclosed exploits to hack into customer networks.

N-able said during this time customers will not be able to use their N-Central service, which allows corporate customers to remotely access and manage other devices and systems on the same network. Remote access technologies are a frequent target of hackers, as these systems can grant vast access to a company’s network and internal data.

Security researcher Ben Bales released the “zero-day” bugs on GitHub on Sunday. In a post on X, Bales dubbed the bugs Able2Shell, describing them as a mix of vulnerabilities capable of stealing credentials, creating new administrative accounts, and gaining root access to affected N-able systems.

Bales told Bleeping Computer that he publicly released the bugs as the company allegedly failed to patch the flaws reported in a different product.

N-able said that it has no evidence of successful exploitation against any of its customer environments. A spokesperson for the company did not respond to a request for comment about whether it has the ability to check, such as server logs, or if it has no way to check.

#260. Starlink Mini 2 adds a swappable battery

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The all-in-one satellite dish can now operate for over four hours without an external power source.﻿

The all-in-one satellite dish can now operate for over four hours without an external power source.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

SpaceX announced a new version of its all-in-one satellite internet terminal that integrates a dish, Wi-Fi router, and now a battery into a single device about the size of a laptop. According to some users on X who shared screenshots of invitations to order Starlink Mini 2, the dish is priced at $999, which includes a full year of Roam Unlimited service that SpaceX values at $2,100.

The Starlink Mini 2 will run for up to 4.5 hours on a full charge, SpaceX claims, but that can be expanded by hot-swapping in additional batteries that will be sold separately, or by connecting the dish to a 60W power source over USB-C.

Despite the addition of an integrated battery, the Starlink Mini 2 dish is thinner than the original Mini. SpaceX has managed to slim it down to 23.1mm thick, while the previous version of the Mini was 38.5mm. The Starlink Mini 2 is slightly heavier at around 3.5 pounds as a result of the added battery weight, but carries forward an IP67 dust- and waterproof rating so it’s still safe to use outdoors.

Preorders are now open “by invitation for select US customers,” according to the Starlink website, but “coming soon” to more customers and countries.

#257. Google broadens its AI hardware portfolio with two new specialized chips

Google is notching up its chipmaking game as the company seeks to reduce its dependence on chips made by other companies for its cloud computing business. Google on Monday unveiled two new variants of its tensor processing units (TPUs), in-house-developed chips meant for AI workloads, that broaden its chip portfolio to additional use cases: confidential inference and more memory capacity for large workloads.

The new units, TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max, are part of the company’s eighth generation of chips introduced in April this year, and build on what those chips offered, which was more inference capacity and support for large training requirements.

TPU 8tx, designed with AMD, is based on the TPU 8t chip, and is geared specifically for confidentially training and running AI models, Google said in a blog post. The chip “builds security directly into the hardware layer, keeping sensitive data and model weights protected and isolated even while in active use,” the company wrote.

The chip also has a custom inferencing network that lets it run reasoning alongside training, so it can be used to distill and train small models on the fly. That ability, according to Google, could prove useful for cases where a company might want to train a model on its proprietary data without having to move the data between devices.

Meanwhile, TPU 8i Max basically combines two of the inference-focused TPU 8i chips the company launched in April, doubling the memory capacity, processing power and bandwidth, but in a single package. The result is a chip that lets AI models handle a larger range of information during queries.

The chip launch comes as hyperscalers like Microsoft, Amazon, and even AI labs like OpenAI and Anthropic are working on their own lines of AI-specific semiconductors and accompanying software in order to reduce their reliance on the dominance of Nvidia.

The news also comes at a particularly vulnerable moment for Nvidia, whose CEO Jensen Huang over the weekend admitted to having an affair with someone alleged to be a Chinese spy.

#254. Apple TV makes sign language interpretation a built-in accessibility feature

Apple TV is making its content more accessible to Deaf audiences with the launch of a new Sign Language Interpretation feature, the company announced on Tuesday. The feature will initially be available across a limited set of titles, starting this Friday with the release of “Being Heumann,” a biopic about disability rights activist Judy Heumann.

American Sign Language (ASL) and British Sign Language (BSL) will also be made available for Apple’s hit movie “F1.” Later this year, other titles will follow, including “Way of the Warrior Kid” and “Greyhound.”

What makes Apple’s offering different from other platforms is that the sign language version of the film is not a separate product — that is, it’s not a separate film designed just for Deaf viewers. Instead, Apple’s support for sign language is just a setting you enable that’s integrated with the main title, similar to how you could turn on or off closed captions.

When a title with the sign language option is playing, an interpreter appears in the bottom right of the screen, translating the spoken words into signs. Apple says it has partnered with a group of DASLs (Directors of Artistic Sign Language) to ensure that the interpreter for each film fits the tone of the film. Notably, the company is not using any AI-generated interpreters, as Apple says that’s not what the Deaf community has said it needs.

The feature is shipping in the latest release of iOS 27.2, and can be turned on through a new sign language option in Settings. After being enabled, a new button will appear in the supported Apple Original titles on Apple TV indicating that sign language is available.

These titles are labeled with a sign language icon that appears on the title page alongside other features like closed captions or audio descriptions. There’s also a new section under languages that will list the specific sign languages that are available with the given title.

To make the titles more discoverable, Apple TV will highlight which titles include the sign language option in a “Movies & Shows with American Sign Language” editorial collection. Meanwhile, users can easily surface supported titles via search by typing in keywords like “with ASL,” for example.

The new feature builds on Apple’s other investments in accessibility features, which it builds itself.

“At Apple, we believe everyone should be able to enjoy great stories and entertainment,” said Sarah Herrlinger, Apple’s senior director of Global Accessibility Policy and Initiatives, in a statement about the launch. “Bringing sign language interpretation to Apple TV is an exciting step forward in making the viewing experience more accessible and inclusive for Deaf audiences. Accessibility is a core value at Apple, and we’re proud to continue creating experiences that allow more people to enjoy the content they love,” she added.

#253. What pet cemeteries reveal about being human

Editor’s Note:  Eric Tourigny is a lecturer in historical archaeology at Newcastle University, UK. His research interprets osteological and material culture remains alongside historic texts to examine changing human-animal relationships in Europe and North America over the past 500 years. The views expressed in this commentary are his own. Read more opinion on CNN.

Why do we bury our departed loved ones in a cemetery? A primary purpose is to provide survivors with an opportunity to grieve and gain a sense of closure. The cemetery is a place for the living as much as it is a place for the dead.

But what about when the dearly departed are not human – but our pets?

The Spanish city of Barcelona recently announced that it will be investing in the establishment of the country’s first public pet cemetery. Set to open next year, it will offer both burials and cremations – with an estimated 7,000 carried out each year.

For me, as someone who has spent years researching the development of pet cemeteries elsewhere in the world, this news came as a shock. Barcelona is a densely populated city with limited privately owned land – one where 50% of families own a pet.

How did a city that is home to 180,000 dogs not already have a public pet cemetery? Until now, the service was only provided by the private sector, according to Barcelona’s Councilor for Climate Emergency and Ecological Transition, Eloi Badia. He added that the municipal-funded initiative was sparked by “constant public demand.”

After all, public pet cemeteries have been around in Europe and the Americas since the late 19th century. Britain’s first public pet cemetery appeared in London’s Hyde Park in 1881. New York’s Hartsdale pet cemetery was founded in 1896, followed a few years later by Paris’ ornate Cimetière des Chiens in 1899.

I became interested in the history of modern pet burial practices while investigating the archaeological record of a centuries-old house in Toronto. I came across a (very) large dog buried in the back garden which, according to the historical record, was occupied between 1840 and 1870.

This dog survived to an elderly age but, sadly, suffered from degenerative joint disease and severe infections during his last months. His ailments progressed to such a state that suggest he received some level of care in his final weeks. He then was buried in a personal plot behind the family home.

This elderly dog led me to think about the different ways people interact with the bodies of their pets after death. Could this behavior be reflective of the relationships they held with their animals in life? In this instance, why take the time to carefully bury a dog in its own space when other, arguably easier options existed?

This, after all, was an era when people often disposed of their dead pets in the river, or might have sold their bodies for meat and skin.

Good hygiene is an obvious reason to choose a burial – no one wants decomposing animal bodies in the street or their garden – but that wouldn’t immediately warrant a personalized, dedicated burial and gravestone.

The most straight forward option would be to dispose of a deceased animal with the household waste. But such treatment would evidently feel less ceremonious and would not offer appropriate emotional closure to what was likely an important relationship.

Like the burial of people, the burial of pets is an intimate cultural practice, one that changes over time and is reflective of the changing relationships that a society has with its cherished creatures.

My study of historic gravestones and epitaphs in Britain from the Victorian period to today shows this changing human-animal relationship. In the 19th century, gravestones were often dedicated to a “loving friend” or “devoted companion,” suggesting pets were mostly considered important friends.

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

Society’s changing attitude towards the role of animals in the afterlife can also be found. Fast-forward a few decades later, and gravestones were more likely to reference a reunion then earlier ones. For example, the owners of Denny, the “brave little cat” buried in an east London cemetery in 1952, wrote on his epitaph “God bless until we meet again.”

I wonder what the epitaphs in Barcelona’s new cemetery will reveal about modern Catalan relationships with animals.

Over time, our ways of treating our animal dead appear to reflect an even closer relationship in life. Once strictly forbidden by law, the last decade saw many jurisdictions, like the state of New York, allowing the co-burial of cremated animals and people, which will undoubtedly lead to changing funerary and commemoration practices for both humans and animals.

For me, the most remarkable similarity between modern and historic pet cemeteries is the striking evidence for the heartbreak and taboos around grieving for animals.

The connection that someone has with their pet can be just as strong and just as significant as their relationships with other humans. Yet today, as in over 100 years ago, individuals continue to struggle to find the appropriate outlet to express their pain, hiding heartache for fear of the social repercussions that might come with public acknowledgment of the existence of such a bond.

The RSPCA reassures the public on its website that they should not feel shame for their grief. In the UK, charities like the Blue Cross and Rainbow Bridge Pet Loss Grief Center offer counseling to bereaved humans.

Close relationships between people and animals have existed for millennia, but in western European cultures, there were few acceptable ways to mourn that relationship. As society becomes more accepting of the importance of human-animal relationships to our collective wellbeing, it is not surprising to see us follow rituals resembling those we use to mourn the loss of our closest human relationships.

At an estimated cost of €200 ($217) per service at the new Barcelona cemetery, it is important to acknowledge that this opportunity to grieve will not be financially available to everyone in the city.

This will not be a space for all of the city’s pets. Pet owners may opt to keep cremated remains within their home or spread out ashes in a meaningful location instead. Online forums and digital pet cemeteries also provide other opportunities to commemorate the relationship and express grief.

Whether one chooses a pet cemetery or not, there are many acceptable ways to express your grief – and to remember your relationship with the important animals in your life.

#249. Sundrop turns your endless baby photos into recaps, time lapses, and more — without AI

New parents take a lot of pictures of their children, but those photos often end up languishing in photo galleries, never to be seen again after the initial snap and share.

A new app, Sundrop, aims to make those pictures more useful by turning them into weekly recaps, highlights, time lapses of the child growing up, and other cute scenes, like putting the child’s photo on a magazine cover or a rookie card.

While Google Photos and Apple Photos already provide extensive tools to organize and manage photos, Sundrop is taking aim at a particular niche: parents with a new baby (usually their first!) who take an overwhelming number of photos and want to do more with those pictures.

The app was developed over the past few months by the same team behind the read-it-later app, Matter: Ben Springwater, Sky Lan, and Rob Mackenzie (all dads themselves).

Notably, Sundrop claims it is designed to be private, and processes all photos locally on the device it is installed on. To make this possible, Sundrop taps into Apple’s Vision framework and on-device Core ML (machine learning) models instead of uploading photos to a cloud server or some other hosted AI service.

Both of the child’s parents can use Sundrop together by linking their accounts, which allows the app to sync their shared photos via Apple’s iCloud service. This feature, the company says, means the pictures aren’t sent to any external servers it runs.

In addition, the team notes that no generative AI is used in the creation of its scenes or highlights.

This differentiates it from many photo-editing apps on the market, including Google’s, which are increasingly tapping generative AI to create new versions of your photos.

To find highlights and recaps, Sundrop scans pictures in the parents’ respective Camera Rolls on their iPhones, skipping things like screenshots or duplicates. It then creates highlights or fun scenes, which it calls “drops.”

One of these is a recap of the week every Sunday, announced as a Lock Screen notification. Others can put two siblings side-by-side at the same age, generate a time-lapse series of photos or a video of a kid growing up, or cut out a photo of the child and place it into a layered template.

Parents can also manually edit the photos Sundrop uses to make its creations, or use the app to automatically organize the best photos into galleries with other similar shots.

The app costs $39.99 per year, and offers a week-long free trial. The annual plan also includes Family Sharing, so only one parent needs to purchase it.
